`CONTRIBUTING.md`:

```md
# Contributing

Corrections are welcome when they come with evidence.

For a semantic correction, include:

1. the exact build identity;
2. the field, RVA, structure, or claim being corrected;
3. whether the new evidence is observed, derived, inferred, or unknown;
4. a reproducible derivation that does not require distributing proprietary binaries or memory;
5. any previous statement the correction supersedes.

Do not use a string, raw opcode match, pointer proximity, handle, or registered callback as proof of invocation by itself. Do not merge dynamic addresses from different launch epochs without proving the relationship.

The public repository does not accept proprietary binaries, raw memory, secrets, network peers, request clients, bypass logic, or exploit material.

```

`README.md`:

```md
# All about EAC

## I reversed Easy Anti-Cheat's Fortnite driver from outside the VM

I wanted to know what `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys` actually does.

So I captured one exact Fortnite EAC build from outside a Windows VM.

## The major findings

### The exact driver

The raw driver I recovered is `44,666,520` bytes:

```text
SHA-256  020d5da6b881408ced33be09b92dc45e12e641b8b3c91f3a327429e5027fef74
Machine  x86-64
Signer   Epic Games Inc.
Timestamp 2026-08-27T15:24:33Z
```

The captured loaded image is `44,986,368` bytes. Three executable sections stayed byte-identical to the signed raw image after I normalized 6,015 relocations. Across five complete loaded-image captures from two launches, I found no executable-section mutation. The later temporal differences were confined to fourteen writable `.data` pages plus the loader-populated IAT state.

That matters because the protector is loud in layout but quiet in observed code mutation. The giant protected section is not unpacking into a constantly rewritten code blob in these captures. It is a stable protected machine with mutable data around it.

### What EAC watches

I recovered the following registered kernel surfaces from two independent physical-memory acquisitions:

| Surface | What I proved |
| --- | --- |
| Process notifications | One active EAC process callback, with create/exit split and recovered exit bookkeeping |
| Thread notifications | One active EAC thread callback, with process/thread lookups, a lock-free publication slot, and separate create/exit paths |
| Image-load notifications | One active EAC image callback, including final-component parsing, main-image discrimination, and system-image exclusions |
| Process-handle policy | Active pre/post Object Manager callbacks for handle creation and duplication |
| Thread-handle policy | Active pre/post Object Manager callbacks for handle creation and duplication |
| Registry policy | One callback at altitude `327530`, handling pre-set, pre-query, and pre-open operations |
| File-system policy | A Filter Manager minifilter for CREATE, SET_INFORMATION, and acquire-for-section-synchronization |
| Bugcheck integration | Two active bugcheck-reason records, including a secondary-dump path that walks page-table state |
| Private callback contexts | Fourteen active per-current-thread contexts on a private EAC list, each tied to a captured System-thread dispatch |
| Device IPC | A named `FILE_DEVICE_UNKNOWN` device with CREATE, CLOSE, DEVICE_CONTROL, and a Win32-visible symbolic link |

That is already a much better description than “it scans for cheats.” EAC watches how processes appear, how threads attach to them, what images enter them, who requests handles, how selected registry areas behave, and what executable files are being opened or mapped. It also maintains private per-thread callback state and crash-time integration.

### The process detector is also crash telemetry

The process callback is not just a create/exit notification veneer. On the recovered exit path, EAC finds its internal process record, marks multiple fields, and conditionally writes eight crash-report values when the tracked game process exits abnormally:

```text
ErrRpt_GameState
ErrRpt_GameCrashProductId
ErrRpt_GameCrashPID
ErrRpt_GameCrashCreateTime
ErrRpt_GameCrashExitTime
ErrRpt_GameCrashExitStatus
ErrRpt_GameCrashInfo01
ErrRpt_GameCrashInfo12
```

I recovered the value types, source fields, and sizes. That gives EAC a structured postmortem view of the protected process instead of a single “process died” bit.

### The thread callback tracks object identity and publishes state lock-free

The thread callback receives the normal `(ProcessId, ThreadId, Create)` tuple, looks up both objects, and releases the outer references on the recovered paths. The interesting part is an exact `lock cmpxchg` against an internal slot at `record+0x4e8`.

On success, the replacement reference is published and a reference-like kernel routine is called. On failure, EAC keeps the previous slot. Create and exit take different internal paths. I can describe the ownership transitions around that slot; I cannot yet name every protected object behind it.

### Image loads are normalized before policy

The image callback receives the standard `FullImageName`, `ProcessId`, and `ImageInfo` inputs. EAC checks the extended-image-information bit before treating `ImageInfo+0x28` as a file object. It can replace the name through an owned buffer, then extracts the final non-empty UTF-16 component.

The final-component helper is strict. It rejects null descriptors, null buffers, short lengths, trailing separators, embedded NULs, colons, and invalid candidate components. For a process image, EAC compares the current process section base with the reported image base so it can distinguish the main image from another mapped module.

The system-image branch explicitly excludes the drivers directory and five graphics-related images before handing the rest to protected bookkeeping and policy helpers.

### The obvious handle-rights story was wrong

The process and thread Object Manager callbacks are registered for both handle creation and handle duplication. The pre callback selects the appropriate requested-access field and replaces `DesiredAccess` with the result of an internal policy helper.

I expected that helper to strip rights. It does not—not on the paths I closed for this build. Both the process and thread transforms return the incoming access mask unchanged. The process mask `0x000e0eba` selects extra association and audit bookkeeping, not access removal. The ordinary post-callback access-delta event path is consequently infeasible because the helper result equals the granted mask.

That correction matters. These callbacks still give EAC object-access visibility and maintain policy context, but the bytes do not support the usual claim that this build's recovered helpers remove handle rights. Runtime-populated tables outside the image still block a universal caller-to-audit-decision map, so I am not inventing one.

### The registry policy is specific

The registry callback handles three classes in the recovered dispatcher:

- `RegNtPreSetValueKey`
- `RegNtPreQueryValueKey`
- `RegNtPreOpenKeyEx`

The pre-set policy covers persistence and system-control areas such as Run/RunOnce, SafeBoot, Active Setup, shell extensions, browser helper objects, Task Scheduler, services, font drivers, KnownDLLs, credential providers, and the Winsock catalogs. A separate predicate recognizes an active blocking firewall rule with a GUID-shaped value name.

The pre-query policy can return `STATUS_OBJECT_NAME_NOT_FOUND` for selected AppCompat, Software Restriction Policy, debugger, Image File Execution Options, AppInit, and Winsock-related paths or values. One `RequireSignedAppInit_DLLs` path synthesizes a DWORD result with exact class- and buffer-length-dependent behavior.

The pre-open policy depends on previous mode and a tracked thread identity. Different protected-name groups apply to the equal-thread and other-thread branches.

This is not a vague “EAC checks the registry” conclusion. It is a set of exact callback information classes, field reads, string constructors, predicates, statuses, and bounded output writes.

### The minifilter watches creation, rename/link operations, and executable mapping

The Filter Manager registration covers:

- `IRP_MJ_CREATE`
- `IRP_MJ_SET_INFORMATION`
- acquire-for-section-synchronization

The rename/link path handles information classes `10`, `11`, `65`, and `72`. The section-synchronization path is especially useful: for an executable mapping request at PASSIVE_LEVEL, the recovered body queries standard file information, applies a normal `8 MiB` cap, reads file bytes from offset zero, runs an internal classifier, releases its allocations, and can complete the request with `STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED`.

The name policy owns a 41-record extension table. I recovered all table rows, their callback veneers, record layout, mutable counters, Startup fallback, and deny-snapshot helper. Four protected delegate bodies remain behind the VM bridge; I know exactly which records they own, but not every inner return predicate.

### The launch chain stages the entire driver through writable shared memory

The service and the EAC-suffixed Fortnite wrapper hold two views of the same 96 MiB pagefile-backed section:

```text
EasyAntiCheat_EOSBin
```

The complete signed driver begins at section offset `0x1c`. Both observed section handles include write access. The section has no backing file pointer, and its exact non-image `_SEGMENT` union state identifies the EAC service as the creator in the captured run.

The launch chain also uses three named events:

```text
EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventModule
EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventDriver
EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventGame
```

Module and driver were already signaled in both physical epochs. Game moved from unsignaled—with one EAC service thread waiting on the exact event object—to signaled with an empty wait list. The wrapper held modify rights to the same object.

I proved the object graph and transition. The exact user-mode creation and signal callsites are still open.

### The device is reachable, but it does not build per-handle state on CREATE

The exact namespace is:

```text
\Driver\EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys
\Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS
\GLOBAL??\EasyAntiCheat_EOS -> \Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS
```

The symbolic-link DACL grants limited access to Everyone and All Application Packages, and broader access to SYSTEM and Administrators. The driver object has its own distinct security descriptor. The captured device object itself has a null security fast-reference, which makes the link/object-manager decision path worth treating separately from the link ACL.

CREATE and CLOSE share one EAC dispatcher. The exact protected trace is only thirteen nodes long: it writes success and zero information into the IRP, completes the request with `IO_NO_INCREMENT`, and returns. It does not read the device object, file object, current stack location, caller, token, or request parameters. It allocates no per-handle state.

The four captured wrapper/game file objects agree: `FsContext`, `FsContext2`, and `FileObjectExtension` are null. `IRP_MJ_CLEANUP` is routed to `IopInvalidDeviceRequest`, while CLOSE still completes normally.

That means any meaningful session authorization is either deeper in DEVICE_CONTROL or in an out-of-band global structure. It is not established during CREATE in this build.

### The device-control surface is real, protected, and still the biggest open boundary

The dispatcher yields fifteen distinct `METHOD_NEITHER`-shaped control-flow classes in the bounded model. Fourteen delegate to separate handler entries; one remains inline. I recovered handler ABIs, stack-location register assignments, several exact length splits, null-pointer behavior, `IRP.UserBuffer` identity on one path, and one exact four-byte output write.

What I do not have is a naturally retained accepted request. Neither physical sweep caught an EAC-device IRP in flight. So these values are candidates that separate control flow, not a promise that every one is accepted by the live driver.

The unresolved part is the important part: live packet layouts, caller authentication, state gates, final statuses, and downstream effects. Anyone presenting those fifteen values as a finished protocol is skipping the hard bit.

### The private callback records were not work items

One of my own early classifications was wrong in a useful way. Sixteen physical records had this tempting shape:

```text
LIST_ENTRY
callback pointer
parameter pointer
```

That looks like a worker item if you stop at the first `0x20` bytes. I did not stop there.

The records split exactly into two kernel bugcheck-reason records and fourteen EAC-owned `0x78`-byte per-current-thread contexts. The private object contains its list entry, callback, associated context, flags, current `KTHREAD`, two embedded notification events, a queryable byte, and two unresolved tail qwords.

I recovered its allocator, list insertion, lookup-by-thread, unlink/free path, one lifecycle-only removal, and the active dispatcher stack pairing for every one of the fourteen callback-bearing nodes. The correct private list-head RVA is `0x30cb90`; an earlier `0x2fcb90` normalization is retired.

This is the kind of correction I care about. Layout similarity is a lead. It is not ownership.

### EAC has deep platform machinery, but instruction presence is not execution proof

I closed every conservatively reachable site in these low-level families:

- 20 CPUID and 2 XGETBV sites
- 15 RDMSR, 18 WRMSR, and 1 RDPMC site
- 22 RDTSC sites
- SIDT and SGDT
- WBINVD and INVD
- four keyed OUT sites
- XBEGIN/XEND
- VMREAD
- SLDT, SMSW, and STR

Some are ordinary runtime dispatch plumbing. The SHA-NI and scalar SHA-256 selection paths are a good example. Others are protected-machine micro-operations whose register numbers, ports, VMCS fields, or operands come from live protected state.

The distinction matters. I can prove exact opcodes, data flow, exception edges, state fields, and continuations. I cannot claim a VMREAD or WRMSR executed during the game just because the instruction is reachable in the binary.

### Crypto and decompression are substantial subsystems

The image contains closed or bounded implementations for SHA-1, SHA-256, SHA-512, MD5, an MD4-family decoder-key path, CRC32, memcmp, memmove, memset, and large fills.

I also recovered a substantial Kraken/Oodle-family entropy decoder. The type-0 Huffman, type-2 binary, and type-4 unary paths are semantically closed through their final seam checks, with exact scratch-size boundaries and table hashes. Type-1 TANS, type-3 RLE, and type-5 recursive branches remain named and edge-closed but not fully expanded.

I did not find a captured live blob that would let me attribute a product feature to one of those paths. The implementation is real; the captured use case is still unknown.

### TPM and network evidence belongs to the companion until proven otherwise

The EAC service retained exact TPM 2.0 request buffers for:

```text
GetCapability(TPM_CAP_HANDLES, 0x810eac00, 1)
ReadPublic(0x810eac00)
```

`GetCapability` persisted across both launches. `ReadPublic` was present in the first. I did not recover a response buffer or completion record, so I will not quietly promote resident request bytes into “the driver queried and consumed the TPM key.” This is user-mode companion evidence.

I also rebuilt the service's handle-rooted IPv4/443 AFD endpoint in the early capture and a surviving wrapper endpoint in the steady capture. The peer value stays out of the public data. A socket object proves connection state; it does not identify every application message or bind it to the TPM buffer.

### The two hardening findings I would review first

First, one live page-table sample marked `43,864,064` of `44,986,368` driver-image bytes writable and executable: `97.505235%` W+X. I found no executable mutation, but this layout broadens the consequences of any independent kernel write primitive.

Second, the complete signed raw driver sits in two writable user-mode views during staging. I have not proved that modified bytes would pass the later integrity/load path, so I am not making a TOCTOU exploit claim. I am saying the post-staging integrity consumer deserves a very close look.

## How EAC fights cheaters in this build

The design is layered.

1. **Observe lifecycle.** Process, thread, and image callbacks give EAC a live view of who appears and what code enters an address space.
2. **Correlate object access.** Pre/post callbacks observe process/thread handle creation and duplication and maintain association/audit state; the closed helpers preserve access rather than stripping it.
3. **Police persistence and policy surfaces.** Registry callbacks cover autoruns, services, debugger and AppCompat controls, shell injection surfaces, firewall rules, and Winsock configuration.
4. **Inspect executable file transitions.** The minifilter sees creates, rename/link activity, and executable section synchronization, with a path that reads and classifies up to 8 MiB before permitting a mapping.
5. **Correlate platform state.** CPU identity, timing, MSR, descriptor-table, VMCS, cache, and I/O-port machinery provides ingredients for platform and virtualization judgments.
6. **Maintain private per-thread execution state.** The private callback contexts tie active protected callbacks to exact System threads and give EAC its own lifecycle/synchronization layer outside the standard registration records.
7. **Retain crash and dump visibility.** Exit telemetry and bugcheck-reason callbacks preserve information when the protected process or the machine fails.
8. **Coordinate a protected launch.** Shared memory, named events, a named device, a wrapper process, and the service form a staged bootstrapping channel around the kernel driver.

The strength is not any single signal. It is the correlation. A suspicious image load means more when the process record, thread history, handle-access context, file classification, registry state, and platform profile all disagree with the expected launch.

The cost is complexity. The same system also exposes a huge protected audit surface, extensive W+X mapping, a writable staging channel, and a privileged `METHOD_NEITHER` boundary whose live protocol is still unresolved.

## How I reversed it

### I stayed outside the guest

I used memflow from the Linux host against a Celestial Windows VM. The observation path did not inject into EAC, hook callbacks, load a helper driver, open the EAC device, generate privileged requests, or upload the binary.

I did use the VM normally: launch Fortnite, let EAC initialize, and capture the machine from outside it. That gave me useful behavior without making the target tell me what I wanted to hear.

### I captured the lifecycle at two speeds

The first watcher followed module, process, range, and page changes around startup. Its active loop was much slower than its nominal target because reading process memory is expensive. That taught me an important lesson: a timestamped poller is not an instruction trace.

I then built a focused 500 Hz lifecycle observer. It collected `210,001` contiguous samples over roughly `420` seconds:

```text
median interval  1.954 ms
p95 interval     1.984 ms
maximum gap      11.866 ms
effective rate   500.005 samples/s
```

I also found and corrected a collector bug where a partial aggregate read suppressed individually successful records. The corrected path preserved successful items and rejected failed ranges one by one.

### I reconstructed the raw file from memory

The service and wrapper exposed multiple copies or fragments of the staged driver. I did not trust a sparse zero-filled convenience image. I built an offset-labelled union from exact source fragments, excluded conflicting observations, recovered every raw byte, and verified the Authenticode structures from the resulting original-length file.

The independent second launch produced the same raw SHA-256. That was the point where offsets and semantic claims could be bound to one exact build instead of “whatever EAC was installed that day.”

### I matched the running Windows kernel before naming structures

The live kernel's CodeView record selected the exact public PDB. I used that build's offsets for `_EPROCESS`, `_ETHREAD`, `_KTHREAD`, `_IRP`, `_IO_STACK_LOCATION`, `_OBJECT_HEADER`, `_KAPC`, `_KDPC`, `_KTIMER`, Filter Manager objects, and callback lists.

This caught real mistakes. `_KAPC.Header.Size` is the byte size `0x58`, not an eleven-qword count. The timer header's adjacent bytes are union fields, not a generic size tuple. When the PDB, six live APC records, and the parser disagreed with my first assumption, the assumption lost.

### I combined static code with live object ownership

Static analysis told me that a function reads `stack_location+0x10`. The matched ABI told me that is `InputBufferLength`. A physical snapshot told me whether the object exists and who owns it. A thread stack told me whether a callback pointer was part of active dispatch. Cross-capture topology told me whether a record persisted or was removed.

None of those is enough alone. Together they turn “looks plausible” into a defensible claim.

### I modeled protected paths instead of pretending the protector was gone

The driver uses short native veneers, context-save stubs, keyed transitions, a large protected executable region, mutable resolver state, and state-dependent indirect calls. I built bounded interpreters around exact entry states and varied one input or external return at a time.

That is how I recovered field touches, status returns, access-mask transforms, length boundaries, cleanup paths, and control-flow classes without executing the driver natively.

The word “bounded” appears often in this repository for a reason. A model over lengths `0..32` proves those 33 cases. It does not prove all 4,294,967,296 possible values. A forced external return proves the branch depending on that return. It does not prove the external routine naturally returned it during the capture.

### I treated negative evidence like evidence, not wishful thinking

I walked all 32 executive worker lists, both DPC queues on all 16 processors, thousands of APC heads, and every timer-wheel slot. I also scanned every aligned qword in both 19.33 GB physical images for pointers into EAC executable code.

No EAC-owned queued executive work item, DPC, APC, or timer survived either capture. That is exact sampled absence, not “EAC never used a DPC.” Anything transient could have existed between or outside the sweeps.

The physical images themselves are sequential acquisitions while the VM continues running. A backlink mismatch may be a torn list observed across two instants. I kept those rejects with their exact stage and link values instead of throwing them away to make the summary cleaner.

### I made every correction public inside the analysis

The private callback list is the best example. The early scanner called its records `WORK_QUEUE_ITEM`-shaped, which was fair as a shape description and wrong as an ownership conclusion. The later allocator, list head, embedded events, current-thread field, lifecycle change, and dispatcher stack proof forced a new type classification.

The corrected machine ledger now says what the evidence says: two bugcheck records and fourteen private per-thread contexts.

That is what good reversing looks like. The goal is not to be right on the first pass. The goal is to make it difficult for a wrong first pass to survive.

## The evidence model

Every serious claim uses one of four labels:

| Label | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| **Observed** | The bytes, field, handle, object, link, PTE, or timestamp are in a captured artifact |
| **Derived** | A deterministic ABI, instruction, paging, PE, PDB, or cross-artifact interpretation of observed data |
| **Inferred** | Multiple independent observations support one explanation, but one decisive private value or event is missing |
| **Unknown** | The required state was not retained, is epoch-incoherent, is runtime-keyed, or remains behind protected control flow |

I do not use “unknown” as an apology. It is a coordinate for the next capture.

### Repository boundaries

This repository contains analysis, normalized layouts, hashes, diagrams, and sanitized ledgers. It does not contain:

- the proprietary EAC binaries;
- guest physical-memory images;
- full process dumps;
- reusable credentials, session secrets, or peer addresses;
- an EAC-device request client;
- exploit, tampering, suppression, or bypass code.

The reason is simple: none of those are necessary to explain what I found.

## Current status

I know an extreme amount about this exact build. I can describe the registered surfaces, major enforcement paths, launch object graph, protected callback context, crash and bugcheck integration, device namespace, CREATE/CLOSE behavior, bounded DEVICE_CONTROL classes, platform machinery, and a large set of exact policy predicates.

I am not claiming omniscience. The largest remaining gaps are the naturally accepted IOCTL protocol and deep authorization state, exact readiness-event creators/signalers, the shared-section post-staging integrity consumer, four protected minifilter delegates, coherent bugcheck invocation data, TPM response ownership, and complete teardown/error-unwind ordering.

Those gaps are documented as a continuation plan rather than buried in a conclusion. If this investigation gets picked up again, [start there](docs/remaining-work.md).

# Research and Disclosure Boundary

I published this repository to document one exact EAC build and the evidence used to understand it.

Please open an issue for factual corrections, reproducibility problems, stale links, or evidence-label disagreements. Do not attach proprietary EAC or Microsoft binaries, physical-memory images, process dumps, credentials, tokens, cookies, account identifiers, network peers, or private game data.

I will not merge request generators, packet schemas derived through active probing, bypass instructions, callback suppression, tampering paths, exploit code, or tools intended to defeat the mechanisms described here.

Module-relative findings remain build-specific. Include the relevant raw-file SHA-256 and report section with any correction.

# Evidence Appendices

I generated these files from the private analysis tree after scrubbing host paths, network peers, and absolute kernel virtual addresses. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, hashes, counts, and semantic boundaries remain intact.

The appendices intentionally retain a formal forensic voice. The first-person narrative and current synthesis live in the repository root and `docs/`.

Some appendices are historical layers. When a newer focused package corrects an older report, use this precedence:

1. focused semantic package;
2. later cross-capture ownership package;
3. current narrative deep dive;
4. older broad runtime/dossier report.

Important supersedence rules:

- process/thread/image behavior comes from `ps-callbacks.md`, not the older broad runtime boundary;
- minifilter name policy comes from `name-policy.md`;
- Object Manager helpers preserve access on the closed normal paths; use `object-access-policy.md`;
- the sixteen callback shapes are two bugcheck records plus fourteen private contexts; use `callback-family.md` and `deferred-execution.md`;
- standard work/DPC/APC/timer state is now enumerated; use `deferred-execution.md`;
- device namespace and CREATE/CLOSE state come from `device-namespace-auth.md`;
- the fifteen DEVICE_CONTROL values remain bounded control-flow classes, not proven accepted commands;
- low-level instruction intent must follow the focused CPUID/MSR/RDTSC/privileged-site reports.

Private-evidence references identify source artifacts that are not distributed. They are provenance notes, not broken public dependencies.

`appendices/callback-family.md`:

```md
# Retiring the “Work Item” False Positive

I analyzed one fixed driver build using two passive physical-memory epochs, its exact loaded image, matched kernel symbols, and captured thread-stack inventories. This appendix contains structure and ownership findings only.

## Result

The sixteen callback-bearing records do not form one `WORK_QUEUE_ITEM` family.

They split into:

- two Windows `KBUGCHECK_REASON_CALLBACK_RECORD` registrations; and
- fourteen active proprietary per-thread callback contexts owned by the driver.

The earlier work-item classification came from a superficial `LIST_ENTRY`, callback, parameter layout match across the first `0x20` bytes. I retired that label. Ownership has to survive the allocator, list head, lifecycle, dispatcher, and surrounding object layout—not merely resemble a public structure.

## Evidence labels

| Label | Meaning |
| --- | --- |
| **Exact** | Literal captured bytes, links, values, or symbol-derived addresses |
| **Derived** | Deterministic instruction, ABI, paging, or cross-artifact correlation |
| **Interpretive** | Multiple exact observations support the semantic name, but the proprietary source type is unavailable |
| **Unknown** | The captured evidence does not close the claim |

## Family split

| Finding | Status |
| --- | --- |
| Sixteen unique callback-bearing scanner records | **Exact** |
| Two records belong to the kernel bugcheck-reason list | **Exact** |
| Fourteen records belong to a private driver list | **Exact** |
| The private records are per-current-thread callback invocation/guard contexts | **Interpretive**, supported by allocator and dispatcher evidence |
| These records are executive work items, DPCs, timers, or APCs | **Excluded** |

## Kernel bugcheck-reason records

The first two records match the complete `KBUGCHECK_REASON_CALLBACK_RECORD` ABI:

| Offset | Field |
| --- | --- |
| `+0x00` | `LIST_ENTRY` |
| `+0x10` | callback routine |
| `+0x18` | component pointer |
| `+0x20` | checksum |
| `+0x28` | callback reason |
| `+0x2c` | state |

Both records are linked bidirectionally beneath the matched kernel symbol `KeBugCheckReasonCallbackListHead`. Their captured reasons are 1 and 2, both have state 1, and both reference the same empty component string.

The matched callback ABI is:

- `RCX`: reason
- `RDX`: callback record
- `R8`: reason-specific data
- `R9`: reason-specific data length

List membership proves completed registration. It does not reveal the protected registration callsite, deregistration path, cleanup order, or a real bugcheck invocation frame. Those remain unknown.

## Proprietary per-thread context

The remaining records are owned by a private driver sentinel at module RVA `0x30cb90`.

This corrects an earlier `0x2fcb90` normalization error. The corrected value comes directly from the native list-query instruction and agrees with both captured list walks.

The recovered object is exactly `0x78` bytes:

| Offset | Recovered field | Status |
| --- | --- | --- |
| `+0x00` | primary private `LIST_ENTRY` | **Exact** |
| `+0x10` | active protected callback entry | **Exact** |
| `+0x18` | associated context or parameter pointer | **Exact value; interpretive role** |
| `+0x20` | mode/flags | **Exact**, observed as `0`, `1`, `0x200`, or `0x201` |
| `+0x28` | current `KTHREAD` | **Derived exact** from the allocator's `gs:[0x188]` load |
| `+0x30` | embedded notification event | **Derived exact** |
| `+0x48` | second embedded notification event | **Derived exact** |
| `+0x60` | byte exposed by the thread-membership query | **Derived exact** |
| `+0x68` | unresolved qword | **Unknown** |
| `+0x70` | unresolved qword | **Unknown** |

The allocation wrapper records the same `0x78` payload size. Physical pool allocation rounds the object upward, but that rounding is not part of the proprietary ABI. Pool tags vary between instances and are not ownership evidence.

## Lifecycle proof

The early epoch contains fifteen private-list members:

```text
sentinel -> lifecycle-null node -> first active node -> ... -> sentinel
```

The later epoch contains fourteen:

```text
sentinel -> first active node -> ... -> sentinel
```

The removed record had a null callback. The first active node's `Blink` changed from the removed record to the sentinel, and the sentinel's `Flink` changed to that active node. Every one of the fourteen callback-bearing records persisted.

This is an exact, backlink-valid unlink of one lifecycle-only context. It is not evidence of an executive work queue consuming an item.

## Allocation, lookup, and removal semantics

The native allocator performs the following sequence:

1. Allocate a `0x78`-byte payload.
2. Clear the callback, context, and mode region.
3. Store the current `KTHREAD`.
4. Initialize two notification events in the unsignaled state.
5. Store a selector-derived byte.
6. Acquire the private list lock.
7. Validate the tail backlink.
8. Insert the new record at the tail.
9. Release the lock and return the object.

The corresponding removal routine:

1. Treats a null record as a no-op.
2. Acquires the same lock.
3. Validates both neighboring backlinks.
4. Unlinks the record in both directions.
5. Releases the lock.
6. Transfers to the driver's free wrapper.

The query routine accepts a sought thread, walks the same private sentinel, compares each record's `KTHREAD` field, optionally returns the byte at `+0x60`, and returns zero when no record matches.

These actions come from complete native function extents. The proprietary names and the final allocator/free primitive beneath the wrappers remain unknown.

## Registration evidence

Two native constructor paths expose direct callback-address materialization:

- one allocates a `0x160`-byte associated context and later links through a context-local list entry;
- one allocates a `0x80`-byte associated context and stores the registration result near its tail.

Other active callback families use protected or indirect registration paths. I can prove their registered state and active dispatch, but not name callsites that never emerge into recoverable native code.

## Dispatch proof

Each of the fourteen active records is associated with a captured System thread.

For every record:

1. Its callback pointer occurs exactly once on the associated kernel stack.
2. The matching dispatcher return address occurs exactly `0x10` below it.
3. That spacing agrees with the recovered indirect-call instruction after the call pushes its return address.

This closes active dispatch independently of list membership. It is stronger than finding callback-looking pointers in pool memory.

The callback entries themselves are five-byte relative-jump veneers into protected context-save stubs. The veneers and initial stubs are exact. None is covered by a native PDATA function extent, so I cannot honestly assign a larger source-level function boundary. Protected-body effects beyond the first recovered state remain unknown.

## Why this is not an executive scheduler object

| Candidate mechanism | Exclusion |
| --- | --- |
| `WORK_QUEUE_ITEM` | Only the first `0x20` bytes resemble it; the real object is `0x78` bytes with thread state, events, a private sentinel, and private lifecycle functions |
| `IO_WORKITEM` | No active record appears on the captured executive worker queues |
| `KDPC` | Its list and deferred-routine offsets conflict with the observed layout |
| `KTIMER` | It begins with a dispatcher header and places its list entry at a different offset |
| `KAPC` | Its header, list-entry, and kernel-routine offsets conflict with the observed object |
| Embedded event/timer parent | The two `KEVENT`s are members used for synchronization; they do not define the parent object |

The passive inventories also contained no EAC-owned queued DPC, timer, APC, or executive-worker match for these records.

## What remains unknown

I do not claim recovery of:

- proprietary source-level type or member names;
- protected callback bodies beyond their first recovered state;
- protected registration callsites for most callback families;
- bugcheck registration or deregistration callsites;
- a real bugcheck callback invocation;
- callback-specific meanings of the mode bits or both events;
- the semantic meaning of the tail qwords;
- the final external primitive beneath the private allocation wrappers.

Those are evidence boundaries, not blanks to fill with guesses.

The central lesson is simple: layout similarity is a lead, not a type system. I only accepted ownership after the list head, allocator, object lifetime, thread association, and dispatcher all agreed.

```

`appendices/context-resolvers.md`:

```md
# EAC context-keyed native resolver sites

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Result

Both sites are completely recovered through the indirect call instruction, including exact instruction boundaries, PDATA extents, unwind programs, frame-relative sources, rotating resident operands, resolver-key semantics, and post transforms. Neither decoded callee can be named from the retained evidence because the runtime frame keys were never captured.

The strongest provenance supported by bytes is **termination-funclet-shaped**, not an active callback claim. RVA `0xd46f6` appears as `HandlerAddress` in three zero-`JumpTarget` scope-record-shaped rows; RVA `0x11f93b` appears in three more. Those rows are orphan protector metadata: no static PDATA entry covers their protected ranges, no target parent unwind record points at them, and their first row is not preceded by the required scope count. A valid in-image control at xdata RVA `0x2aba258` has the complete `UNW_FLAG_EHANDLER -> EAC 0xb8c3b -> Count -> SCOPE_TABLE` chain, and `0xb8c3b` resolves exactly through the native resolver to `ntoskrnl.exe!__C_specific_handler`.

## Exact site equations

| Site | Resolver key RCX | Resolver RDX | Resolver R8 | Decoded-call RCX | Target equation |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| `0xd4728` | `[entry_RDX+0xe0]` | `[EAC+0x34d8b8]` | `[EAC+0x34d8b0]` | `[entry_RDX+0xc8]` | `(resolver_return * 0x5c7b8940f788d7cd) XOR 0x84184fd3e7dd24fd` |
| `0x11f971` | `[entry_RDX+0x188]` | `[EAC+0x30d848]` | `[EAC+0x30d840]` | `[entry_RDX+0x180]` | `(resolver_return * 0x82cf57bd0a96400d) XOR 0x341f3feb620ac73c` |

All multiplication is modulo `2^64`. At each target call, RCX is the only argument explicitly authored after the resolver returns. RDX/R8/R9 are volatile resolver residues, and no stack arguments are authored.

## Frame and unwind proof

Both entries home RDX at `[entry_RSP+0x10]`, save the same eight nonvolatile GPRs, and set `RBP=entry_RDX+0x80`. The first allocates `0x48` bytes (`RSP` delta `0x88` including pushes). The second allocates `0x68` bytes (`RSP` delta `0xa8`) and saves XMM6 at post-prolog `[RSP+0x50]`. Their PDATA entries have `UNW_FLAG_NHANDLER`; neither handler has its own language-specific callback metadata.

The missing parent unwind record matters: it is the only authority that maps the unwinder's `EstablisherFrame` (entry RDX) to protected-parent locals. Without it, nearby same-offset stores cannot be promoted to proven frame provenance.

## Caller/callback provenance

There is no direct call/jump to either handler in the Phase-1 CFG, no bytewise direct transfer candidate in `.text`, and no absolute handler VA stored in either loaded image. The only RVA references outside PDATA are the six orphan scope rows. Canonical Phase-3 callback ledgers contain no target linkage, and all saved thread-stack ledgers have zero attributions in the handlers or protected scope ranges.

The protected bodies do have exact jump-island predecessors: `0x37edbc -> 0xd40ab` and `0x3877f1 -> 0x11f336`. These are protected-dispatch entry links, not handler-call edges.

## Rejected producer hypothesis

The second protected body writes `R9` to `[R12+0x108]` and `RAX` to `[R12+0x100]`. Those addresses equal the handler's key/argument fields only if `R12 == entry_RDX+0x80`. No active parent unwind record establishes that equality. Moreover, R9 is an eight-byte `00/ff` comparison mask; enumerating all 256 masks selects zero canonical modulus addresses in each of the two exact loaded resolver states. The offset resemblance is therefore not valid provenance.

## Physical and lifecycle cross-check

Two full linked-state raw captures and one after-stop raw capture were searched. Every scope-pattern hit is an exact copy of the original metadata page, none has the required immediately preceding scope count, and there are zero candidate dynamic `RUNTIME_FUNCTION` pairs or handler/resolver-return absolute addresses. This excludes a retained standard registration or active/stale target stack in those captures; it does not prove the thunks never execute.

## Exact closure requirement

An exact target name requires one passive, host-side memflow sample that captures handler-entry RDX (or an equivalent contemporaneous trap/vCPU context), the two frame qwords, the resident resolver operands, selected modulus, and module ledger in the same resolver epoch. If the connector cannot expose vCPU registers and no trap frame exists during the transient call, passive memory alone cannot reconstruct RDX after return.

No sample instruction was executed, no guest instrumentation was installed, and no bypass/client artifact is included.

```

`appendices/cpuid-xgetbv.md`:

```md
# Reachable CPUID/XGETBV site closure

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Result

All 20 conservatively reachable CPUID sites and both reachable XGETBV sites in the exact captured EAC image now have bounded local-dataflow classifications. The raw image contains 118 `0f a2` byte pairs and 44 `0f 01 d0` byte triples; raw-only matches are retained separately and are not promoted to executed instructions.

## Exact classes

- Nine sites are entry-keyed, memoized CPUID bit predicates. They keep only a boolean-derived encoded token, not the raw four-register response. Two of those gates choose SHA-NI versus scalar SHA-256 compression.
- Six sites plus both XGETBV sites are the already bounded byte-fill/general-ISA dispatch initializers.
- `0xad102` executes leaf 0, forms the exact `EBX || EDX || ECX` 12-byte vendor tuple, compares it against two protected table-derived candidates, and returns a class token.
- `0x8fdff` is an interpreter CPUID operation: inputs are state qwords `+0x8/+0x10`; sign-extended outputs are written to `+0x8/+0x20/+0x10/+0x18` for EAX/EBX/ECX/EDX.
- `0x179d9e` measures repeated RDTSC/CPUID latency against an equal-relation adjacent-RDTSC baseline, retains the last CPUID tuple plus both cycle totals in a local record, and passes that record to an unresolved entry-key-resolved callee.
- `0x21504e` caches one CPUID.ECX-derived gate and selects an entry-key-resolved accelerated path or a reflected table-driven 32-bit checksum recurrence.
- `0x237c06` executes CPUID, increments qword `[R8]`, and reaches the same INT3 through complementary JE/JNE edges; the apparent `0xe9` fallthrough is unreachable.

## Evidence boundary

Bytes, PDATA containment, field offsets, branches, and in-body dataflow are exact for the captured image. Sites with entry-key-derived leaves retain that requirement explicitly. This is not an instruction trace, and unresolved callees are not assigned semantics.

## Verification

- Unit tests: 7/7 pass.
- Statement coverage: 97.2%.
- Validation: `pass`.

# Clean cryptographic function semantics

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

This package closes four large, fully decoded, no-direct-call PDATA ranges without turning static presence into an execution claim.

## Exact results

- `0x14b22c..0x14c2ee` is a 64-round, 64-byte-block MD5 compression transform when its decoder produces internal key `0xe7c9ceb6`. Exact captured bytes match the independent MD5 reference on three digest vectors and 64 deterministic randomized state/block vectors (1-3 blocks). State words are at context offsets `+0x8..+0x14`; `r9d` is a byte count reduced by 64; return is the advanced input pointer.
- `0xbcd22..0xbd23b` and `0xbd526..0xbda66` are two SHA-512 compression ABIs. Both decode the exact 80-qword table at RVA `0x2c4ae0`, use the SHA-512 rotation tuple, byte-swap the first 16 message words, expand the schedule, execute 80 rounds, and feed forward eight 64-bit state words. The exact SHA-512 IV is resident at RVA `0x2c4a00`.
- `0xbf24c..0xbf9a0` has the exact 48-round MD4 body, block/state shape, nonlinear rounds, rotation cycle, and constants for the two recovered low-32 decoder-key classes. It remains a conditional family match because no live caller argument or invocation was captured.

## SHA-512 cross-boot pointer recovery

Both independent boots rotate the encoded qwords at RVAs `0x2f1560` and `0x2f1570`, but base-normalization and the per-ABI subtraction reconstruct the same RVA `0x2c4ae0`. The decoded internal keys are `0xb5e09b7166f3d4d` and `0xc5de007c26b1e0e0`; both produce left rotations `[50,46,23,36,30,25]`, equivalent to the SHA-512 right rotations `[14,18,41,28,34,39]`.

## Evidence boundary

These are exact static/behavioral semantics for the captured image. None of the four entrypoints has a resolved direct caller or a captured live invocation. The MD5 seed is a constructive key preimage used to prove the captured body; it is not claimed as the driver's observed call argument. MD4 remains conditional for the same reason.

## Reproduce

```bash
PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 python3 analysis/clean-crypto-semantics/build_crypto_semantics.py
PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 python3 analysis/clean-crypto-semantics/validate_crypto_semantics.py
```

# Deferred-execution ownership recovery

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

This package closes the captured-state ownership question for executive work queues, per-processor DPC queues, timer wheels, and per-thread APC queues using only the two complete host-side memflow physical captures. It does not treat an empty captured list as historical non-occurrence.

## Exact identity and method

- EAC module: `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys`, base `KVA-0055`, `SizeOfImage=0x2ae7000` (44,986,368 bytes).
- Captured loaded image SHA-256: `779254e58f0c5881281af07a3afbd369af1824670d8cdb1fb7dd81ea7ae0e90a`.
- Kernel module: `ntoskrnl.exe`, base `KVA-0091`.
- Build-matched symbol identity: `ntkrnlmp.pdb-BEA4BE7B19EEF0C97610A5ECE65CAE0C-1`; PDB SHA-256 `3a299a28a5936597f38f3fce00ac08973efa35f49d182137ef2825db8c867b4f`.
- Capture `raw-complete`: 19,327,352,832 bytes, no failed pages, SHA-256 `4b8fc07340473e6148d1032d5e388fa2c80de646b01c9edb984f667ab292a03a`.
- Capture `steady-state`: 19,327,352,832 bytes, no failed pages, SHA-256 `f6952b001bbd3b1e0762731c8b3cf07173dcef1274e3a2ab60e6ff2c8c8ea13b`.

The collector opens each raw snapshot through `FileIoMemory`, instantiates the memflow-win32 OS layer, opens the System virtual address space, walks only build-matched public-PDB layouts, and independently streams every byte of the physical image while hashing it and testing every aligned qword for an EAC executable-section pointer. No guest code, hook, injection, request generator, privileged client, or memory write is present.

## Corrections made during this pass

Two prior layout assumptions were wrong and are retired:

1. `_KTIMER` dispatcher-header byte 1 is `TimerControlFlags`, byte 2 is `Hand`, and byte 3 is `TimerMiscFlags`. Those bytes are not a generic Type/Size header.
2. `_KAPC.Size` at `+0x02` stores the byte size `0x58` (88), not the qword count `11`. The corrected rule was proved both from the matched PDB (`sizeof(_KAPC)=0x58`) and from six linked live `_KAPC` headers with bytes beginning `12 00 58`.

The exact frozen layouts and address formulas are in symbol-layouts.json (`private evidence: symbol-layouts.json`).

## Executive worker queues

`nt!ExWorkerQueue` resolves at `KVA-0092` to `_EX_WORK_QUEUE KVA-0093`. Its dispatcher header is Type 21 and header byte 2 is 172. All 32 priority-list heads were readable and completed a sentinel-terminated walk in both captures. Every list was empty at both capture instants:

- queued items walked: 0 / 0;
- EAC-owned `_WORK_QUEUE_ITEM.WorkerRoutine`: 0 / 0;
- EAC-owned `_IO_WORKITEM.Routine`: 0 / 0.

This is exact captured-list state. It does not prove that EAC never submitted an executive work item before, between, or after the captures.

## Per-processor DPC queues

`nt!KeNumberProcessors` reports 16, and all 16 pointers in `nt!KiProcessorBlock` were walked. For both `_KPRCB.DpcData[0]` and `[1]` on every CPU, the `_KDPC_LIST.ListHead` was null and each walk completed with count zero. `_KPRCB.CurrentDpcRoutine` never pointed into an EAC executable section.

- queued DPC entries: 0 / 0;
- current EAC DPC routines: 0 / 0;
- physical `_KDPC` layouts with an EAC `DeferredRoutine`: 0 / 0.

The DPC walker uses the matched `_KDPC_LIST` singly-linked layout, subtracts the exact `DpcListEntry +0x08`, accepts normal Type 19 and threaded Type 26, and preserves queue/argument/context fields for any positive match.

## Per-thread APC queues

The collector walks `_EPROCESS.ThreadListHead`, proves each `_ETHREAD.ThreadListEntry` with bidirectional backlinks, then walks current and saved `_KAPC_STATE.ApcListHead[KernelMode/UserMode]`. Each positive APC requires:

- Type `0x12`, Size `0x58`;
- exact owning `_ETHREAD` identity;
- `Inserted=1`;
- list index equal to `ApcMode`;
- exact bidirectional queue backlinks.

`raw-complete` covered 99 live processes, 2,246 backlink-valid threads, and 8,984 APC heads. Both current heads were empty for every covered thread. Saved heads were either exactly empty (808 per mode) or zero-initialized (1,438 per mode). There were no APC-list errors and no queued APC.

`steady-state` covered 103 live processes, 2,303 backlink-valid threads, and 9,212 APC heads. It contains six exact current/kernel APCs: two embedded `_KTHREAD.SchedulerApc` records in `FortniteClient` PID 8592 (TIDs 1624 and 1612) and four in `backgroundTask` PID 12272 (TIDs 2384, 4360, 6360, and 9852). Every record has:

- object address `_ETHREAD+0x288`;
- `KernelRoutine = RundownRoutine = nt+0x48ed20`, whose matched public aliases are `KiSchedulerApcNop` and `KiSchedulerApcRundown`;
- `NormalRoutine = nt+0x267080` (`KiSchedulerApc`);
- `NormalContext = owning ETHREAD`, `ApcStateIndex=0`, `ApcMode=0`, and `Inserted=1`.

All six exact records are retained in analysis.json (`private evidence: analysis.json`). None of their three routine slots resolves into EAC. The complete physical pointer scans also found no dormant or unlinked `_KAPC` layout whose Kernel, Rundown, or Normal routine points into EAC.

## Timer wheels

Both 256-slot timer tables on each of 16 `_KPRCB`s were walked using the matched nested-array formula. A timer is counted only when Type is 8 or 9, `TimerType<=1`, and its `_KTIMER.TimerListEntry` has exact bidirectional backlinks.

- `raw-complete`: 100 backlink-valid timers; 118 linkage rejections and five zero/invalid-header layout rejections.
- `steady-state`: 101 backlink-valid timers; 100 linkage rejections and no layout rejection.
- timers stored inside the EAC image: 0 / 0;
- timers whose linked `_KDPC.DeferredRoutine` points into EAC: 0 / 0.

The captures were streamed while the VM was running, so timer-list pages were acquired at different instants. The rejected entries are preserved individually in the raw JSON with CPU/table/slot/head/entry/stage and exact link values. They are evidence of a non-atomic snapshot boundary, not silently discarded negatives.

## Full physical pointer scan and ownership correction

Every aligned qword in each 19.33 GB image was examined: 2,415,919,104 qwords per capture. The scan retained all 2,959 (`raw-complete`) and 7,507 (`steady-state`) pointers into EAC executable sections; no retention cap was reached.

Sixteen physical objects in each capture have this generic byte shape: `LIST_ENTRY +0x00`, EAC callback `+0x10`, parameter `+0x18`. For each, physical bytes equal the virtual bytes and both virtual backlinks resolve exactly. Their virtual address, callback RVA, and parameter identities are stable across captures. They are not on `ExWorkerQueue`, and the overlapping `_WORK_QUEUE_ITEM` byte shape is not type ownership.

The dedicated ownership recovery resolves them exactly:

- two are `KBUGCHECK_REASON_CALLBACK_RECORD`s on `nt!KeBugCheckReasonCallbackListHead` (`KVA-0094`): the EAC-embedded record at EAC RVA `0x3003a8`, callback RVA `0x58b45`, Reason 1; and pool record `KVA-0095`, callback RVA `0xa1120`, Reason 2;
- fourteen are EAC-owned 0x78-byte per-current-KTHREAD callback-context records on the EAC sentinel at RVA `0x30cb90`, created by RVA `0x1ccfe9`, removed/freed by RVA `0x1cd372`, and queried by ETHREAD at RVA `0x1cd5a8`;
- the custom record fields are `LIST_ENTRY +0x00`, active callback veneer `+0x10`, callback parameter/context `+0x18`, mode/flags `+0x20`, current KTHREAD `+0x28`, embedded notification events `+0x30` and `+0x48`, and cookie/status byte `+0x60`;
- a fifteenth custom record at `KVA-0096` had a zero callback and existed only in `raw-complete`. Its removal explains both the list-head transition and the sole detected persistent-node topology change: record `KVA-0097` changed Blink from that transient record to EAC sentinel `KVA-0098`.

This also corrects a stale earlier normalization: the custom sentinel is EAC RVA `0x30cb90`, not `0x2fcb90`. Exact rel32 decoding and the captured virtual/physical topology agree on `0x30cb90`. The companion package linked-callback-family-semantics (`private evidence: ../linked-callback-family-semantics/REPORT.md`) contains the function-level proof.

## Evidence classification and boundaries

| Finding | Classification | Boundary |
|---|---|---|
| Module/PDB identities, offsets, object bytes, list links, hashes, counts | Exact | Build/capture specific |
| EAC routine ownership | Derived exactly | Pointer must fall in a captured executable PE section |
| Six scheduler APC public names | Derived exactly | Matched PDB publics plus captured NT section mapping |
| Sixteen callback-shaped objects' true types | Exact | Closed by list-head ownership and function-level creation/query/removal analysis |
| No EAC work/DPC/APC/timer object in these lists | Exact captured-state negative | Not a historical non-occurrence claim |
| Timer linkage failures | Exact rejected observations | Non-atomic acquisition prevents a coherent cross-page list instant |
| EAC use of transient deferred objects outside the two captures | Unknown | Requires a capture overlapping that transient state |

## Reproduction and verification

The exact commands, input hashes, sidecars, and binary/source hashes are in input-provenance.json (`private evidence: input-provenance.json`). The collector has 15 unit tests; all pass, and `cargo clippy -- -D warnings` passes. Instrumented unit coverage is 26.13% regions / 24.02% lines for the full OS-connector binary; the `_KAPC`, `_KDPC`, `_KTIMER`, and `_WORK_QUEUE_ITEM` parsers, code-ownership predicates, address formulas, pointer gates, and layout-rejection classifiers are individually 100% line/region covered. Live memflow traversal branches are validated by the two full replay outputs rather than synthetic connector mocks.

validation.json (`private evidence: validation.json`) records 114/114 semantic, identity, integrity, provenance, ownership-closure, and safety checks passing before manifest sealing. deep-validation.json (`private evidence: deep-validation.json`) records 104/104 after independently rehashing both 19.33 GB snapshots. manifest.json (`private evidence: manifest.json`) hashes every package file.

# EAC DEVICE_CONTROL inner-handler semantics

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Scope and evidence boundary

This package evaluates the fourteen delegated handler entries and preserves the fifteenth inline path for the exact loaded image `cfc623de01a9c436c239c56a016d936a9070cdd2432596c06f75a3d88ae68081`. Captured bytes are decoded and interpreted offline. No driver instruction is executed natively, no device is opened, and no request or payload generator is produced. Results are exact only within the declared seeded-memory and differential probe domains.

The Phase-2 candidate set remains a set of fifteen distinct `METHOD_NEITHER` dispatcher classes, not a claim that all fifteen are accepted live controls. This package advances the inner ABI, exact field-touch sites, adjacent length/pointer transitions, call targets, returns, and writes without weakening that boundary.

## Handler ABI and seeded-path field surface

| Candidate | Entry RVA | stack-location register | exact modeled accesses |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| `0x0022e007` | `0xc79c0` | r9 | stack_location 0x10/4B x1, stack_location 0x20/8B x1 |
| `0x0022e00f` | `0xca4a3` | r8 | stack_location 0x10/4B x1, stack_location 0x20/8B x2, synthetic_irp 0x70/8B x1 |
| `0x0022e017` | `0xcaec2` | rdx | stack_location 0x10/4B x1, stack_location 0x20/8B x1 |
| `0x0022e01b` | `0xcb391` | rdx | stack_location 0x10/4B x1, stack_location 0x20/8B x1 |
| `0x0022e01f` | `0xcb637` | r9 | stack_location 0x8/4B x1, stack_location 0x10/4B x1, stack_location 0x20/8B x1, synthetic_irp 0x70/8B x1 |
| `0x0022e023` | `0xcc32f` | r9 | stack_location 0x10/4B x1, stack_location 0x20/8B x1 |
| `0x0022e043` | inline | - | top-level `+0x10/+0x20` evidence retained from Phase 2 |
| `0x0022e047` | `0xcc729` | r8 | none in seeded path |
| `0x0022e04b` | `0x576e6` | r9 | none in seeded path |
| `0x0022e04f` | `0xcd26c` | - | none in seeded path |
| `0x0022e053` | `0xcd4fa` | rcx | stack_location 0x10/4B x1, stack_location 0x20/8B x1 |
| `0x0022e05b` | `0xcd89e` | r9 | stack_location 0x10/4B x1, stack_location 0x20/8B x1 |
| `0x0022e063` | `0xcdd2e` | r8 | none in seeded path |
| `0x0022e067` | `0xce12d` | rcx | none in seeded path |
| `0x0022e06b` | `0xce405` | rcx | none in seeded path |

The top-level call records prove that the stack-location sentinel rotates across `RCX`, `RDX`, `R8`, and `R9` by handler. `0x22e04f` delegates to `0xcd26c` without carrying that sentinel in the four register arguments; the missing location is not guessed. Exact instruction RVAs and bytes for every modeled region access are in `analysis.json`.

The same top-level record also preserves the deterministic synthetic IRP pointer in seven delegated ABIs: `0x22e00f`/`0x22e01f` in `RCX`, `0x22e023`/`0x22e04b` in `RDX`, `0x22e053`/`0x22e06b` in `R8`, and `0x22e063` in `R9`.

## Newly closed bounded predicates

- `0x22e007` / `0xc79c0` reads `Type3InputBuffer` at stack-location `+0x20` and `InputBufferLength` at `+0x10`, with the stack location in `R9`.
- A null Type3 pointer takes a distinct early control-flow class. For every tested non-null pointer class, the adjacent probes `0x237` and `0x238` take different classes; the same transition was found by adaptive bisection and frozen here as `[567, 568]`. This states the exact tested boundary, not acceptance or a symbolic proof over all 32-bit lengths.
- The inline `0x22e043` path retains the earlier complete contiguous `0..32` length observation: values `0..8` and `9..32` occupy two exact classes.
- `0x22e01f` / `0xcb637` additionally reads output length at stack-location `+0x08` and a caller-context qword at `+0x70` in the distinct-argument model.
- The exact pointer identity proves that caller-context is the modeled IRP for `0x22e01f`; x64 ABI assignment identifies `IRP+0x70` as `UserBuffer`. The handler forwards `(InputBufferLength, Type3InputBuffer)` and `(OutputBufferLength, UserBuffer)` to the same state-derived callee and returns the zero-extended input length for every listed 32-bit probe.
- Complete contiguous `0..32` probes show a `0x22e00f` split at `15/16`, a single selected `0x22e01b` length of `12`, and a single selected `0x22e05b` length of `28`. These are handler-path predicates, not accepted packet-size claims.
- In the seeded `0x22e067` / `0xce12d` path, the second argument receives an exact four-byte zero write at offset `0`. Its higher-level field name is unknown because no accepted live packet was retained.

## Call and pointee boundary

The ledgers preserve every reached target identity and vary unmodeled call returns while holding context-return infrastructure fixed. Several paths reach internal RVAs `0x655fe`, `0xdff43`, `0xe0314`, and `0x3626c3`; other state-derived targets remain unresolved. None of the seeded baseline paths dereferenced the separate Type3 pointee region. That is a bounded negative for these modeled states only: it does not prove an accepted live request never dereferences a user pointer.

## Remaining blockers

Live acceptance, caller/handle authentication, complete packet layouts, accepted-state predicates, statuses at the IRP completion boundary, and the semantic ownership of state-derived callees remain unknown without retained real request state. The machine ledger records the first exact boundary rather than filling it with product-purpose inference.

`appendices/device-namespace-auth.md`:

```md
# EasyAntiCheat EOS device namespace and authorization boundary

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Result

I closed the device namespace, live object fields, link and driver-object ACLs, dispatch assignments, `IRP_MJ_CREATE`/`CLOSE` status path, `IRP_MJ_CLEANUP`, and captured per-handle `FILE_OBJECT` state for the exact second-capture image. I also bounded the part that is still not recoverable from the retained state: the historical device-creation API/security inputs, the effective object-manager decision that admitted each open, deeper IOCTL-handler authentication, any out-of-band state table, and exact global teardown/error-unwind order.

The authoritative loaded image is `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys` at capture base `KVA-0055`, size `0x2ae7000`, SHA-256 `779254e58f0c5881281af07a3afbd369af1824670d8cdb1fb7dd81ea7ae0e90a`. I performed this work offline over the frozen memflow acquisition. I did not instrument the guest, open the device, issue requests, execute sample code, or retain request/secret payloads.

Evidence labels in this report mean:

- **Observed exact**: bytes or object fields present in a retained capture.
- **ABI-derived**: a matched public kernel PDB or public Windows ABI names an exact captured offset/value.
- **Derived exact bounded static model**: exact captured instructions interpreted inside the declared offline model; not native execution.
- **Strongly inferred**: exact evidence converges, but a cross-capture or internal boundary remains.
- **Unknown**: the evidence does not prove the claim.

## Native namespace and device construction state

I re-walked all 37 buckets of the captured root, `GLOBAL??`, `Device`, and `Driver` object directories through offline memflow reads. The following names and relationships are **observed exact**:

| Object | Exact native name or target |
|---|---|
| Driver | `\Driver\EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys` |
| Device | `\Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS` |
| Symbolic link | `\GLOBAL??\EasyAntiCheat_EOS` |
| Link target | `\Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS` |

The captured `DEVICE_OBJECT` is **observed exact**, with the flag names **ABI-derived**:

| Field | Value | Meaning |
|---|---:|---|
| `DeviceType` | `0x22` | `FILE_DEVICE_UNKNOWN` |
| `Flags` | `0x40` | `DO_DEVICE_HAS_NAME` |
| `Characteristics` | `0x100` | `FILE_DEVICE_SECURE_OPEN` |
| `StackSize` | `1` | single device stack location |
| `DeviceExtension` | `0` | no device extension pointer |
| `AttachedDevice` | `0` | no attached upper device |
| `CurrentIrp` | `0` | no current IRP pointer in this snapshot |
| object-header security fast-reference | `0` | no security descriptor referenced by the captured device header |

The `0x40` flag is not buffered I/O; `DO_BUFFERED_IO` is `0x4`. The device instead exposes a method-neither control surface in the recovered dispatcher work.

I could not recover an exact call chain identifying `IoCreateDevice`, `IoCreateDeviceSecure`, a WDF equivalent, or the symbolic-link creation call. Those APIs are not ordinary imports in this image, and the protected DriverEntry closure does not reach a trustworthy dynamic resolver identity for them. Consequently, the creation API, original SDDL, class GUID, symbolic-link creation API, and provenance of the null device security reference are all **unknown**. I do not reconstruct an SDDL string from the later ACL bytes and call it the original input.

## ACLs and the object-manager open boundary

The symbolic-link object has a self-relative descriptor (`Control=0x8004`) with this **observed-exact** DACL:

| ACE | SID | Allow mask |
|---:|---|---:|
| 0 | Everyone (`S-1-1-0`) | `0x00020001` |
| 1 | Local System (`S-1-5-18`) | `0x000f0001` |
| 2 | Built-in Administrators (`S-1-5-32-544`) | `0x000f0001` |
| 3 | All Application Packages (`S-1-5-12`) | `0x00020001` |

The driver object has a separate self-relative descriptor with this **observed-exact** DACL:

| ACE | SID | Allow mask |
|---:|---|---:|
| 0 | Local System (`S-1-5-18`) | `0x001f01ff` |
| 1 | Built-in Administrators (`S-1-5-32-544`) | `0x001200a9` |

These descriptors apply to the link object and driver object respectively. Neither is evidence of the target device's effective DACL. `FILE_DEVICE_SECURE_OPEN` is relevant to security checks for opens in a device namespace, but it cannot supply the missing target descriptor or creation-time access state. Because the captured target `DEVICE_OBJECT` header security fast-reference is exactly null, the effective object-manager ACL decision, caller token evaluation, requested `DesiredAccess`, and share-access arguments for the historical successful opens remain **unknown**.

Two successful handles are nevertheless **observed exact** and stable from the early to steady snapshots:

| Process role | PID | Handle | Granted access | Stability |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| EAC-suffixed game wrapper | 2184 | `0x6d8` | `0x0012019f` | same handle, file object, and grant in both phases |
| protected game | 8592 | `0x8` | `0x0012019f` | same handle, file object, and grant in both phases |

The grant contains `READ_CONTROL`, `SYNCHRONIZE`, and the file-specific read/write/data/EA/attribute bits listed in the machine ledger. Presence of these handles proves successful opens occurred; it does not recover the original requested mask or the exact object-manager access-check path.

## `IRP_MJ_CREATE` and `IRP_MJ_CLOSE`

The captured `DRIVER_OBJECT.MajorFunction` table assigns both CREATE (`0x00`) and CLOSE (`0x02`) to EAC RVA `0xcca29`. I rebound the existing protected-code interpreter to the exact second-capture base and image hash, verified the dispatch and save-context stubs byte-for-byte, and ran alternating CREATE/CLOSE trials with distinct initial `IoStatus`, `Information`, stack major code, `FILE_OBJECT` context sentinels, and randomized VM context.

The full common route is **derived exact bounded static model** evidence:

```text
0x2392537 -> 0x43e238 -> 0x28d7d08 -> 0x362005 -> 0x4251b7
-> 0x243d1de -> 0x43e238 -> 0x28d7d08 -> 0x37987b
-> 0x11ff73c -> 0x43e238 -> 0x28d7d08 -> 0x39d4e9
-> 0x2893bf9 -> 0x43e238 -> 0x28d7d08 -> 0x36287b
-> 0x3a0b9f -> 0x1dfdde -> 0x43e238 -> 0x28d7d08 -> 0x384135
```

Only two request writes occur:

| Instruction RVA | IRP offset | Exact effect |
|---:|---:|---|
| `0x28d839a` | `+0x30` | `IoStatus.Status = STATUS_SUCCESS (0)` |
| `0x28d83a9` | `+0x38` | `IoStatus.Information = 0` |

The path resolves and calls `ntoskrnl!IofCompleteRequest` at matched-kernel RVA `0x3ab770` with the exact IRP pointer and priority boost zero. The final restore/return stub at EAC RVA `0x384135` restores `RAX=0`, so the dispatch routine also returns `STATUS_SUCCESS`.

Across the bounded model, I observed no reads from the IRP, `DEVICE_OBJECT`, `FILE_OBJECT`, or current stack location; no CREATE/CLOSE branch predicate; no process/token query; and no write to a per-handle field. This closes the direct CREATE/CLOSE driver path as status-only. It does not prove that object-manager access checks before driver dispatch were permissive, nor does it exclude an out-of-band global map maintained somewhere outside this path.

## `IRP_MJ_CLEANUP`

`IRP_MJ_CLEANUP` (`0x12`) is not assigned an EAC handler. Its exact captured dispatch pointer resolves through the matched second-capture kernel to `ntoskrnl!IopInvalidDeviceRequest` at RVA `0x3ab220`.

That exact routine:

1. writes `0xc0000010` (`STATUS_INVALID_DEVICE_REQUEST`) to `IRP.IoStatus.Status` at `+0x30`;
2. calls `IofCompleteRequest(Irp, IO_NO_INCREMENT)`;
3. returns `0xc0000010`.

It does not write `IoStatus.Information`. Therefore this driver has no custom cleanup path and no cleanup-time release of a `FILE_OBJECT` context. CLOSE still runs through the shared success path described above.

## Captured `FILE_OBJECT` and per-handle state

I parsed four exact 0xd8-byte `FILE_OBJECT` bodies using the captured kernel's matched public PDB (`_FILE_OBJECT` type index `0x13cf`). The early and steady images are byte-identical per role:

| Role | SHA-256 (early = steady) | Flags |
|---|---|---|
| wrapper | `0cb2e0c9766007deba7e98ba6d4c9c8797e767bd63c91125c2792fe802c51104` | `0x00040002` = `FO_HANDLE_CREATED | FO_SYNCHRONOUS_IO` |
| protected game | `b92bff055de9e4159e783326b3285f15370100655919821d6bedebc2a57da221` | `0x00040000` = `FO_HANDLE_CREATED` |

For all four snapshots, these fields are exactly zero or empty:

- `FsContext`
- `FsContext2`
- `FileObjectExtension`
- `SectionObjectPointer`
- `PrivateCacheMap`
- `CompletionContext`
- `RelatedFileObject`
- `FileName.Length`, `FileName.MaximumLength`, and `FileName.Buffer`

The IRP list is empty/self-linked for each object. The `ReadAccess`, `WriteAccess`, `DeleteAccess`, and share booleans are also zero in the retained bodies; I do not reinterpret those zeros as the handle table's granted mask.

The exact conclusion is narrower than “there is no authentication”: no cookie or state exists in the captured `FILE_OBJECT` fields, neither direct CREATE nor CLOSE allocates or releases one, and CLEANUP is generic invalid-request handling. A separately allocated process/session table keyed by some other identity remains **unknown**.

## DEVICE_CONTROL authorization boundary

The `DRIVER_OBJECT` assigns DEVICE_CONTROL to EAC RVA `0xccaaf`. In the exact recovered common prefix, the only request-side reads before candidate-specific delegation are:

| Instruction RVA | ABI-derived field |
|---:|---|
| `0x1d7202a` | `IRP.CurrentStackLocation` at `IRP+0xb8` |
| `0x1d722b2` | `IO_STACK_LOCATION.Parameters.DeviceIoControl.IoControlCode` at stack `+0x18` |

There is no common-prefix read of `IO_STACK_LOCATION.FileObject`, `FILE_OBJECT.FsContext`, `FsContext2`, caller process, requestor mode, or token, and no recovered process/token resolver call before delegation. This is a **bounded negative**, not a claim that every descendant lacks authorization.

The 15 distinct dispatcher candidates recovered in the previously swept `DeviceType=0x22`, access-bits `3`, 12-bit function, all-method domain encode `FILE_READ_ACCESS | FILE_WRITE_ACCESS`. Both captured handle grants contain the required low access bits, so the public CTL_CODE/I/O-manager access gate is **ABI-derived satisfied** for those handles. This does not prove any candidate is accepted after driver-specific checks.

The inner-handler package closes several stack length/pointer reads and first call boundaries, but it does not close real caller/handle authentication, packet acceptance, state predicates, or completion statuses. Those deeper decisions are **unknown**. I preserve that boundary instead of turning absence from a synthetic common-prefix trace into a bypass claim.

## Per-handle and global lifecycle

The per-handle lifecycle is closed as follows:

| Phase | Exact result |
|---|---|
| CREATE | shared EAC path; `IoStatus.Status=0`, `Information=0`, completion priority 0, return 0; no object/request reads |
| CLEANUP | kernel invalid-request path; status/return `0xc0000010`; no custom state cleanup |
| CLOSE | same shared EAC path as CREATE; status/information/return 0; no custom context teardown |
| final reclamation | Windows object-manager reference lifecycle (**ABI-derived**) |

In the exact second frozen snapshot, `DriverUnload` points into `FLTMGR.SYS+0x510c0`; the private target identity is not assigned without a matching symbol identity.

I also correlated a separate passive memflow lifecycle capture with the same PE machine/timestamp/size/entry tuple. It is not cryptographically bound to this exact loaded-image snapshot, so I label the binding **strongly inferred** and keep it supplemental. Within that supplemental capture, the state transitions themselves are **observed exact**:

- normal state: unload target `FLTMGR.SYS+0x510c0`, three process/thread/image registrations, four minifilter instances;
- teardown sample: the same driver/device objects have unload target EAC RVA `0xce846`, callback registrations are empty, and minifilter instances are zero;
- the filter disappears 4,236.984 ms after the unload-pointer transition;
- the driver/device disappear 4,532.664 ms after that transition, 295.680 ms after filter disappearance;
- reload produces fresh driver/device/filter identities, three registrations, and four instances.

Sampling is non-atomic, so these timestamps establish observed state order, not causal call order. The exact unload invocation, link/device deletion call chain, callback removal order inside the unload routine, and partial-initialization rollback remain **unknown**.

## Closure matrix

| Question | Result | Evidence class |
|---|---|---|
| exact driver/device/link names and target | closed | observed exact |
| device type, flags, characteristics, stack/extension | closed | observed exact + ABI-derived names |
| link and driver-object ACLs | closed | observed exact |
| target device DACL/effective object-manager open check | open | unknown |
| creation API, original SDDL/class GUID, link API | open | unknown |
| CREATE status, information, completion, return, predicates | closed in exact bounded model | derived exact bounded static model |
| CLEANUP status/completion/return | closed | observed exact + ABI-derived kernel identity |
| CLOSE status, information, completion, return, teardown | closed in exact bounded model | derived exact bounded static model |
| captured `FILE_OBJECT` contexts/cookies | closed: absent in all retained fields/phases | observed exact + ABI-derived layout |
| IOCTL coarse CTL access gate for captured handles | closed: satisfied | ABI-derived |
| common-prefix FILE_OBJECT/process/token gate | closed: none recovered before delegation | derived exact bounded static model |
| deeper handler caller/token/global-state authorization | open | unknown |
| global teardown state transitions | supplemental closure | observed exact in supplemental capture; binding strongly inferred |
| exact global deletion/unwind call order | open | unknown |

## Explicit remaining-work ledger

| ID | Unresolved question | First exact boundary | Evidence that would close it |
|---|---|---|---|
| `DNA-U1` | Which creation/link APIs and security inputs were used? | protected DriverEntry dynamic resolver/call chain | exact resolver closure or retained creation-time call context |
| `DNA-U2` | Which object-manager ACL decision admitted each open? | null captured device SD and absent historical access state | creation-time security provenance plus open access-state capture |
| `DNA-U3` | Do delegated handlers authenticate process/token/global state? | opaque/state-derived calls and return-dependent descendants | passively retained real request state or broader exact symbolic closure |
| `DNA-U4` | Is state stored out of band rather than in `FILE_OBJECT`? | no recovered map ownership/key relation | exact map update/lookup tied to a captured file object or process |
| `DNA-U5` | What is the exact global teardown and partial-init rollback order? | non-atomic transitions and unresolved unload body | exact unload/error-unwind closure or passive call-context evidence |

These are capture/closure blockers, not requests for a privileged client or active request generator. No such artifact is included.

## Reproduction and validation

The machine-readable authority is `device-namespace-auth-ledger.json`. `input-provenance.json` binds every external source by path, size, and SHA-256; `raw/namespace-extraction.json` binds every retained namespace/object artifact. The RED baseline is retained in `red-baseline.txt`. Ten unit tests pass, and Python's standard-library line tracer reports 93.5% implementation coverage, above the required 80% threshold. `validation.py`, `validation.json`, and `manifest.json` independently re-check source hashes, raw structures, image identity, ledger regeneration, tests, coverage, syntax, report markers, and package membership before `COMPLETE` is created.
