# Phase-3 1.5M-sample callback execution analysis

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Bound capture

- Capture manifest SHA-256: `7eb5f0b07319def862f8b92382bde1996882ea0a9082232333a2cf3ffe2757bb`
- Termination: duration complete; 1,500,000 contiguous samples in 300.069736 seconds.
- Effective sampling rate: 4998.838003 Hz.
- Full manifest verification: `pass` across 15 frozen entries.
- Summary cross-check: `pass`.

## Exact callback-body sightings

- `0x215cdd` (create_set_information_preop_body_entry): sequences [515823, 796437]; 2 exact stack-qword sightings.
- `0x21664b` (acquire_for_section_synchronization_preop_body_entry): sequences [502235, 543069]; 2 exact stack-qword sightings.
- `0x21678c` (acquire_for_section_synchronization_body_continuation): sequences [502235, 543069]; 2 exact stack-qword sightings.

These are exact RVAs from the bound EAC image observed as qwords in selected active kernel-thread stack windows. The original collector did not unwind frames, so the ledger preserves that attribution boundary.

## Why zero validated callback objects is non-negative evidence

Every one of the four callback-body sighting samples had exactly 128 callback-pointer candidates, the compiled hard cap. The frozen collector source proves that it scans all aligned canonical kernel qwords from EAC-bearing stack windows into one sample-global map, stops at 128, and only then performs paired `FLT_CALLBACK_DATA` and Iopb validation. A true object appearing after the first 128 distinct qwords is therefore unobservable to that validator. The run's zero validated objects cannot be interpreted as callback non-execution.

## Timing and blind bounds

- Sample duration (us): {'min': 101, 'p50': 134, 'p95': 228, 'p99': 359, 'max': 41563}.
- Schedule lateness (us): {'min': 0, 'p50': 51, 'p95': 2235, 'p99': 12370, 'max': 55217}.
- Previous-end to next-start gap (us): {'min': 1, 'p50': 62, 'p95': 96, 'p99': 118, 'max': 39428}.
- Batch partial failures: 3,000,076; item-stage validations under partial aggregate status: 16,058.

The capture remains sequential and lock-free from the guest's perspective. Paired reads reject some races but do not make the whole sample atomic; unsampled intervals, unselected threads, and the pre-validation candidate cap remain explicit blind bounds.

## Verification

- Unit tests: 6/6 pass.
- Statement coverage: 93.2% for the streaming analyzer.
- Validation status: `pass`.

# EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys function census

## Exact identity and census totals

- Resident SHA-256: `cfc623de01a9c436c239c56a016d936a9070cdd2432596c06f75a3d88ae68081`

- The modeled call at `0x137e5e` resolves to target RVA `0xdfce7`; `0xcfce7` is instruction interior.

`appendices/interface-dataflow.md`:

```md
# EAC early-boot interface and data-flow forensic report

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Scope and conclusion

This report is limited to read-only, offline memflow analysis of the two sealed guest-physical acquisitions in `eac-memflow-second-20260913T235113Z`. No guest command, hook, launch, replay, or live instrumentation was used.

The recoverable integration path is unusually concrete:

1. **[OBSERVED + DERIVED MEMBER SELECTION]** `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.exe` (PID 9396) retained a 96 MiB pagefile-backed section named `EasyAntiCheat_EOSBin`; the exact non-image `_SEGMENT` union interpretation conditionally selects PID 9396 as `CreatingProcessId`. The historical create call itself was not observed.
2. **[OBSERVED]** the full-path EAC-suffixed process `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping_EAC_EOS.exe` (PID 2184) held the same section object and the same `EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventGame` event object. This is the process containing the second recovered EOSBin view; it is not the protected PID 8592 process.
3. **[OBSERVED]** the section's committed prefix held an envelope followed by the complete raw `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys`, including its PE Security Directory. The two process views were byte-identical and their embedded raw bytes matched all four independent watchdog captures.
4. **[OBSERVED]** PID 2184 and the protected/memflow-skeletal `FortniteClient` (PID 8592) each held a distinct `FILE_OBJECT` to the same `EasyAntiCheat_EOS` device and `EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys` driver object. Both device handles and their access masks were invariant across early and steady acquisitions.
5. **[OBSERVED]** early `EventGame` was unsignaled and had EAC thread 5644 on its wait list; at steady state the same event object was signaled and had no waiter. `EventModule` and `EventDriver` were already signaled at both phases.
6. **[OBSERVED]** EAC, PID 2184, and later PID 8592 held file/device surfaces to ACPI/TPM, CNG, KsecDD, NSI, AFD, certificate stores, named pipes, and the filesystem. **[BOUNDARY]** an open handle proves object reachability and granted rights, not a completed query, its parameters, or returned data.
7. **[OBSERVED]** two valid steady-state IRPs survived in protected PID 8592: an NPFS named-pipe listen and an NTFS directory-change notification on the user's `My` certificate-store directory. Neither targeted the EAC device. No valid EAC-device IRP remained in an ETHREAD list, `DEVICE_OBJECT.CurrentIrp`, or its device queue.

There is no evidence in these two acquisitions of executable-section mutation after relocation normalization: five recovered loaded images matched the raw executable sections byte-for-byte. Four temporal loaded-image snapshots changed only 14 writable `.data` pages and zero executable pages.

## Claim vocabulary

- **[OBSERVED]**: bytes or fields read from the sealed image, or an exact equality/hash over those bytes.
- **[DERIVED]**: deterministic interpretation of observed bytes using the exact-build structure layout or PE format.
- **[INFERRED]**: a lifecycle/data-flow explanation that fits the observations but is not itself stored as a historical record.
- **[UNKNOWN]**: not retained in the sampled state or outside the resolver's coverage.

## Evidence set and acquisition boundary

| Phase | Physical image | Acquisition-metadata SHA-256 | Size | Failed pages | Filesystem mtime (UTC) |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| early/raw-complete | `physical-raw-complete/guest-physical.raw` | `4b8fc07340473e6148d1032d5e388fa2c80de646b01c9edb984f667ab292a03a` | 19,327,352,832 | 0 | 2026-09-14 00:00:19.014774 |
| steady-state | `physical-steady-state/guest-physical.raw` | `f6952b001bbd3b1e0762731c8b3cf07173dcef1274e3a2ab60e6ff2c8c8ea13b` | 19,327,352,832 | 0 | 2026-09-14 00:03:05.462407 |

**[OBSERVED]** the collector was armed at `2026-09-13T23:58:24Z`, the raw-complete marker was observed at `23:59:47Z`, and acquisition stopped/completed at `2026-09-14T00:04:29Z`.

**[BOUNDARY]** each physical image is a sequential sweep of the guest-physical address space, not an atomic VM snapshot. The file mtime gives ordering, not one exact guest instruction boundary. Pointer/value invariance across both sweeps therefore carries more weight than a transient single-list field. The two invalid steady System-thread IRP-list targets discussed below are treated as torn/inconsistent candidates for this reason.

Build-matched offsets came from the live kernel's CodeView GUID `BEA4BE7B-19EE-F0C9-7610-A5ECE65CAE0C`, image age 1, and the corresponding stripped Microsoft public PDB type graph. The public PDB has Info-stream age 6 and DBI-stream age 1; the GUID and DBI age match the image, while the Info age satisfies the public-PDB validation rule of being at least the image age. The complete field ledger is `ledgers/symbol-offsets.json`; raw identity, age, and type-record evidence is in `ledgers/io-stack-layout-pdb.json`.

## Process roles: keep the two Fortnite identities separate

| Role in this evidence | PID | EPROCESS | memflow name/path visibility | Early state | Steady state |
|---|---:|---:|---|---|---|
| EAC service | 9396 | `KVA-0038` | `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.exe`, full path visible | 449 handles; 10 active / 11 linked threads | 398 handles; 3 active / 4 linked threads |
| Full-path EAC-suffixed wrapper/launcher-side process | 2184 | `KVA-0039` | `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping_EAC_EOS.exe`, full path visible | 431 handles; 11 active / 12 linked threads | 431 handles; 7 active / 8 linked threads |
| Protected game process | 8592 | `KVA-0040` | skeletal `FortniteClient`; empty path/command line; DTB `PA-0004` yielded no memflow modules or ranges | 2 handles; 1 active / 2 linked threads | 6,672 handles; 153 active / 159 linked threads |

**[OBSERVED]** kernel object-table and ETHREAD evidence for PID 8592 remained readable even though its protected address space did not. **[BOUNDARY]** this permits kernel-object claims for PID 8592, but no PID-8592 user-space mapping, module, or EOSBin-view claim.

The linked-thread count exceeded `EPROCESS.ActiveThreads` in every process/phase. **[DERIVED]** this is a list-versus-counter state difference during a sequential sweep; it is not sufficient by itself to identify the extra linked entries as hidden or malicious.

## Named-object synchronization and exact linkage

### Invariant handles

| Owning process | Handle | Type/name | Object body | Access | Early -> steady |
|---|---:|---|---:|---:|---|
| EAC PID 9396 | `0x2d8` | Section `EasyAntiCheat_EOSBin` | `KVA-0041` | `0xf0007` | exact invariant |
| PID 2184 | `0x6d4` | Section `EasyAntiCheat_EOSBin` | `KVA-0041` | `0x6` | exact invariant |
| EAC PID 9396 | `0x2dc` | Event `EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventModule` | `KVA-0042` | `0x1f0003` | exact invariant |
| EAC PID 9396 | `0x2e0` | Event `EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventDriver` | `KVA-0043` | `0x1f0003` | exact invariant |
| EAC PID 9396 | `0x2e4` | Event `EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventGame` | `KVA-0044` | `0x1f0003` | exact invariant |
| PID 2184 | `0x6d0` | Event `EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventGame` | `KVA-0044` | `0x100002` | exact invariant |
| PID 2184 | `0x1e4` | Semaphore `EasyAntiCheatLauncherSemaphore` | `KVA-0045` | `0x1f0003` | exact invariant |

**[DERIVED]** the section rights decode as:

- EAC `0xf0007`: standard object rights plus `SECTION_QUERY`, `SECTION_MAP_WRITE`, and `SECTION_MAP_READ`.
- PID 2184 `0x6`: `SECTION_MAP_WRITE` and `SECTION_MAP_READ`.

The exact shared body pointers prove that the EAC and PID-2184 handles refer to the same kernel section and event, not merely same-named objects.

### Event state transition

| Event | Early | Steady |
|---|---|---|
| `EventModule` body `KVA-0042` | signal state 1; empty wait list | signal state 1; empty wait list |
| `EventDriver` body `KVA-0043` | signal state 1; empty wait list | signal state 1; empty wait list |
| `EventGame` body `KVA-0044` | signal state 0; one waiter | signal state 1; empty wait list |

The early `EventGame` wait entry is exact:

- `KWAIT_BLOCK`: `KVA-0046`
- `ETHREAD`: `KVA-0047`; PID 9396; TID 5644
- wait type 1; block state 4; wait key 2
- wait object's body: `KVA-0044`, equal to both processes' `EventGame` body
- thread start: `ntdll.dll+0x8c510`; Win32 start: `sechost.dll+0x1e2b0`; trap RIP: `wow64cpu.dll+0x1f83`

**[INFERRED, high confidence]** the observed ordering is consistent with EAC having completed its module and driver stages, then waiting for the PID-2184/game-stage handshake; the steady sweep captures that final event signaled. The event names and transition do not expose the code path that signaled them.

## EOSBin section graph and recovered content

### Kernel object graph

**[OBSERVED]** both section handles resolve to one graph:

```text
SECTION KVA-0041, Size 0x6000000 (96 MiB)
  -> CONTROL_AREA KVA-0048
       SectionRefs 1, PfnRefs 0, MappedViews 2, UserRefs 3
       FilePointer 0x0
       -> SEGMENT KVA-0049
            TotalPtes 0x6000, Size 0x6000000
            u1 raw 0x24b4 -> CreatingProcessId 9396
            u2 raw 0x45a0000 -> FirstMappedVa 0x45a0000
            PrototypePte KVA-0050
```

**[EXACT STRUCTURE, DERIVED MEMBER SELECTION]** the matched public PDB defines `SEGMENT.u1` at `+0x30` as the `ImageCommitment/CreatingProcessId` union and `SEGMENT.u2` at `+0x38` as the `ImageInformation/FirstMappedVa` union. The resolver preserves both raw values. Here `_MMSECTION_FLAGS.Image=0` and `CONTROL_AREA.FilePointer=0`, so it selects the non-image, null-file-pointer members rather than naming the unions unconditionally.

The two matching VADs are:

- EAC PID 9396: `0x45a0000..0xa59ffff`, full 96 MiB reserve.
- PID 2184: `0x1e8b0100000..0x1e8b60fffff`, full 96 MiB reserve.

**[DERIVED]** the null `CONTROL_AREA.FilePointer`, two mapped views, and matching segment support a pagefile-backed shared-memory classification. There is no file-backed section path to recover from this control area.

### Exact content recovery

The offline memory-map walker found a readable/writable, non-executable committed prefix of `0x2a99000` bytes in each view:

- EAC: `0x45a0000..0x7038fff`
- PID 2184: `0x1e8b0100000..0x1e8b2b98fff`

Both recovered view files have SHA-256 `3f1f4f15421ae324849ba4856ce4eaf4c606f42edac3a3775168bc699111b6ae`.

At view offset `0x18`, the envelope records raw length `0x2a98e98`; raw PE bytes begin at `0x1c`, followed by 332 trailing bytes in the committed view. The embedded raw PE is:

- SHA-256: `020d5da6b881408ced33be09b92dc45e12e641b8b3c91f3a327429e5027fef74`
- machine `0x8664`, PE32+ `0x20b`, timestamp `0x6a9054fa`
- 9 sections, preferred image base `0x140000000`
- `SizeOfImage 0x2ae7000`, entry RVA `0x2594f8`
- Security Directory file offset `0x2a93c00`, size `0x5298`, end `0x2a98e98` -- exactly the recorded raw length

**[OBSERVED]** the EAC and PID-2184 embedded raw bytes are identical, all four watchdog raw captures are identical, and the offline raw hash equals the watchdog hash. This ties the named shared section directly to the exact loaded kernel image identity without reconstructing a file from sparse pages.

**[BOUNDARY]** only the `0x2a99000` readable committed prefix was copied. The remainder of the 96 MiB reserve was not claimed as resident content.

## Driver device interface

### Two independent file handles, one device

| Process | Handle | FILE_OBJECT | Access | DEVICE_OBJECT | DRIVER_OBJECT | Stability |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|---:|---|
| PID 2184 full-path EAC-suffixed process | `0x6d8` | `KVA-0051` | `0x12019f` | `KVA-0052` | `KVA-0053` | exact invariant |
| PID 8592 protected `FortniteClient` | `0x8` | `KVA-0054` | `0x12019f` | `KVA-0052` | `KVA-0053` | exact invariant |

**[OBSERVED]** the two `FILE_OBJECT` bodies are distinct, while both point to the same named device `EasyAntiCheat_EOS` and driver `\Driver\EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys`. The PID-8592 device handle already existed when that process had only two allocated handles, making it the earliest recoverable protected-process integration object.

The common device and driver fields are:

- device type 34 (`FILE_DEVICE_UNKNOWN`), flags `0x40` (`DO_DEVICE_HAS_NAME`), characteristics `0x100` (`FILE_DEVICE_SECURE_OPEN`), stack size 1, no attached device
- driver base `KVA-0055`, size `0x2ae7000`
- `DriverInit KVA-0056` = EAC RVA `0x2594f8`
- `IRP_MJ_DEVICE_CONTROL KVA-0057` = EAC RVA `0xccaaf`
- `IRP_MJ_INTERNAL_DEVICE_CONTROL KVA-0058` = `ntoskrnl.exe+0x3ab220`
- `DriverUnload KVA-0059` = `FLTMGR.SYS+0x510c0`

**[DERIVED]** access `0x12019f` includes synchronization/read-control plus file read, write, append, EA, and attribute rights. **[BOUNDARY]** this is the granted file-object mask, not an IOCTL allow-list.

**[INFERRED, moderate confidence]** a `DriverUnload` target inside FLTMGR is consistent with filter-manager registration replacing/wrapping the unload path. This runtime resolver did not enumerate private `_FLT_FILTER` or `_FLT_INSTANCE` objects. The separate static Phase-2 analysis has since recovered an exact `FLT_REGISTRATION` at EAC RVA `0x2ea4a0`, with CREATE, SET_INFORMATION, and ACQUIRE_FOR_SECTION_SYNCHRONIZATION pre-operation registrations; that static structure strengthens the minifilter-registration interpretation but does not fill the missing live `_FLT_FILTER`/instance graph.

## File, network, ACPI/TPM, and crypto surfaces

### Early inventory across all three processes

The resolver retained 26 File handles: 13 in EAC, 12 in PID 2184, and the single EAC-device handle in PID 8592. Categories were:

| Category | Count |
|---|---:|
| ACPI/TPM stack | 1 |
| EAC driver device | 2 |
| EAC log files | 2 |
| filesystem/volume | 8 |
| CNG/KsecDD crypto devices | 6 |
| AFD/NSI network surfaces | 4 |
| Null device | 3 |

High-value exact rows:

- EAC handle `0x2b8`, access `0x12019f`, `FILE_OBJECT KVA-0060` -> ACPI device `KVA-0061` named `00000019` -> attached TPM device `KVA-0062`.
- EAC KsecDD handles `0x34c` (`0x100003`) and `0x62c` (`0x100001`); CNG handle `0x358` (`0x100001`).
- EAC NSI handle `0x3cc` (`0x100080`) and AFD `\Endpoint` handle `0x51c` (`0x16019f`).
- PID 2184 CNG handle `0x298`; KsecDD handles `0x334`/`0x380`; AFD `\AsyncConnectHlp` `0x3c4`; NSI `0x3e0`; EAC device `0x6d8`.
- EAC log `\Windows\SysWOW64\config\systemprofile\AppData\Roaming\EasyAntiCheat\service.log`, handle `0x288`, with non-null shared-cache and image-section objects.
- PID 2184 log `\Users\<redacted:class=user_profile_path_component,length=5>\AppData\Roaming\EasyAntiCheat\prod-fn\<redacted:class=high_entropy_id,length=32>\anticheatlauncher.log`, handle `0x26c`, with non-null shared-cache and image-section objects.

The volume-attached stack reachable from those file objects is exact: `volmgr -> fvevol -> iorate -> volume -> volsnap`. This establishes the reachable device chain, not which layer completed a historical request.

### Steady protected-process expansion

**[OBSERVED]** PID 8592 expanded from 2 to 6,672 allocated handles and from 1 to 150 File handles while retaining exact device handle `0x8`. Its 150 steady File handles resolve by direct driver to:

- `volmgr`: 117
- `AFD`: 12
- `Npfs`: 9
- `DeviceApi`: 6
- `KSecDD`: 2
- `CNG`, `EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys`, `nsiproxy`, and `pcw`: 1 each

**[OBSERVED]** EAC's early AFD `\Endpoint` handle `0x51c` was closed by steady state; the rest of the EAC/PID-2184 high-value file graph remained stable. **[INFERRED, moderate confidence]** closure of that AFD endpoint and the two EAC Winsock catalog keys below is consistent with completion of an early network/bootstrap phase. Peer addresses, DNS names, TLS material, request bytes, and responses are not encoded in the retained handle graph.

**[OBSERVED + ABI-DERIVED]** the corrected v5 handle-rooted AFD parse recovers an early service-owned endpoint with signature `0xafd2`, state `4`, flags `0x100`, and a stable `AF_INET` remote structure on port `443`; the exact peer bytes remain sealed in the restricted ledger. The same early image has one PID-2184 wrapper endpoint with no remote pointer. At steady state, 13 handle-rooted endpoints are admitted: one PID-2184 wrapper endpoint and 12 PID-8592 protected-game endpoints. Both global-list walks fail node/backlink validation, so their zero global-list count is not absence evidence and the admitted handle-rooted set is not a system-wide endpoint census.

**[UNKNOWN]** the exact ACPI/TPM method, CNG/KsecDD operation, AFD initiating call, connect/send completion, packets, payloads, DNS/TLS behavior, and returned data: no matching valid in-flight IRP survived.

## Registry KCB names

All resolver-visible `CM_KEY_BODY -> CM_KEY_CONTROL_BLOCK -> NameBlock` parent chains resolved. The ledger preserves both:

- `raw_parent_components`: exact recursive KCB names as stored, including duplicated hive-root naming where present;
- `full_path`: a deterministic canonical collapse to `\REGISTRY\...`.

Counts:

| Phase/process | Resolved Key handles |
|---|---:|
| early EAC PID 9396 | 53 |
| early PID 2184 | 47 |
| early PID 8592 | 0 |
| steady EAC PID 9396 | 51 |
| steady PID 2184 | 47 |
| steady PID 8592 | 97 |

The early-to-steady total is 100 -> 195: 97 PID-8592 additions and two EAC removals. The two removed EAC KCBs are exact:

- handle `0x388`, body `KVA-0063`, KCB `KVA-0064`, access `0xf003f`: `\REGISTRY\MACHINE\SYSTEM\CONTROLSET001\SERVICES\WINSOCK2\PARAMETERS\PROTOCOL_CATALOG9`
- handle `0x390`, body `KVA-0065`, KCB `KVA-0066`, access `0xf003f`: `\REGISTRY\MACHINE\SYSTEM\CONTROLSET001\SERVICES\WINSOCK2\PARAMETERS\NAMESPACE_CATALOG5`

Other directly observed families include:

- EAC: Image File Execution Options, TCPIP/TCPIP6 interface roots, Cryptography OID configuration, Crypt32, machine/default-user certificate stores, policies, NLS, Explorer folder descriptions.
- steady PID 8592: Winsock catalogs, TCPIP/TCPIP6 interface roots, Internet Settings/policies, machine and user certificate stores (`Root`, `AuthRoot`, `CA`, `Trust`, `Disallowed`, `TrustedPeople`, `SmartCardRoot`, `My`), Cryptography OID configuration, audio MMDevices, WindowsRuntime, MediaFoundation, and package-deployment keys.

**[BOUNDARY]** KCB existence plus access rights shows a live registry surface. It does not prove a particular value was read or set, preserve the value returned, or establish that EAC rather than a linked Windows library opened the key.

## Threads, stacks, and retained IRPs

### Thread inventory and EAC range attribution

The resolver walked all linked ETHREAD lists with exact start, Win32-start, trap-frame, stack-bound, state, wait-reason, and IRP-list fields. It also walked 216 System threads early and 220 steady.

- No System thread had an exact `StartAddress`, `Win32StartAddress`, or trap RIP inside the EAC module in either acquisition.
- Raw aligned qwords within active `KernelStack..StackBase` storage fell inside EAC's declared module range on 15 System threads early and 18 steady.
- **[BOUNDARY]** these are address-range candidates, not unwind-proven return addresses. They may include arguments, data pointers, or stale-but-still-active stack storage. They must not be promoted to “EAC-owned system threads.”

The complete per-thread candidates and exact RVAs are in `ledgers/threads-irps.json`.

### Two valid protected-process IRPs at steady state

**[OBSERVED]** PID 8592 TID 6728 (`ETHREAD KVA-0067`) retained IRP `KVA-0068`:

- valid type 6, size `0x160`, requestor mode 1, one-entry complete IRP list
- original file `[redacted account-derived pipe component]\Fortnite`
- current major `IRP_MJ_FILE_SYSTEM_CONTROL`
- exact current-stack `FsControlCode 0x110008`, which decodes to `FSCTL_PIPE_LISTEN`; `Type3InputBuffer` at `+0x20` is null
- device stack NPFS `NamedPipe` -> attached FLTMGR device
- one raw active-stack candidate at EAC RVA `0x15a05d`

**[OBSERVED]** PID 8592 TID 7092 (`ETHREAD KVA-0069`) retained IRP `KVA-0070`:

- valid type 6, size `0x508`, requestor mode 1, one-entry complete IRP list
- original file `\Users\<redacted:class=user_profile_path_component,length=5>\AppData\Roaming\Microsoft\SystemCertificates\My`
- current major/minor `IRP_MJ_DIRECTORY_CONTROL / 2`
- **[DERIVED]** minor 2 is `IRP_MN_NOTIFY_CHANGE_DIRECTORY`; output length `0x20`; completion filter `0x1b`, requesting file-name, directory-name, size, and last-write changes
- device stack NTFS -> attached FLTMGR device
- one raw active-stack candidate at EAC RVA `0x15a05d`

Neither IRP's current device stack contains the EAC device or driver. **[BOUNDARY]** the identical raw EAC-range qword on both threads is not enough to claim EAC issued either IRP.

### EAC-device queue and invalid System-list candidates

- EAC `DEVICE_OBJECT.CurrentIrp` was null at both phases.
- Its `KDEVICE_QUEUE` walked complete with zero entries at both phases.
- EAC, PID 2184, and early PID 8592 ETHREAD IRP lists contained no valid entry.
- Two steady System threads with raw EAC-range stack candidates had one list target each, but both targets failed `_IRP.Type == 6` / size validation. They are retained as inconsistent/torn candidates and are not counted as IRPs.

**[UNKNOWN]** completed EAC IOCTLs, requests between physical sweeps, and historical IRP stack locations are not recoverable from empty/current lists.

## FLTMGR/minifilter evidence

**[OBSERVED]** FLTMGR attached-device objects are present above both NPFS and NTFS in the two valid PID-8592 IRPs. **[OBSERVED]** the EAC `DRIVER_OBJECT.DriverUnload` pointer resolves into FLTMGR. **[INFERRED]** together these are consistent with normal Filter Manager participation and EAC filter registration. **[CORRELATED STATIC EVIDENCE]** the separately recovered exact `FLT_REGISTRATION` at EAC RVA `0x2ea4a0` registers CREATE, SET_INFORMATION, and ACQUIRE_FOR_SECTION_SYNCHRONIZATION pre-operations.

**[UNKNOWN]** the private `_FLT_FILTER`, `_FLT_INSTANCE`, altitude, operation-registration table, and callback-data history were not enumerated by this resolver. The presence of a FLTMGR device in an IRP stack does not identify which registered minifilter, if any, processed that request.

## Exact image correlation and mutation boundary

The shared-section raw image is the same exact file used for raw-to-loaded normalization. `analysis/second_capture/raw-loaded-comparison.json` compares five loaded images after applying 6,015 relocation entries:

- `.text`, both unnamed executable sections, `.rsrc`, `.reloc`, `.pdata`, and the other immutable section match the raw mapped bytes across all five images.
- `.text` normalized SHA-256 is `fab345a8f621e95647a41485c7abe7820a06636fba9e58af86c417a6503609f9` in every image.
- `analysis/second_capture/live-phase-diff.json` compares four complete loaded-memory phases and finds 14 changed pages, all writable `.data`; executable changed pages: 0.

**[DERIVED]** this is strong integrity evidence for the observed loaded executable ranges and exact raw identity. **[BOUNDARY]** it does not prove that no transient patch existed between captures, nor does it validate pages outside the declared PE image.

## Defensible findings

1. **High confidence -- staged raw driver transfer.** Exact same section body, two mapped views, creator PID 9396, full raw file including signature, matching independent hashes, and named synchronization objects jointly establish a raw driver staging/transfer channel between EAC and PID 2184.
2. **High confidence -- two client-side device connections.** PID 2184 and protected PID 8592 own distinct file objects to the same EAC device/driver, both stable across phases. PID 8592's handle exists before its main handle table and thread population expands.
3. **High confidence -- observable lifecycle boundary.** Module/driver events are already signaled; `EventGame` changes from unsignaled with an EAC waiter to signaled without one; PID 8592 concurrently grows from skeletal to full process state.
4. **High confidence -- executable integrity across sampled phases.** All normalized executable sections match exact raw bytes; only writable `.data` pages vary.
5. **High confidence -- stored AFD endpoint state and ownership.** The early service endpoint contains an exact stable `AF_INET`/443 remote structure, while corrected role labels separate the full-path PID-2184 EAC wrapper from the truncated-name protected PID 8592. The peer value is retained only in restricted evidence.
6. **Moderate confidence -- early networking/bootstrap completion.** EAC's AFD endpoint and two Winsock catalog KCB handles disappear by steady state. This is a lifecycle inference, not traffic reconstruction.
7. **Moderate confidence -- Filter Manager registration.** EAC's unload pointer is in FLTMGR and FLTMGR attached devices occur in recoverable file IRPs; private filter identity/altitude remains unenumerated.
8. **Reliability boundary -- no historical operation census.** Handle rights, callbacks, empty queues, and open KCBs cannot yield every past query or response. The report therefore separates interfaces that were reachable from operations that were actually retained in-flight.

## Coverage and unresolved blockers

| Requested surface | Result | Confidence / boundary |
|---|---|---|
| EAC/PID-2184/PID-8592 handle tables | all three phases/tables incorporated | observed; PID 8592 address space still inaccessible |
| named section/events | exact body/access/state/waiter graph | observed, phase-invariant except EventGame signal state |
| exact driver device handles/access | both PID 2184 `0x6d8` and PID 8592 `0x8` | observed and phase-invariant |
| section/control-area backing | full SECTION/CA/SEGMENT/VAD graph | observed plus pagefile-backed classification derived |
| section content | both committed views and complete embedded raw PE | exact bytes and hashes |
| registry KCB full names | 100 early and 195 steady key handles | all resolved; operations/values unknown |
| ACPI/TPM/CNG/KsecDD/network surfaces | file objects, access, device stacks | observed; completed calls/results unknown |
| EAC-relevant IRPs | two valid protected-process file IRPs; none to EAC device | exact retained state; history absent |
| thread starts/stacks | all target ETHREADs; System starts and raw range candidates | starts exact; stack candidates not unwound |
| file/minifilter objects | FILE_OBJECT/SectionObjectPointers/device chains; FLTMGR attachments | private FLT globals not enumerated |
| PID 8592 memory modules/mappings | no memflow ranges/modules | blocked by protected/skeletal DTB |
| network peers/payloads | exact early service `AF_INET`/443 stored remote structure; wrapper/protected endpoint ownership corrected | peer bytes restricted; connect/send completion, payloads, DNS/TLS, and initiating call unknown; global lists torn |
| completed IOCTL/ACPI/CNG results | not retained without an in-flight IRP | unknown |

The machine-readable status ledger is `ledgers/coverage.json`; corrected AFD process-role supersedence, handle-rooted counts, admitted records, and torn-list boundaries are in `ledgers/afd-endpoint-supersedence-v5.json`.

## Artifacts and reproduction

Primary artifacts:

- `ledgers/phase-timeline.json`: ordered acquisition metadata and non-atomicity boundary
- `ledgers/object-linkage.json`: named handles, exact cross-process bodies, event states/waiters, invariance
- `ledgers/protected-game.json`: PID-8592 object-table lifecycle and invariant EAC device handle
- `ledgers/sections.json`: SECTION/CONTROL_AREA/SEGMENT/VAD graph, PE metadata, hashes
- `ledgers/registry-keys.json`: every resolved KCB path and access mask
- `ledgers/file-surfaces.json`: every resolved File handle, SectionObjectPointers, and attached device stack
- `ledgers/threads-irps.json`: target/System thread evidence, valid and invalid IRP-list rows, derived operation names
- `ledgers/symbol-offsets.json`: build-matched field offsets and complete public-PDB age provenance
- `ledgers/io-stack-layout-pdb.json`: live CodeView identity, public-PDB type records, aligned union offsets, and validation predicates
- `ledgers/artifact-hashes.json`: SHA-256 ledger of source and derived artifacts
- `section-view-eac.bin` and `section-view-game.bin`: exact committed shared-section views

Read-only resolver invocation shape:

```text
offline_interface_resolver <guest-physical.raw> \
  <handles-eac.json> <handles-game.json> <handles-protected-game.json> \
  <output.json>
```

The resolver is `<private-analysis>/memflow-process-dump/src/bin/offline_interface_resolver.rs`; compact ledgers are regenerated by `analysis/physical-early-interface/derive_interface_ledgers.py` with the local restricted source-page map supplied through `--restricted-source-map`. Public section ledgers retain stable source-page ordinals and content-group predicates while withholding raw page digests. All acquisition inputs are ordinary sealed files, and the physical images are opened read-only by memflow's file connector.

`appendices/kernel-callbacks.md`:

```md
# EAC matched-kernel callback inventory

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Scope and executable identity

Two independently sealed, host-only, read-only memflow acquisitions enumerated the live callback registrations. In each acquisition, the collector committed every declared executable section immediately before and after enumeration using paired 64 KiB reads. Every pre/post executable commitment is complete and equal within its acquisition, and the aggregate and all three section hashes are equal across both acquisitions.

Executable aggregate SHA-256: `99802a5bf982dc329e6ff57685360f5743d06c29dcacea0812ac795d6963dad1`.

| Executable section | RVA | Virtual bytes | SHA-256 |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| `.text` | `0x1000` | `2458916` | `fab345a8f621e95647a41485c7abe7820a06636fba9e58af86c417a6503609f9` |
| `unnamed` | `0x35a000` | `31232` | `576fa921da82da4c84ef234daaf560b31a2f5877dd895a205cd2d1fbf9869a21` |
| `unnamed` | `0x362000` | `41369600` | `2cbc7606491bb9fbd08dff5b5d1a58b4eee9393f1640af41ae4670c38917abe4` |

This is executable-section identity, not a full loaded-image equality claim. Each acquisition had one unavailable or unstable pre-enumeration 64 KiB full-image chunk in mutable/non-executable image state; the post full-image read completed, with a different post hash between acquisitions. The collector therefore records the full-image bracket as unmatched and does not promote either post hash to a whole-image identity claim.

The live kernel PE/CodeView record exactly matched the `ntkrnlmp.pdb` GUID and age used for all kernel-symbol RVAs. Ps slot values and registration blocks, OB payloads, Cm payloads, and bugcheck records were all accepted only after immediate paired reads matched. Sequential paired reads are not an atomic guest snapshot.

## Exact EAC registrations reproduced across both acquisitions

| Family | Exact EAC fields |
|---|---|
| Process notify array | slot `9`, callback RVA `0x6787c`, context scalar `2` |
| Thread notify array | slot `2`, callback RVA `0x67f68`, null context |
| Image notify array | slot `1`, callback RVA `0x684c6`, null context |
| Process OB list | active, operations `3` (create and duplicate), altitude `327530`, pre RVA `0xea9b9`, post RVA `0xebd81` |
| Thread OB list | active, operations `3` (create and duplicate), altitude `327530`, pre RVA `0xea9b9`, post RVA `0xebd81` |
| Registry callback list | callback RVA `0x225088`, altitude `327530`, null context; current-build primary list entry at node `+0x00` |
| Bugcheck-reason list | callback RVA `0x58b45`, raw reason `1`, state `1`, empty component at EAC RVA `0x2f3fb2` |
| Bugcheck-reason list | callback RVA `0xa1120`, raw reason `2`, state `1`, empty component at EAC RVA `0x2f3fb2` |

After excluding only the captured bugcheck checksum DWORD, the complete EAC registration projection is byte-for-byte identical across the two acquisitions. The checksum DWORD changed on both bugcheck records:

| Callback RVA | Reason | Acquisition 1 | Acquisition 2 |
|---|---:|---:|---:|
| `0x58b45` | `1` | `1992346360` | `1990118136` |
| `0xa1120` | `2` | `1992642772` | `1990414548` |

Those DWORD values and changes are exact observations. Their formula and semantic meaning remain unknown; no causal interpretation is asserted. The raw reason values map to `KbCallbackReserved1` (`1`) and `KbCallbackSecondaryDumpData` (`2`). List membership does not prove either callback ran.

## Enumerated families in acquisition 1

| Family | Stable | Structural closure | Total observed | EAC observed |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| `Ps.ProcessNotifyRoutine` | `true` | `opaque` | `10` | `1` |
| `Ps.ThreadNotifyRoutine` | `true` | `opaque` | `3` | `1` |
| `Ps.LoadImageNotifyRoutine` | `true` | `opaque` | `2` | `1` |
| `Ob.PsProcessType.CallbackList` | `true` | `true` | `2` | `1` |
| `Ob.PsThreadType.CallbackList` | `true` | `true` | `2` | `1` |
| `Cm.CallbackListHead` | `true` | `true` | `4` | `1` |
| `Ke.BugCheckReasonCallbackListHead` | `true` | `true` | `166` | `2` |
| `Ke.BugCheckCallbackListHead` | `true` | `true` | `1` | `0` |
| `Ke.BugCheckAddRemovePagesCallbackListHead` | `true` | `true` | `2` | `0` |
| `Io.NotifyShutdownQueueHead` | `true` | `true` | `8` | `0` |
| `Io.NotifyLastChanceShutdownQueueHead` | `true` | `true` | `2` | `0` |
| `Iommu.InterfaceStateChangeCallbackListHead` | `true` | `true` | `0` | `0` |
| `Pop.ShutdownNotificationCallbackList` | `true` | `opaque` | `opaque` | `0` |
| `Emp.CallbackListHead` | `true` | `opaque` | `opaque` | `0` |
| `Se.FileSystemNotifyRoutinesHead` | `true` | `opaque` | `opaque` | `0` |
| `Se.FileSystemNotifyRoutinesExHead` | `true` | `opaque` | `opaque` | `0` |
| `Ki.NmiCallbackListHead` | `true` | `opaque` | `opaque` | `0` |

Current-build `CmRegisterCallbackEx` disassembly establishes that `CallbackListHead` links the allocation base at node `+0x00`; Context, Callback, altitude descriptor/buffer, and the separate secondary list are at `+0x20/+0x28/+0x30/+0x38/+0x40`. Under that exact layout, all current records have coherent module callbacks and readable altitudes, and EAC is present at callback RVA `0x225088`, altitude `327530`, null context. Payload fields were independently paired and stable; topology closure alone would not validate them.

The decoded standard bugcheck, add/remove-pages, shutdown, last-chance shutdown, and IOMMU lists had no EAC-attributed payload in the sampled acquisitions under their stated layouts. Opaque Pop/Emp/Se/NMI heads are structural observations only and cannot support record-absence claims.

## Provenance

- Acquisition 1 input manifest SHA-256: `fad0ada0f7451ec104b6b872233a1fbe0fb5fea20914e6b963d83df855b8282a`.
- Acquisition 2 input manifest SHA-256: `1ad54af6945447792d1916a2389c7363b96cff51908e0c830f164fa73ec38ab3`.

Only module-relative callback identities, layout fields, counts, hashes, and structural booleans are persisted: no absolute kernel address, payload bytes, guest instrumentation, IOCTL, or guest mutation. Snapshot absence is bounded to these acquisitions, and membership is bounded to registration—not invocation, predicates, queried values, mutations, or side effects inside the callback bodies.

`appendices/kernel-runtime.md`:

```md
# EAC kernel runtime and integration report

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

> **Historical broad-pass warning.** This report preserves the first complete kernel survey, including hypotheses later corrected. For current semantics, the focused process/thread/image, object-access, device-namespace, name-policy, and callback-family appendices take precedence.

Capture authority: `<private-capture-01>`

This report is a read-only reduction of the sealed, host-side memflow capture. It covers linked-module lifecycle, the recovered resident image, Windows kernel objects, dispatch pointers, the three surveyed Ps callback arrays, System-process thread starts, page-table permissions, and outside-module executable mappings. It does not convert sampled states into an execution trace.

## Confidence language

- **Observed**: directly present in a sealed file or derived arithmetically from directly captured bytes/addresses.
- **Strongly inferred**: one explanation is well supported by multiple observed facts, but the decisive structure or event was not captured.
- **Tentative**: correlation exists, but attribution is not established.
- **Unknown**: the collector did not acquire the surface required to decide the claim.

Every conclusion below uses one of those labels. An absence means only “not present in the enumerated structure at that sample,” never “could not have existed between samples.”

## Executive findings

1. **Observed:** a single valid module, `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys`, appeared at `2026-09-13T23:23:04.649Z`, linked at `KVA-0001` with size `0x2ae7000` (44,986,368 bytes). It was present in the live survey and absent from the final linked-module survey.
2. **Observed:** the live survey recovered all 44,986,368 resident bytes with SHA-256 `cfc623de01a9c436c239c56a016d936a9070cdd2432596c06f75a3d88ae68081`. This is a loaded-memory layout, not an authenticated copy of the disk file.
3. **Observed:** the live kernel state added exactly one named EAC driver object, one named EAC device object, and three EAC callbacks—process, thread, and image. All three object/callback sets returned byte-for-byte to their detected-survey state in the final survey.
4. **Observed:** only `IRP_MJ_CREATE`, `IRP_MJ_CLOSE`, and `IRP_MJ_DEVICE_CONTROL` point into EAC. The other 25 slots share the kernel invalid-request routine, which completes the IRP with `STATUS_INVALID_DEVICE_REQUEST` (`0xc0000010`).
5. **Observed:** no surveyed System-process thread began directly inside EAC, and no captured EAC driver, callback, or thread-start pointer fell outside a linked module. This is useful negative evidence over the surveyed fields, not proof that no worker, DPC, timer, stack frame, or transient code existed.
6. **Observed:** 43,864,064 of 44,986,368 image bytes (97.505235%) were simultaneously executable and writeable in the live page-table sample. This is a consequential hardening condition; it is not, by itself, evidence of exploitation.
7. **Tentative:** a live-only executable/writeable 4 KiB mapping at `KVA-0002`, exactly `0x2000` after EAC’s image end, contains executable register/return thunks. Proximity is exact, but no captured pointer or literal reference attributes the page to EAC.
8. **Observed + strongly inferred provenance:** the `kernel-survey-final` directory is composite. An EAC image file written at `23:28:16.960Z` remains in its `kernel-images` subdirectory, while the later final JSON written from `23:28:23.904Z` onward says EAC was absent and does not list that image. The survey implementation does not clear an existing output directory. Therefore the orphan image is earlier-pass evidence, not proof EAC remained linked in the final snapshot. The invocation path for the earlier pass is unknown from the sealed capture.

## Capture integrity and acquisition boundary

### Integrity

- **Observed:** the capture was armed at `2026-09-13T23:22:27Z`, stopped at `2026-09-13T23:28:32Z`, and contains 46,405 SHA-256 manifest entries.
- **Observed:** `sha256sum -c --quiet SHA256SUMS` completed successfully for the full capture during this analysis.
- **Observed:** the acquisition mode in `session.json` (`private evidence: <private-capture-01>/session.json`) is “host-only memflow KVM to Win32”; guest commands and guest agents are false.
- **Observed:** `kernel-module-watch/errors.jsonl` (`private evidence: <private-capture-01>/kernel-module-watch/errors.jsonl`) is empty.

### What the watchdog actually measured

- Module-link sampling interval: **25 ms**.
- Baseline module records: **162**, of which 161 were valid named/nonzero records and one was a nameless size-zero parser record.
- Module event records: **114**: 58 `appeared`, 56 `disappeared`.
- **Observed:** 113/114 events concern the changing nameless size-zero record. They are enumeration noise, not 113 real driver loads/unloads.
- **Observed:** the sole valid named event is the EAC appearance. No EAC disappearance event exists.
- **Unknown:** exact unload time. It is bracketed by the earlier EAC image at `23:28:16.960Z` and the EAC-absent final module list at `23:28:23.904Z`, but those are not a watcher disappearance event.

The earlier `eac-detected-utc.txt` (`private evidence: <private-capture-01>/eac-detected-utc.txt`) marker contains `2026-09-13T23:23:03Z`. It is not the driver-load time: the detected module list written at `23:23:04.265Z` still lacks EAC, and the module watcher first observes EAC at `23:23:04.649Z`. The marker reflects the broader selector trigger, not a linked-kernel-module observation.

## Snapshot distinction and exact lifecycle

| UTC | Evidence | EAC state | Confidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| 23:22:27.378 | `kernel-module-watch/session.json` | watchdog started | Observed |
| 23:23:03 | `eac-detected-utc.txt` | broader selector detected; not yet proof of driver load | Observed marker; interpretation strongly inferred |
| 23:23:04.265 | detected `kernel-modules.json` | absent from 161 valid linked modules | Observed |
| 23:23:04.649 | module event sequence 1485, elapsed 37,266 ms | appeared at `KVA-0001`, size `0x2ae7000` | Observed |
| 23:23:59.942 | live `kernel-modules.json` | present | Observed |
| 23:24:09.876 | live loaded image mtime | complete resident image acquired | Observed |
| 23:28:16.960 | orphan image under `kernel-survey-final/kernel-images` | EAC bytes readable in an earlier pass | Observed bytes; pass provenance strongly inferred |
| 23:28:23.904 | final `kernel-modules.json` | absent | Observed |
| 23:28:32 | `stopped-utc.txt` | capture sealed | Observed |

Phase counts:

| Surface | Detected | Live | Final | EAC delta |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| module records / valid records | 162 / 161 | 163 / 162 | 162 / 161 | absent → present → absent |
| driver objects | 116 | 117 | 116 | +1 then removed |
| device objects | 168 | 169 | 168 | +1 then removed |
| surveyed callbacks | 13 | 16 | 13 | +3 then removed |
| System-process threads | 190 | 207 | 190 | churn; no direct EAC start |
| unlinked driver objects by collector rule | 0 | 0 | 0 | none enumerated |
| drivers with pointers outside linked modules | 0 | 0 | 0 | none enumerated |
| callbacks outside linked modules | 0 | 0 | 0 | none enumerated |

The valid detected and final module tuples `(name, path, base, size)` are identical. The raw module JSON is not byte-identical because the invalid nameless record’s low base changed. In contrast, these detected/final artifacts are byte-identical:

- `driver-objects.json`: `f0108eb7cb21ad780f4fbb7a355d1c7790a1e1d8576c326cf30ad69e4bebb7e6`
- `device-objects.json`: `df7e44efada9c561297b8938f47a5a4ca2cf134eea0fc2cb6461279419e3c528`
- `kernel-callbacks.json`: `f92a1268452f6f28c4bb9aae34c046e82be8eaed2a63cb295ae03c496372b033`

That is strong sampled-state evidence of object and surveyed callback cleanup after unload.

### Why the final directory must not be read as one instantaneous snapshot

The orphan file `KVA-0003-EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys-2ae7000.loaded.bin` (`private evidence: <private-capture-01>/kernel-survey-final/kernel-images/KVA-0003-EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys-2ae7000.loaded.bin`) was written 6.944 seconds before the final module list. The later `kernel-image-analysis.json` (`private evidence: <private-capture-01>/kernel-survey-final/kernel-image-analysis.json`) does not list EAC. The implementation writes `kernel-modules.json` near the start of a survey and image files near the end, so that ordering cannot come from the same survey pass. It also calls `create_dir_all` without clearing old files. At least one earlier pass therefore wrote the orphan file before the later post-unload pass overwrote the JSON. The sealed capture does not preserve the earlier invocation record, so its cause is **unknown**.

- Name: `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys`
- Kernel path: `\??\C:\Program Files (x86)\EasyAntiCheat_EOS\EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys`
- module-list entry address / `DriverSection`: `KVA-0004`
- load base: `KVA-0001`
- size and image end: `0x2ae7000` bytes, end-exclusive `KVA-0005`
- architecture from memflow: `X86(64, false)`

- bytes requested/read: 44,986,368 / 44,986,368
- unreadable pages: 0

- preferred image base: `KVA-0001`, equal to captured load base

- DLL characteristics: `0x01e0` (high-entropy VA, dynamic base, force integrity, NX-compatible bits)

Raw page digests and content-addressed blob paths remain in the restricted source mapping. The partial result proves the header and 57 other sampled pages were already resident; it is not a complete load-time version.

### Earlier-pass image under the final directory

The changed data are consistent with live state mutation or non-atomic acquisition; their semantic owners are **unknown**.

## DRIVER_OBJECT and unload integration

Observed live object fields from `driver-objects.json` (`private evidence: <private-capture-01>/kernel-survey-live/driver-objects.json`):

| Field | Value | Attribution |
|---|---|---|
| object | `KVA-0007` | EAC driver object |
| directory name | `EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys` | `\Driver` directory entry |
| `DriverName` | `\Driver\EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys` | EAC |
| service key | `EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys` | EAC |
| `DriverStart` | `KVA-0001` | EAC base |
| `DriverSize` | `44,986,368` | EAC image size |
| `DriverSection` | `KVA-0004` | exact EAC module-list entry |
| `DriverInit` | `KVA-0006` | EAC + `0x2594f8` |
| `DriverUnload` | `KVA-0008` | `FLTMGR.SYS` + `0x510c0` |
| `DeviceObject` | `KVA-0009` | named EAC device |
| pointers outside linked modules | empty | none in parsed fields |

`DriverUnload` does not point into EAC. It points to a nontrivial FLTMGR routine. The captured prologue saves nonvolatile registers, allocates `0xd0` stack bytes, initializes two 0x48-byte buffers, and continues through FLTMGR globals and ntoskrnl calls. **Strong inference:** Filter Manager owns the unload path, which is consistent with a registered minifilter. This pointer alone does not prove a specific minifilter name, altitude, volume instance, or callback-operation table; those registrations were not enumerated.

## Initialization and protected transition points

All addresses below are exact correlations to the captured live image:

| Role | EAC RVA | VA | First transition |
|---|---:|---:|---|
| `DriverEntry` wrapper | `0x2594f8` | `KVA-0006` | calls `0x2594c8`, then `0xe4cfc` |
| protected initializer | `0xe4cfc` | `KVA-0010` | `E9` to RVA `0x39624d` / VA `KVA-0011` |
| process notify | `0x6787c` | `KVA-0012` | `E9` to RVA `0x3cd7f0` |
| thread notify | `0x67f68` | `KVA-0013` | `E9` to RVA `0x3a372b` |
| image notify | `0x684c6` | `KVA-0014` | `E9` to RVA `0x3bfa3c` |
| create/close dispatch | `0xcca29` | `KVA-0015` | `E9` to RVA `0x420bef` |
| device-control dispatch | `0xccaaf` | `KVA-0016` | `E9` to RVA `0x39e76d` |

Observed `DriverEntry` behavior is narrow and conventional: it preserves the two incoming arguments, calls the RVA `0x2594c8` security-cookie helper, then passes the saved arguments to RVA `0xe4cfc`. The cookie helper reads the global at RVA `0x2fd188`, compares it to zero and `0x2b992ddfa232`, fail-fasts with interrupt `0x29` subcode 6 for an invalid/default cookie, and writes the complemented cookie to RVA `0x2fd190` otherwise. The protected initializer immediately jumps into the large protected executable section. Semantics beyond that transition are **unknown from runtime sampling** and belong to static reconstruction.

| Field | Value | Meaning |
|---|---:|---|
| object | `KVA-0009` | EAC `DEVICE_OBJECT` |
| `\Device` directory name | `EasyAntiCheat_EOS` | native device name |
| driver object | `KVA-0007` | exact EAC `DRIVER_OBJECT` |
| `DeviceType` | `34` / `0x22` | `FILE_DEVICE_UNKNOWN` |
| flags | `64` / `0x40` | `DO_DEVICE_HAS_NAME` |
| characteristics | `256` / `0x100` | `FILE_DEVICE_SECURE_OPEN` |
| `AttachedDevice` | `0x0` | no device attached above this object |

The collector did not parse `DeviceObjectExtension->AttachedTo`, `NextDevice`, the driver-defined device extension, object security descriptor/DACL, `GLOBAL??` symbolic links, interface GUIDs, or minifilter volume instances. Therefore:

- **Observed:** no device is attached above the captured named object.
- **Unknown:** whether it attaches to a lower object through the extension.
- **Unknown:** any DOS/Win32 symbolic link, device interface, or caller ACL.
- **Unknown:** whether a non-admin process can open it. `FILE_DEVICE_SECURE_OPEN` is not a substitute for inspecting the object DACL.

| 18 | `IRP_MJ_CLEANUP` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 19 | `IRP_MJ_CREATE_MAILSLOT` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 20 | `IRP_MJ_QUERY_SECURITY` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 21 | `IRP_MJ_SET_SECURITY` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 22 | `IRP_MJ_POWER` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 23 | `IRP_MJ_SYSTEM_CONTROL` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 24 | `IRP_MJ_DEVICE_CHANGE` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 25 | `IRP_MJ_QUERY_QUOTA` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 26 | `IRP_MJ_SET_QUOTA` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |
| 27 | `IRP_MJ_PNP` | `KVA-0017` | ntoskrnl + `0x3ab220` | invalid request |

The shared ntoskrnl routine has the exact core sequence:

```text
mov dword ptr [rdx+0x30], 0xc0000010
xor edx, edx
call KVA-0018
mov eax, 0xc0000010
ret
```

This writes `IoStatus.Status`, completes the IRP through the nearby kernel routine, and returns the same status. **Observed:** the named device exposes a create/close path and a device-control path, not read/write IRPs. **Unknown:** IOCTL codes, transfer methods, buffer validation, authorization rules, and side effects; no IRP arguments or calls were traced. `IRP_MJ_CLEANUP` using the invalid-request default despite create/close support is an unusual reliability point worth exercising with benign open/duplicate/close lifecycle tests, but no failure was observed in this capture.

## Registered process, thread, and image callbacks

Exactly three EAC callback blocks were added in the live survey:

| Array / slot | Fast ref / block | Callback VA | EAC RVA | Context | First protected jump |
|---|---|---:|---:|---:|---|
| `PspCreateProcessNotifyRoutine[10]` | `KVA-0019` / `KVA-0020` | `KVA-0012` | `0x6787c` | `0x2` | RVA `0x3cd7f0`, VA `KVA-0021` |
| `PspCreateThreadNotifyRoutine[2]` | `KVA-0022` / `KVA-0023` | `KVA-0013` | `0x67f68` | `0x0` | RVA `0x3a372b`, VA `KVA-0024` |
| `PspLoadImageNotifyRoutine[1]` | `KVA-0025` / `KVA-0026` | `KVA-0014` | `0x684c6` | `0x0` | RVA `0x3bfa3c`, VA `KVA-0027` |

Evidence and first bytes: `callbacks.csv` (`private evidence: analysis/kernel/callbacks.csv`).

- **Observed:** each captured function pointer lies inside the exact live EAC image.
- **Observed:** all three begin with direct `E9` transfers into the protected executable section.
- **Observed:** detected and final callback arrays are byte-identical, so all three EAC blocks were absent after unload.
- **Unknown:** semantic behavior inside the protected targets. The capture records registrations, not callback invocations or arguments.
- **Unknown:** Object Manager (`ObRegisterCallbacks`), Registry (`CmRegisterCallbackEx`), Filter Manager operation callbacks, WFP callouts, NDIS filters, ETW providers, power/PnP notifications, and other registration families. The collector only enumerated these three Ps arrays.

## System-process threads

Observed counts and deltas:

- detected → live: 17 thread objects added, 0 removed
- live → final: 2 added, 19 removed
- detected → final: 3 added, 3 removed
- no phase contains a `StartAddress` or `Win32StartAddress` inside EAC
- no phase contains a surveyed start address outside a linked module
- all 17 live-added starts resolve into `ntoskrnl.exe`

The exact ETHREADs, IDs, addresses, module offsets, and transition sets are in `system-thread-diff.csv` (`private evidence: analysis/kernel/system-thread-diff.csv`).

This supports only a narrow statement: **no dedicated System thread with a directly EAC-owned start address was observed**. It does not rule out ntoskrnl worker-thread stubs, queued work items, transient threads between surveys, thread stacks executing EAC, APCs, DPCs, timers, or non-System process threads because those were not captured.

## Page-table permission state

The live page map covers the EAC image exactly in five ranges:

| VA | EAC RVA | Size | Captured page type | Executable |
|---:|---:|---:|---|---|
| `KVA-0001` | `0x0` | `0x1000` | `WRITEABLE | NOEXEC` | no |
| `KVA-0028` | `0x1000` | `0x259000` | `WRITEABLE` | yes |
| `KVA-0029` | `0x25a000` | `0x100000` | `WRITEABLE | NOEXEC` | no |
| `KVA-0030` | `0x35a000` | `0x277c000` | `WRITEABLE` | yes |
| `KVA-0031` | `0x2ad6000` | `0x11000` | `WRITEABLE | NOEXEC` | no |

Totals:

- executable + writeable: 43,864,064 bytes
- writeable + non-executable: 1,122,304 bytes
- executable fraction: 97.505235%

This is **observed PTE state at the live sample**. It materially broadens the consequences of any kernel memory-corruption or arbitrary-write defect because executable EAC code pages were also writeable. It is not proof such a primitive exists, not proof code was modified, and not evidence of an active attack. The exact live/prior complete-image diff found only non-executable data mutations.

## Linked, unlinked, and outside-module execution evidence

### Linked/unlinked object state

- **Observed:** EAC is conventionally linked in the live module list and its `DriverSection` points to the same loader entry.
- **Observed:** `\Driver\EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys` and `\Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS` are enumerated through the normal object directories live.
- **Observed:** collector-defined unlinked driver count is 0 in detected, live, and final.
- **Observed:** no parsed EAC driver pointer, surveyed callback, or System-thread start falls outside linked modules.
- **Unknown:** whether any allocation, callback family, work item, or executable mapping not reachable through those enumerators belongs to EAC.

### Outside-module executable scan

| Phase | Records | Virtual bytes | Bytes read | Unreadable pages |
|---|---:|---:|---:|---:|
| detected | 227 | 74,219,520 | 7,045,120 | 16,400 |
| live | 231 | 74,285,056 | 6,623,232 | 16,519 |
| final | 228 | 74,223,616 | 6,561,792 | 16,519 |

Two small MZ-prefixed mappings exist identically in all three phases and are therefore not EAC-load-specific:

- `KVA-0032`, 8 KiB, SHA-256 `a5cb2048bd3009054464433aafbe40d18f8b5a3803ec0a1b2b6821878736cce4`
- `KVA-0033`, 12 KiB, SHA-256 `63b2a03772ed70c3e0aff4aa3805d2752d406ba413efc328375ca605b959d223`

No new MZ record appears only in the live phase. Raw `vmx`, `svm`, or `ept` substring hits in these broad memory dumps are not function or ownership attribution.

Twelve `(base,size,hash)` outside-executable records are live-only relative to both detected and final. Some reflect map coalescing/splitting or ordinary allocation churn; four 32 KiB records are all-zero. No captured ownership metadata ties them to EAC.

The one proximity candidate is:

- VA `KVA-0002`, 4 KiB, `WRITEABLE` and executable
- SHA-256 `11c775a3621cab991104dcbb753e4b39c1f9ab4dc4a2ce08163a09863f4432cc`
- gap from EAC image end: `0x2000`
- contents: a short integer-offset header followed by aligned executable thunks, including `mov r10, KVA-0034; call ...` and register-to-return-slot stubs
- no literal 64-bit reference to the page base occurs in the recovered EAC image
- no surveyed callback, driver dispatch field, unload/init pointer, or thread start targets the page

Classification: **tentative EAC association, ownership unknown**. Proximity and temporal lifetime justify preserving it; they do not justify calling it an EAC allocation. Relative branches, dynamically stored pointers, and uncollected ownership structures remain possible.

The large unreadable-page count is an explicit coverage limit: the outside-executable scan cannot prove the absence of executable code in unreadable portions.

## Defensive security and reliability findings

### KR-01 — predominantly executable/writeable image mapping

- Priority: high hardening review
- Evidence: 97.505235% of the exact EAC image is W+X in the live PTE sample.
- Impact: an independent write primitive could alter executable code without a permission transition; W+X also complicates integrity monitoring.
- Claim limit: no write primitive, malicious modification, or exploitation was observed.
- Defensive validation: repeat page-table acquisition across clean boots and supported Windows builds; compare with expected protector behavior and any platform code-integrity policy.

### KR-02 — active device-control surface with unknown authorization and contract

- Priority: high audit gap
- Evidence: named `FILE_DEVICE_UNKNOWN` object, create/close dispatcher, and protected `IRP_MJ_DEVICE_CONTROL` dispatcher.
- Impact: IOCTL parsers are a privileged input boundary; caller authorization, methods, lengths, and integer/pointer validation determine exposure.
- Claim limit: the capture has no security descriptor, symbolic link, IOCTL codes, requests, or handler reconstruction. No vulnerability is claimed.
- Defensive validation: acquire the object DACL and `GLOBAL??` links through memflow, reconstruct the dispatcher statically, and test only benign malformed-length/error paths in a rollback-capable VM.

### KR-03 — live-only executable page adjacent to image

- Priority: medium attribution gap
- Evidence: exact live-only 4 KiB W+X thunk page `0x2000` beyond image end.
- Impact: an owned code allocation outside the module list would need separate integrity and lifetime accounting.
- Claim limit: EAC ownership is unproven; no captured pointer targets it.
- Defensive validation: correlate pool headers/allocation tags and page-table/PFN ownership from a future read-only capture; sample the page continuously through load/unload.

### KR-04 — cleanup slot defaults to invalid request

- Priority: low reliability review
- Evidence: create and close share an EAC dispatcher, but `IRP_MJ_CLEANUP` uses ntoskrnl’s invalid-request routine.
- Impact: unusual handle lifecycle behavior can expose cleanup/resource-retention edge cases.
- Claim limit: no leak, hang, or incorrect cleanup was observed.
- Defensive validation: inspect the create/close protected target and collect object/reference counts across benign open, duplicate, process-exit, and close sequences.

### KR-05 — composite final-output directory

- Priority: medium evidence-reliability issue
- Evidence: orphan EAC image predates later EAC-absent final JSON; collector output directories are not cleared.
- Impact: analysts can falsely claim a module persisted into the final snapshot by reading directory contents without cross-checking `kernel-image-analysis.json` and mtimes.
- Claim limit: the prior pass’s invocation source is unknown.
- Defensive remediation: use a unique immutable subdirectory per survey pass or clear/stage-and-rename the destination, then embed a survey ID and start/end timestamps in every JSON record.

### KR-06 — module-event noise and missing unload event

- Priority: low collector reliability
- Evidence: 113/114 events are nameless zero-size record churn; EAC appearance exists but disappearance does not.
- Impact: event counts are misleading and unload timing cannot be recovered precisely.
- Defensive remediation: suppress invalid module keys, record parser errors separately, and preserve a final watcher sample before shutdown.

### Positive cleanup evidence

The final snapshot contains no linked EAC module, named EAC driver/device object, or EAC process/thread/image callback. The detected/final object and callback JSON are byte-identical. This is good evidence that the enumerated state was removed cleanly. It is not a blanket statement about freed memory, unenumerated callbacks, residual allocations, or transient execution.

The phrase “capture everything that happens” exceeds what a 25 ms module watcher plus three point-in-time surveys can establish. This acquisition exactly preserves selected resident states and bytes; it does not preserve every call, query, IRP, callback invocation, stack, allocation, filesystem operation, registry operation, or network operation between polls.

`appendices/kraken.md`:

```md
# Kraken entropy semantic recovery

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Outcome

The exact captured clean cluster is closed for framed entropy-array dispatch and the explicit type-0/type-2/type-4 Huffman lane. RVA `0x250100` is an explicit-type framed dispatcher structurally corresponding to `Kraken_DecodeBytes`; RVA `0x2542c0` is the Type12 old/new Huffman decoder. The package recovers the exact ABI, return contracts, scratch layout, 64-bit bitreader state, both header formats, canonical LUT layout, 11-bit reversal, one- and two-partition streams, six CPU bulk variants, scalar seam validation, Rice/range helpers, and CRT helpers.

This is exact-byte and exact offline function validation, not a live-execution claim. The source oracle establishes structural semantics only; it does not establish producer or version provenance.

## Authority

- Loaded image: `<private-capture-01>/kernel-survey-live/kernel-images/KVA-0003-EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys-2ae7000.loaded.bin`
- Loaded image SHA-256: `cfc623de01a9c436c239c56a016d936a9070cdd2432596c06f75a3d88ae68081`
- Size/image base: `44986368` bytes / `KVA-0001`
- Function census SHA-256: `202603ddf03bbc52d3d25196d8106e844d3954af2b84db53ada1c2112eda61d9`
- Static direct-edge CSV SHA-256: `3ccb3fca6830634eea7a8208c5439e520f551287b0674546c70b61382ea5efcf`
- Source oracle: Oozle `kraken.cpp`, commit `38c45786dadd23fadea9308f4dc55804455db84b`, file SHA-256 `0b03d5a7403866478e60fba9674b8c25b3c1d886a29b2378ccf5a06a55cdf25d`

| `0x9de0` | `0xa357` | `dc575dc73a5fc9529628d7dd0c527010261027c2690df35b4cdfe1ac4a91a257` | Huff_Decode3Streams_dual_AMD_AVX_BMI_bulk |

`0x2592c0` is exactly CRT `memset`; the Type12 one-symbol lane calls it to fill output. `0x254a80` is exactly the scalar three-stream finisher and seam validator, including the optional second-partition reset. The low RVAs are not unrelated stubs: `0x8a80/0x8d20/0x9000` are baseline/BMI2/AMD-tuned single-partition bulk rounds and `0x9300/0x9860/0x9de0` are their dual-partition counterparts.

## ABI and framing

`0x250100` receives the decompressor type explicitly in `RCX`: 0 or 2 selects simple Type12, 4 selects dual Type12, and 1/3/5 select TANS/RLE/recursive. `RDX` is `u8 **output`, `R8` is source, `R9` is source end, and stack arguments are decoded-size pointer, capacity, scratch, and scratch end. Short framing selects on bit 7 and requires four total bytes for its exact dword load, while using three header bytes for 10+10-bit sizes; header bits 22..20 do not dispatch. Long framing is a five-byte 18+18-bit form and rejects `src_size >= dst_size`. It writes decoded size only after the child consumed exactly the framed source size.

`0x2542c0` uses `RCX=src`, `RDX=src_size`, `R8=output`, `R9=output_size`, with `dual_partition`, scratch, and scratch-end at stack `+0x28/+0x30/+0x38`. It returns compressed bytes consumed or `-1`. Old single-symbol input returns the positive exact header length and calls `memset`.

The scratch acceptance boundary is exact. Every path checks `end-start >= 0x1030` and `end-(align+0x1020) >= 0x500`; therefore the early one-symbol fill requires alignment padding plus `0x1520`. Multi-symbol decode additionally checks `end-(align+0x1520) >= 0x10a0`, requiring alignment padding plus `0x25c0`. Tests cover offsets 0, 1, 7, and 15 at one byte below and exactly at both boundaries.

## Bitreader and header semantics

The binary's primary reader is `{p,p_end,u64_msb_reservoir,signed_bitpos}` at offsets `0/8/0x10/0x18`. Effective bytes consumed are `p_offset - trunc((63-bitpos)/8)`. The old reader enters after selector `0` at bitpos 8; the new reader enters after selector `10` at bitpos 9. Reserved selector `11` is rejected.

Old sparse headers use an 8-bit count, a 3-bit code-length width bounded by four, and `{symbol,code_length-1}` entries; count one is the fill case. Old dense headers walk all 256 symbol positions using gamma-plus-one zero/present runs and an adaptive signed-zigzag length predictor starting at `avg_bits_x4=32`. New headers decode `count=(u8+1)`, truncated-binary `fluff`, unary Rice quotients plus 0..3 forced bits, then reconstruct lengths from `running_sum=0x1e`. This binary rejects new count one. Fluff parity encodes an initial symbol gap; pairs encode a run and following gap; strict byte-alphabet bounds apply.

Canonical starts are `[0,0,2,6,14,30,62,126,254,510,766,1022]`. `Huff_MakeLut` requires exactly 2048 entries and lays out length bytes at `+0`, symbol bytes at `+0x810`. RVA `0x254860` maps `target=reverse11(source)` and packs length in the low byte and symbol in the high byte.

## CPU dispatch

The feature word is at RVA `0x2ff290` and equals `0x1fff` in the capture. Type12 checks bit 9 first, then bit 7 through the sign of `AL`, then baseline. Bit 7 is derived from CPUID leaf-7 `EBX[8]` (BMI2). Bit 9 is restricted to the decoded AMD family condition and is finally retained only when `(mask & 0x5fe) == 0x5fe`. Exact instruction evidence and full mnemonic fingerprints are in the JSON ledger.

All six branches were executed on deterministic equal, variable, old-dense, and new-ranged fixtures. Every successful branch produced the same bytes and return and proceeded through `0x254a80`.

## Reproducible validation

Run:

```sh
python3 analysis/kraken-entropy-semantics/build_kraken_entropy_semantics.py
python3 analysis/kraken-entropy-semantics/validate_kraken_entropy_semantics.py
```

The validator requires the exact image/census/edge hashes, compares this report and ledger to a fresh deterministic build, and executes 687 fixtures. The local Python model and Unicorn harness never connect to the guest and invoke no driver/device interface.

## Closure boundary

Closed: every byte extent, listed constant, direct immediate call in the recovered map, all 22 census inbound calls to the dispatcher, and the complete type-0/type-2/type-4 semantic path through final seam validation.

Boundary-only: the explicit type-1 TANS, type-3 RLE, and type-5 recursive branches are named and edge-closed but not semantically expanded. Their descendants and the remainder of the larger decompression engine are outside this package. The mutable targets behind the two optional callback thunks cannot be recovered from static bytes.

Not established: live reachability during the game capture, actual live compressed inputs/outputs, exact Oodle product/version attribution, or producer provenance. No guest instrumentation or new acquisition was performed.

# Passive memflow EAC minifilter callback captures

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Collector and acceptance model

The collector used only host-side memflow KVM/Win32 virtual-memory reads. It performed no guest writes, instrumentation, hooks, injection, IOCTLs, or request/client generation.

Each sample applied these gates:

1. Refresh and fail closed on the exact EAC PE tuple and exact registration/veneer bytes.
2. Read each KPRCB `CurrentThread` twice and keep only paired-stable canonical ETHREAD values.
3. Read one exact-size ETHREAD snapshot, require valid CID/state and canonical bounded `StackLimit`, `StackBase`, and `KernelStack`.
4. Scan only that thread's bounded current-stack window for an exact EAC native-body or registered-veneer return-address anchor.
5. Preserve candidate qword position, owner thread, CPU set, anchor RVA, and distance only in memory; rank exact entry/native/veneer anchors and nearest same-thread candidates before caps.
6. Read CallbackData, IOPB, CallbackData again, and IOPB again. Require exact size/layout, exact owning ETHREAD, and byte-for-byte pair stability.
7. Require a registered operation compatible with the anchored EAC body.
8. Require `IOPB.TargetInstance` to be a member of the exact EAC instance list. On a compatible miss, immediately rewalk that exact list. The independent parent-filter check is diagnostic only and never substitutes for list membership.

Per-thread candidate cap was `512`; global per-sample cap was `4096`. The configured stack window was at most `0x6000` bytes and the same-stack anchor radius was `0x600` bytes.

## Sealed runs

| Run | Samples / elapsed | Anchored windows / anchor qwords | Candidates before / after caps | Paired CallbackData / paired IOPB | Compatible registered near-hits | Exact-instance validated | Errors | Manifest SHA-256 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `20260914T140530Z` | 720,001 / 180.039185 s | 6 / 10 | 363 / 363 | 2 / 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 | `d6137c3e691b25b197c643d3e6726c0c6e11a25c361b17a206965aff3bb6489d` |
| `20260914T141131Z` | 720,001 / 180.005597 s | 6 / 11 | 457 / 457 | 7 / 6 | 2 | 0 | 0 | `f0481215fb3c9be6a48c41476a16a5153b472e80b41e3aa818000169a4564f1d` |
| `20260914T141733Z` | 720,001 / 180.166003 s | 20 / 24 | 1,404 / 1,404 | 11 / 10 | 6 | 0 | 0 | `1211b3691bc1c20c1584ace41c61ab1d2c02e95fa51190503b6812efe14eef58` |

Every run has `status=complete`, `termination=duration_complete`, no `run.incomplete` marker, and thirteen manifest entries whose current hashes match. Every privacy validation reports pass with no guest virtual addresses, raw stack/payload bytes, names, or captured-value digests persisted.

## Final run: exact observations

```

```

| --- | ---: |

The eleven near-misses divide exactly as follows:

| Anchor / distance | Count | Registered and body compatible | Operation | Revalidation | Exact EAC instance result |
| --- | ---: | --- | --- | --- | --- |

| `0x215f03` / 1 qword | 4 | no | not persisted | not attempted | false |

Totals from `summary.json`:

```text

## Exact result and boundary

The final run observed EAC return-address-bearing stack windows and recovered paired-stable structures that passed CallbackData, IOPB, operation-registration, and body-compatibility gates. It did not recover any structure whose `TargetInstance` was an exact live EAC minifilter instance, including after immediate exact-list revalidation. Strict acceptance correctly remained zero.

This result is bounded to observed snapshots. It does not cover callbacks between samples, active live-register-only CallbackData, non-current threads, bytes outside selected stack windows/radii/caps, or structures rejected because any sequential paired read changed. Absence of a validated row is not proof that EAC received no callback.
