# Secondary bugcheck semantic recovery

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

This package uses only captured resident bytes, sealed passive memflow evidence, and read-only physical snapshots. Driver bytes are decoded/interpreted only; no guest execution, guest write, hook, injection, bugcheck, request generation, or upload occurs.

## Closed semantics

The live record is a `KBUGCHECK_REASON_CALLBACK_RECORD` for raw reason 2 (`KbCallbackSecondaryDumpData`), State 1, callback RVA `0xa1120`, and the observed empty Component at EAC RVA `0x2f3fb2`. The callback ABI is RCX Reason, RDX Record, R8 ReasonSpecificData, and R9 ReasonSpecificDataLength. No invocation was passively observed.

RVA `0x1f278d` is an obfuscated constant/offset resolver. For fixed RCX `0x0d7643c629271f7b`, the captured state predicate matches, internal resolver RVA `0x16a9a0` resolves `ntoskrnl+0x469d90`, and two exact opcode predicates accept bytes `8b 81`. The subsequent dword read begins two bytes later and yields `0x1f4`; the function updates its state dword and returns `input RDX + 0x1f4` (or null if the decoded dword were zero).

RVA `0xa2137` calls helpers `0xece38`, `0xed179`, `0xed26c`, and `0xed358`, which recursively derive the PTE, PDE, PPE, and PXE self-map bases. It checks Present at PXE, PPE, PDE, and PTE, checks the PDE Large bit, and returns the PDE self-map entry address for a present 2 MiB mapping or the PTE self-map entry address for a present 4 KiB mapping. A failure at any tested Present predicate returns zero. The body performs no page-table write.

The exact outer protected path reads CR3 at RVA `0x422838` (`0f2018`) and reloads it at RVA `0x3fa6a4` (`0f2218`). The interpreter preserves the current CR3, masks only address/PCID bits for page-table walking, and never writes the snapshot. System CR3 `PA-0005` has self-reference index `0x140`; both sealed snapshots map `ntoskrnl+0x469d90` to physical `PA-0006` through a 2 MiB PDE and contain identical probe bytes.

The page-loop call bridge is RVA `0x414af3`; its modeled RDX candidate advances by exactly `0x1000` on every adjacent call. The prior 5,000-block bounded run observed 518 calls without reaching termination.

## Exact boundaries

The resident image used for the earlier coherent protected-state replay and the available complete physical snapshots are different load epochs. A stale old-epoch kernel VA fails at a non-present PDPTE, while rebasing the exact kernel RVA into the snapshot epoch succeeds. The later loaded-image capture retains protected control data that transfers into the earlier image epoch, so it cannot supply a coherent full callback replay. The old coherent image in turn reaches an old-epoch non-image pool dependency that is absent from both later snapshots. Therefore actual callback-thread CR3, real ReasonSpecificData contents, the loop's concrete start/end candidates, and its terminating predicate remain unobserved. Registration and cleanup callsites also remain unrecovered: live list membership proves registered state, while the resident image has no direct absolute reference to either register/deregister export.

```

`appendices/tpm.md`:

# EAC TPM command-buffer recovery

> **Evidence appendix.** I generated this formal report from the private evidence vault. Kernel virtual addresses are deterministic aliases, host paths are scrubbed, and private binaries, memory, peers, and secrets are not distributed. Module-relative RVAs, structure offsets, counts, hashes, and semantic boundaries are preserved.

<a id="private-evidence-reference"></a>

## Scope and claim boundary

This is an offline scan of sealed memflow page-version blobs for the exact EAC service process in two protected-launch captures. The scanner validates complete TPM 2.0 wire layouts before admitting a finding. `Observed exact` means the buffer bytes and their page/sample location survived capture. `ABI-derived` means big-endian fields were assigned TPM 2.0 structure names. `Strongly inferred` is limited to the shared-handle relationship between two resident request buffers.

A resident request buffer is **not** proof that EAC submitted it, that a TPM transport accepted it, that the device executed it, or that any response returned. No such claim is made here.

## Exact resident command buffers

| Evidence | Capture | Sample / UTC | Buffer VA | Command | Exact parameters | Buffer SHA-256 |
|---|---|---|---:|---|---|---|
| `launch-1-cmd-001` | `launch-1` | `771` / `2026-09-13T23:24:07.167Z` | `0x3056760` | `TPM2_GetCapability` (`0x0000017a`) | capability=0x00000001 (TPM_CAP_HANDLES); property=0x810eac00; count=1 | `1589216b0f48d54c73eb1cdd4e0edcd70f372eefb77ef4a72d9bd6ae9cab1d20` |
| `launch-1-cmd-002` | `launch-1` | `771` / `2026-09-13T23:24:07.167Z` | `0x30577a0` | `TPM2_ReadPublic` (`0x00000173`) | objectHandle=0x810eac00 | `ee1add22be3728bdd28986a025af1d7c9e066236eaacbfa4ea9ae9a3e3157c3e` |
| `launch-2-cmd-001` | `launch-2` | `3275` / `2026-09-14T00:00:48.903Z` | `0x34c69a0` | `TPM2_GetCapability` (`0x0000017a`) | capability=0x00000001 (TPM_CAP_HANDLES); property=0x810eac00; count=1 | `1589216b0f48d54c73eb1cdd4e0edcd70f372eefb77ef4a72d9bd6ae9cab1d20` |

Every admitted request uses `TPM_ST_NO_SESSIONS` (`0x8001`) and has a header length equal to the exact captured buffer length. The first capture contains both commands in the same page-version sample and both name handle `0x810eac00`. Their virtual-address ordering is memory layout only; it is not execution ordering.

## Cross-run result

- `TPM2_GetCapability` with `{"capability": "0x00000001", "capability_name": "TPM_CAP_HANDLES", "property": "0x810eac00", "property_count": 1}` occurs in `launch-1`, `launch-2`; byte-identity assessment: `identical_across_multiple_occurrences`.
- `TPM2_ReadPublic` with `{"object_handle": "0x810eac00"}` occurs in `launch-1`; byte-identity assessment: `not_applicable_single_occurrence`.

`TPM2_GetCapability(TPM_CAP_HANDLES, property=0x810eac00, propertyCount=1)` is reproduced byte-for-byte in both launches. `TPM2_ReadPublic(objectHandle=0x810eac00)` survives only in the first launch. The same-sample, same-handle relationship strongly supports an intended enumerate-then-read dependency, while submission and order remain unobserved.

## Response search and blocker

The strict success-response scan admitted `0` correlated resident response buffer(s). It required either a complete `TPM_CAP_HANDLES` response containing a requested persistent handle or a complete outer `TPM2_ReadPublic` response shape with internally consistent TPM2B lengths and name algorithms.

No admitted response means the captured page versions do not close transport, completion, response-code, public-area, name, qualified-name, or certificate semantics. The page-version collector records new anonymous pages once and did not continuously version every non-executable write, so an overwritten response can be absent even when a request was submitted. Polling/captured-state absence is therefore not treated as proof of non-occurrence.

## Coverage and reproducibility

- `launch-1`: 24875 EAC page records, 14383 unique blobs hash-verified, 2 admitted requests, 0 admitted strict success responses.
- `launch-2`: 22724 EAC page records, 11693 unique blobs hash-verified, 1 admitted requests, 0 admitted strict success responses.

Reproduce from the workspace root:

```text
eac_tpm_repro=$(mktemp -d <temporary-directory>)
PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 python3 analysis/tpm/analyze_tpm_buffers.py --capture launch-1=<private-capture-01> --capture launch-2=<private-capture-02> --output "$eac_tpm_repro/public" --restricted-output-root "$eac_tpm_repro/restricted"
PYTHONDONTWRITEBYTECODE=1 python3 -m unittest analysis/tpm/test_analyze_tpm_buffers.py
```

The released ledger contains hashes and decoded fields; byte-exact request slices are sealed in explicitly selected, capture-external restricted bundles with independent manifests. Public artifacts retain only opaque bundle identity, entry count, and storage/restriction/source-provenance classifications: they intentionally do not serialize or commit to a restricted-manifest digest. This prevents a public digest from becoming an offline oracle over restricted provenance. The analyzer rejects restricted destinations beneath either sealed source capture.

```

`docs/detection-surfaces.md`:

```md
# Detection Surfaces

I do not think of this build as one detector. I think of it as a set of sensors and enforcement points feeding protected policy state.

The table below is the compact map. The sections after it show what I actually recovered.

| Surface | Exact input | Recovered action | What is still unknown |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Process callback | `Process`, `ProcessId`, `CreateInfo` | Create/exit split, internal record lookup, exit markers, crash telemetry | Several protected helper meanings and one stack-derived object identity |
| Thread callback | `ProcessId`, `ThreadId`, `Create` | Object lookups, lock-free reference publication, create/exit split | Some protected context fields and final reference owner |
| Image callback | `FullImageName`, `ProcessId`, `ImageInfo` | Extended info gate, final component extraction, main-image detection, system exclusions | Several downstream protected classifiers |
| Process/thread Object Manager callbacks | create/duplicate parameters | Run identity access transforms and maintain association/audit context; closed transforms preserve access | Runtime table population and universal caller-to-audit-decision matrix |
| Registry callback | operation class and class-specific structure | Deny, hide, synthesize, or bypass selected operations | Invocation frequency and protected helper context ownership |
| Minifilter | CREATE, SET_INFORMATION, acquire-for-section | Name normalization, rename/link policy, executable file read/classify/deny | Four protected extension delegate predicates and live callback instances |
| Device IPC | IRP and current stack | Fifteen candidate classes, handler-specific fields and boundaries | Accepted protocol, deep authentication, complete packets and effects |
| Platform probes | protected/interpreter state | CPU, timing, MSR, descriptor, VMCS, cache, and port operations | Exact live operands and which sites executed |
| Crash/bugcheck | process exit or bugcheck reason | Registry telemetry, atomic state, secondary-dump page walk | Real reason-specific data and final output |
| TPM companion path | TPM request buffer | Persistent-handle capability/public query construction | Completion, response consumer, and network relation |

## Process lifecycle

The process callback uses the normal ABI:

```text
(PEPROCESS Process, HANDLE ProcessId, PPS_CREATE_NOTIFY_INFO CreateInfo)
```

`CreateInfo != NULL` is create. `CreateInfo == NULL` is exit.

The recovered exit path resolves an internal record from a process-derived PID, marks byte and atomic state fields, passes through a protected helper chain, and finalizes through a shared internal routine. When the tracked game record matches the current process and the exit status is nonzero, EAC writes eight structured crash values.

This gives EAC at least three useful dimensions:

- process identity and lifecycle;
- expected game-record correlation;
- abnormal-exit telemetry with create/exit times and product state.

I do not have a complete model of the create-side protected record population. The live callback registration is exact; full execution coverage is not.

## Thread lifecycle

The thread callback receives:

```text
(HANDLE ProcessId, HANDLE ThreadId, BOOLEAN Create)
```

It looks up the process, passes create state plus looked-up/current process objects into protected policy, separately looks up the original thread, and releases the recovered outer references.

The most concrete internal state transition is:

```text
lock cmpxchg [record+0x4e8], replacement
```

Success publishes the replacement reference and calls a reference-like native address. Failure preserves the old slot. Create reaches a create-only record path; exit reaches a distinct handler and releases the looked-up thread.

That is enough to say EAC maintains thread-linked process state, not just a counter. The exact meaning of every protected field is still open.

## Image loads

The image callback receives:

```text
(PUNICODE_STRING FullImageName, HANDLE ProcessId, PIMAGE_INFO ImageInfo)
```

It checks `ImageInfo.Properties & 0x400` before reading `ImageInfo+0x28` as the extended `FileObject`. An optional helper can produce an owned replacement-name buffer, which is later released.

The recovered final-component helper:

- validates the input descriptor and buffer;
- walks in-bounds UTF-16 code units;
- selects the final non-empty path component;
- rejects NUL, colon, backslash, empty, and trailing-component cases;
- writes a normalized output `UNICODE_STRING` on success.

For process images, EAC calls per-process policy and compares the current process section base with `ImageInfo.ImageBase`. That gives it a clean main-image test.

For system images, it excludes `\System32\Drivers\` plus five exact graphics-related image names before invoking the remaining protected bookkeeping and policy chain.

## Process and thread handle policy

Both process-type and thread-type Object Manager registrations use altitude `327530` and cover handle create plus duplicate.

The pre callback:

1. identifies create versus duplicate;
2. selects the target/current process as required by that operation;
3. distinguishes process objects from thread objects;
4. locates the class-specific desired-access field;
5. calls the internal process or thread access helper;
6. stores EAX back into `DesiredAccess`;
7. returns `OB_PREOP_SUCCESS`.

The normal recovered helper paths preserve the input access mask, but the process helper also maintains context/list state and tests a side-effect mask. The post callback gates on successful status, operation type, object equality, handle mode, and granted-access-like state. Its normal event/delta tail is infeasible under the closed path conditions.

The remaining caller-dependent association and audit decisions rely on runtime table/list slots outside the image. I know where that state plugs in. I do not have every live policy entry, and I found no access-bit removal in the closed helpers.

## Registry enforcement

The dispatcher handles only raw classes corresponding to pre-set, pre-query, and pre-open in its recovered native body. Other classes return success there.

The string matcher performs `0xffdf` masking on UTF-16 code units. For the ASCII policy literals in this build, that is case-insensitive comparison. It is not general Unicode folding.

### Pre-set

The pre-set path reads:

```text
Object      +0x00
ValueName   +0x08
Type        +0x14
Data        +0x18
DataSize    +0x20
```

It queries the object name, tests 25 persistence/system-control substrings, and returns `STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED` on a match in the bounded model. The exact set spans:

- Run and RunOnce;
- SafeBoot;
- Active Setup;
- shell context-menu, drag/drop, and overlay handlers;
- browser helper objects;
- Task Scheduler;
- services and font drivers;
- HTML open-command handling;
- KnownDLLs;
- credential providers;
- Winsock protocol and namespace catalogs.

A separate firewall predicate requires a string value, a GUID-shaped value name, `Action=Block`, `Active=TRUE`, and the exact firewall-rules key. That path returns `STATUS_CALLBACK_BYPASS` without writing the set-information structure.

### Pre-query

The pre-query path reads the complete callback structure through `ResultLength`. It can return `STATUS_OBJECT_NAME_NOT_FOUND` for selected AppCompat, Software Restriction Policy, debugger, Image File Execution Options, AppInit, and Winsock paths or values.

The `RequireSignedAppInit_DLLs` case is more interesting. For `KeyValuePartialInformation`, its result changes at exact output-length boundaries. Depending on length, the path returns success without output, buffer-too-small with required size, or callback-bypass with a synthesized DWORD value of one.

### Pre-open

The pre-open path reads `CompleteName`, previous mode, current thread ID, and an internal context thread field. Kernel-mode callers return success on the closed path. User-mode policy differs when the current thread equals the tracked context thread versus when it does not.

This is a caller-context gate, not a flat list of forbidden keys.

## File-system enforcement

The minifilter has no post-operation callbacks and no registered stream/file/instance contexts. Its action is concentrated in the pre-operation bodies and EAC's own records.

### CREATE

The outer path acquires and parses normalized name information, reads desired access, checks internal context state, and reaches the recovered name/access policy. It owns all cleanup for the name information and temporary state on the modeled exits.

### SET_INFORMATION

The recovered path recognizes the rename/link information classes:

```text
FileRenameInformation        10
FileLinkInformation          11
FileRenameInformationEx      65
FileLinkInformationEx        72
```

It handles source and destination name state and can deny the operation through the shared policy machinery.

### Executable section synchronization

At PASSIVE_LEVEL and under the executable-protection branch, EAC:

1. queries standard file information;
2. validates the returned structure size;
3. inspects the signed file length;
4. applies a normal 8 MiB cap;
5. allocates a buffer;
6. reads from offset zero;
7. invokes an internal classifier;
8. frees the buffer;
9. permits or completes with `STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED`.

That is a direct file-content policy point on the executable mapping path.

## Device communication

The device-control dispatcher reads the current stack location and the control code before delegated handler entry. The common prefix does not read the file object, `FsContext`, caller token, or caller process in the recovered path.

The CTL-shaped candidates require both read and write access, and both captured game/wrapper handles have enough granted access to pass that coarse I/O-manager gate.

Deep authorization could still occur in a handler or through a global process/file-object map. Without a naturally captured accepted IRP, I cannot collapse “candidate handler” into “public protocol.”

## Platform and virtualization signals

The static reachable inventory includes:

- CPU vendor and feature tuples;
- ISA dispatch and SHA acceleration selection;
- CPUID timing against adjacent RDTSC baselines;
- dynamic MSR reads/writes and performance-counter reads;
- descriptor-table and selector reads;
- VMREAD status behavior;
- cache invalidation operations;
- keyed I/O-port writes;
- RTM transaction probing.

Some paths have explicit exception fallbacks. Others have no local handler. Most operands come from protected runtime state, so the binary does not expose a tidy list of fixed MSRs, ports, or VMCS fields.

I treat these as platform-observation capability. I do not claim each instruction ran during the capture.

## Crash and bugcheck state

The process exit path preserves structured game crash information. Separately, EAC registers two bugcheck-reason callbacks.

Reason 1 performs a reason-insensitive atomic state transition in the closed model. Reason 2 enters a protected path that derives a kernel offset, computes paging self-map bases, checks present and large-page state, reads and reloads CR3, and iterates candidate addresses by 4 KiB.

That is consistent with secondary dump-data preparation. A real invocation and output buffer were not captured, so the final emitted content is still unknown.

## How the surfaces combine

The useful mental model is a correlation engine:

```text
process identity
  + thread history
  + loaded-image identity
  + handle-access context
  + registry policy state
  + file classification
  + platform profile
  + launch-stage state
  + crash/dump evidence
```

I can prove the sensors and several enforcement paths. The complete scoring or policy-combination layer remains protected.

```

`docs/device-and-ipc.md`:

```md
# Device Namespace, IPC, and Authorization

The old version of this investigation said the device ACL and symbolic link were unknown. That is no longer true.

The current result is more useful: I know the namespace, symbolic-link target, security descriptors, dispatch table, CREATE/CLOSE behavior, captured handle state, and the point where deeper authorization becomes opaque.

## Namespace

The exact native objects are:

```text
Driver object   \Driver\EasyAntiCheat_EOSSys
Device object   \Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS
Symbolic link   \GLOBAL??\EasyAntiCheat_EOS
Link target     \Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS
```

That global DOS-device link is the Win32-visible entry point used by the captured launch components.

## Device object

The live device fields are:

| Field | Value |
| --- | --- |
| DeviceType | `0x22` (`FILE_DEVICE_UNKNOWN`) |
| Flags | `0x40` (`DO_DEVICE_HAS_NAME`) |
| Characteristics | `0x100` (`FILE_DEVICE_SECURE_OPEN`) |
| StackSize | `1` |
| DeviceExtension | null |
| AttachedDevice | null |
| CurrentIrp | null in the captured states |
| Object-header security fast-reference | null |

The device is not attached above another device in the captured state. It has no extension-backed per-device context exposed through `DeviceExtension`.

`FILE_DEVICE_SECURE_OPEN` means trailing-name checks use the device's security semantics. It does not magically explain a null captured device security fast-reference. The effective Object Manager decision path is still a separate question from the symbolic-link DACL.

## Symbolic-link security

The link has a self-relative DACL with these allow ACEs:

| Principal | SID abbreviation | Access mask |
| --- | --- | ---: |
| Everyone | WD | `0x00020001` |
| LocalSystem | SY | `0x000f0001` |
| Built-in Administrators | BA | `0x000f0001` |
| All Application Packages | AC | `0x00020001` |

The limited entries combine link-query rights with `READ_CONTROL`. SYSTEM and Administrators also receive the broader standard-rights set.

This is broad namespace visibility. It is not proof that every caller can open the target device with useful I/O rights.

## Driver-object security

The driver object has a separate descriptor:

| Principal | Access mask |
| --- | ---: |
| LocalSystem | `0x001f01ff` |
| Built-in Administrators | `0x001200a9` |

The link ACL and driver-object ACL protect different objects. Neither is a substitute for reconstructing the final device-open decision.

## Dispatch table

The 28-entry major-function table reduces to three behaviors:

```text
CREATE          EAC shared create/close dispatcher
CLOSE           EAC shared create/close dispatcher
DEVICE_CONTROL  EAC protected command dispatcher
everything else nt!IopInvalidDeviceRequest
```

`IRP_MJ_CLEANUP` is in the last group. The native invalid-request routine writes `STATUS_INVALID_DEVICE_REQUEST`, completes with `IO_NO_INCREMENT`, and returns the same status.

That is unusual but internally consistent with a CREATE/CLOSE path that allocates no per-file resources.

## CREATE and CLOSE

The exact second-capture protected model produces one shared thirteen-node path for CREATE and CLOSE.

The only IRP mutations are:

```text
IRP.IoStatus.Status      = STATUS_SUCCESS
IRP.IoStatus.Information = 0
IofCompleteRequest(IRP, IO_NO_INCREMENT)
return STATUS_SUCCESS
```

The path performs zero reads from:

- the incoming `DEVICE_OBJECT`;
- the IRP outside the two output fields;
- the current stack location;
- the `FILE_OBJECT`;
- `DesiredAccess`;
- share access;
- caller process;
- caller token.

It performs no allocation and stores no `FsContext` or `FsContext2`.

This is a useful negative because it removes a common assumption: the driver does not establish a cryptographic session, PID cookie, or per-handle context during CREATE in the closed path.

## Captured file objects and handles

The EAC-suffixed wrapper and protected game both hold persistent device handles across early and steady snapshots.

All four file-object observations agree:

```text
FsContext            0
FsContext2           0
FileObjectExtension  0
FileName             empty
```

The wrapper and game file-object flags differ slightly, but neither contains driver-owned per-handle state. Both handles have granted access `0x12019f`.

The recovered CTL-shaped candidates encode both read and write access requirements. These handles therefore satisfy the coarse I/O Manager access check before the driver sees a request.

## DEVICE_CONTROL common prefix

The command dispatcher reads:

- `IRP.CurrentStackLocation`;
- `IO_STACK_LOCATION.Parameters.DeviceIoControl.IoControlCode`.

Before delegated handler entry, the recovered common prefix does not read:

- the file object;
- `FsContext` or `FsContext2`;
- caller PID;
- current process;
- access token;
- security context.

That does not prove there is no authentication. It places any deeper authentication after dispatch selection or in a global state lookup.

## Bounded command classes

The dispatcher model separates fifteen `METHOD_NEITHER`-shaped control-flow classes. Fourteen delegate to individual handler entries; one is inline.

Recovered field behavior includes:

- handler-specific placement of the stack-location pointer across `RCX`, `RDX`, `R8`, and `R9`;
- `InputBufferLength` reads;
- `OutputBufferLength` reads on selected handlers;
- `Type3InputBuffer` reads;
- `IRP.UserBuffer` identity on one path;
- a null-Type3 early split;
- exact tested boundaries at `15/16`, `0x237/0x238`, and selected singleton lengths;
- one four-byte zero write to a handler argument;
- several internal native/protected targets.

One path forwards `(InputBufferLength, Type3InputBuffer)` and `(OutputBufferLength, UserBuffer)` into the same state-derived callee and returns the zero-extended input length in every declared probe.

These statements are exact within their seeded differential models.

## What I will not claim yet

I have not proved:

- that all fifteen candidate control values are accepted naturally;
- complete request or response structures;
- the session state required before a handler succeeds;
- caller, process, token, or image authorization inside delegated handlers;
- final completion statuses for accepted requests;
- downstream mutations or effects;
- an out-of-band process/file-object authorization map;
- original `IoCreateDevice` versus `IoCreateDeviceSecure` construction;
- original SDDL or class GUID;
- exact symbolic-link construction callsite;
- full unload deletion order.

The biggest mistake would be turning a control-flow discriminator into a client protocol. I have not done that.

## Current authorization model

What the bytes support today is:

```text
global symbolic link is query-visible
  -> target device open succeeds for captured launch components
  -> CREATE establishes no per-handle state
  -> captured handles have read/write-capable granted access
  -> I/O Manager applies CTL access bits
  -> DEVICE_CONTROL selects a protected handler
  -> deeper state/caller authorization remains unresolved
```

That is a much narrower and more accurate boundary than either “the device is open to everyone” or “EAC authenticates every handle during create.”

```

`docs/evidence-rules.md`:

```md
# Evidence Rules and Limitations

I used strict vocabulary because this target punishes loose vocabulary.

A callback pointer can exist without the callback running. A socket can exist without the message I care about crossing it. A TPM request can sit in memory without ever reaching the TPM. A static VMREAD can be reachable without executing during the capture. A queue can be empty at two points while work runs between them.

The labels below keep those cases separate.

## Claim classes

### Observed

The claim is directly present in captured state:

- exact bytes;
- a kernel object field;
- a handle-table entry;
- a list link;
- a PTE permission;
- a resident buffer;
- a thread-stack qword;
- a timestamped watcher record;
- a complete physical-memory read.

Observed does not mean globally complete. It means the stated value exists in the stated artifact.

### Derived

The claim follows deterministically from observed evidence:

- a PE RVA from section mapping;
- a field name from the exact matched PDB offset;
- a callback ABI from the registered interface;
- a branch predicate from decoded instructions;
- a virtual-to-physical translation;
- a raw-to-loaded relocation normalization;
- a structure member chosen from an exact union discriminator;
- a model result over an explicitly declared input domain.

If the derivation uses a bounded domain, the bounds stay in the claim.

### Inferred

Multiple observations support the explanation, but one private event or value is missing.

Example: the service waits on the game event while the wrapper holds modify rights, and the event later becomes signaled. It is reasonable to infer a wrapper-side game-readiness signal. The actual `NtSetEvent` callsite was not captured, so the signaler remains unproved.

### Unknown

The required evidence is absent or incoherent:

- the event completed between polls;
- the object was freed before capture;
- the pointer belongs to another load epoch;
- the resolver key lived only in a missing frame;
- the protected path depends on a runtime table outside the captured image;
- the user address space cannot be translated;
- the proprietary name would require a missing private PDB.

Unknown is not a synonym for unlikely.

## Registration is not invocation

I recovered active registrations for process, thread, image, Object Manager, registry, Filter Manager, and bugcheck callbacks.

That proves Windows would dispatch to EAC when the corresponding event occurs and the registration is active. It does not prove a specific callback fired during a selected capture interval.

Active private per-thread contexts are stronger because their callback and dispatcher return appear on associated stacks with exact call spacing. Even there, protected body effects beyond the captured frame remain separate.

## A resident buffer is not a completed operation

The TPM request buffers are exact. The response is not present.

The shared section contains the entire driver. The final consumer and verifier are not yet identified.

The service has an AFD endpoint. The application payload is not retained.

I refuse to manufacture data-flow edges from co-location.

## A handle is not a query

The service and game processes hold handles to ACPI, TPM, CNG, KsecDD, registry, certificate, AFD, NSI, pipe, file-system, section, event, and device objects.

That proves reachability and granted rights. It does not enumerate every `DeviceIoControl`, registry query, file read, crypto operation, or socket send completed through those handles.

## A raw stack pointer is not an unwind frame

Several System threads contain aligned qwords in EAC executable ranges. Two surviving user-requestor IRPs also have EAC-range qwords in active stack windows.

Unless call spacing, return sites, unwind metadata, and control-flow ownership agree, I label those as raw range hits. They may be return addresses, arguments, data, or stale content.

The private callback contexts earned active-dispatch status because all those conditions did agree.

## Physical captures are complete and non-atomic

Each sealed physical image is a complete 19.33 GB sequential sweep with zero failed pages. The VM continued running during acquisition.

That creates two truths:

- every byte position in the output was read exactly once;
- different pages may represent different guest instants.

List entries crossing pages can therefore tear. I keep backlink mismatches and record the exact failure stage. I do not silently discard them or call them corruption.

## Polling absence is scoped

The 500 Hz observer materially improves the lifecycle timeline. Its maximum blind interval was still 11.866 ms.

An object that exists for 500 microseconds can be real and never sampled. Therefore:

```text
not observed in the selected samples
```

does not become:

```text
never happened
```

The scheduler inventory uses the same rule. No EAC work item, DPC, APC, or timer survived the two physical epochs. That is a strong captured-state negative, not a historical ban on those mechanisms.

## Build identity is part of every semantic claim

Every RVA and function model in this repository belongs to one raw SHA-256. A later EAC build may move, replace, or remove any path.

The Windows kernel layouts also belong to one exact kernel CodeView identity. I did not use offsets from a nearby Windows revision because they looked close.

## Epoch identity is part of every pointer claim

Code RVAs can be rebased when executable identity matches. Dynamic state cannot.

I do not transplant:

- pool addresses;
- process or thread objects;
- CR3 values;
- list heads;
- VADs;
- resolver tables;
- stack values;
- object headers;
- mutable driver globals

between launch epochs without proving the relationship.

This is the explicit blocker on the secondary bugcheck replay: one image has coherent protected state, another snapshot has coherent page tables, and the stale pool dependency does not bridge them.

## Bounded models say exactly what they modeled

The static interpreters use seeded memory, selected captured state, and controlled external-call returns. Their results are deterministic.

They do not become live traces.

Examples:

- testing lengths `0..32` proves those lengths;
- finding a split at `0x237/0x238` proves the adjacent probes in the declared state;
- forcing a string helper to “match” proves the caller's match branch;
- forcing an external return to a resident pointer proves the caller's pointer branch;
- a branch that never reads the seeded Type3 region proves a bounded negative for that state, not for all valid requests.

## Hashes have scope too

I use different identities for different artifacts:

- raw-file SHA-256;
- full loaded-image SHA-256 for one epoch;
- stable executable-section aggregate;
- per-section hashes;
- per-page hashes;
- normalized raw-to-loaded comparison;
- manifest hashes.

A sparse image with zero-filled holes is not assigned the raw-file identity. A time-smeared mapped fragment is not assigned a loaded-image identity.

## Public-release boundary

The public repository contains the information needed to evaluate the findings:

- exact build hashes;
- module-relative RVAs where useful;
- normalized structure layouts;
- counts and state transitions;
- bounded field semantics;
- sanitized ledgers;
- validation totals;
- evidence and uncertainty rules.

It excludes:

- EAC and Windows binaries;
- physical-memory and process-memory captures;
- full stack and object payload dumps;
- credentials, tokens, cookies, account identifiers, pipe components, and network peers;
- host-specific absolute paths;
- protected-state keys that are not needed to explain a finding;
- an EAC request client;
- packet-generation, bypass, suppression, tampering, or exploit instructions.

This does not weaken the technical conclusions. It separates a public explanation from the evidence vault used to derive it.

## Corrections are first-class evidence

Two corrections are deliberately prominent:

1. `_KAPC.Header.Size` is `0x58` bytes, not eleven qwords.
2. the sixteen callback-shaped records are not one work-item family; they split into two bugcheck records and fourteen private EAC contexts, with the private sentinel corrected to RVA `0x30cb90`.

I would rather publish a correction with the proof than preserve a clean narrative built on a false assumption.

```

`docs/kernel-architecture.md`:

```md
# Kernel Architecture and Execution

The driver is large enough to make “open it in a disassembler and start at `DriverEntry`” a bad strategy.

The captured loaded image is about 42.9 MiB. Roughly 39.45 MiB belongs to one high-entropy executable section behind a protected state machine. The normal native surface—entry, callback veneers, dispatch functions, string and structure helpers, crypto, decompression, and a few large policy bodies—is the scaffolding around that machine.

I mapped the scaffolding first.

## Image layout

The raw file and loaded image have different sizes:

| Form | Size | Identity |
| --- | ---:| --- |
| Raw signed PE | 44,666,520 bytes | SHA-256 `020d5da6…ef74` |
| Captured loaded image | 44,986,368 bytes | epoch-specific full-image hash |
| Stable executable aggregate | 43,859,748 bytes | SHA-256 `99802a5b…d1` |

The executable aggregate is made of three sections:

| Section | RVA | Virtual size | SHA-256 |
| --- | ---: | ---: | --- |
| `.text` | `0x1000` | 2,458,916 | `fab345a8…f9` |
| unnamed executable | `0x35a000` | 31,232 | `576fa921…21` |
| protected executable | `0x362000` | 41,369,600 | `2cbc7606…e4` |

I normalized 6,015 relocations before comparing raw and loaded code. All three executable sections matched across five complete images from two launches.

## Entry

The native entry sequence is short:

1. preserve the two incoming arguments;
2. initialize and validate the security cookie;
3. pass the saved arguments into the protected initializer;
4. transition into the protected state machine.

The cookie helper compares the global against zero and the default cookie constant, fail-fasts on an invalid/default state, stores the complement, and returns. The meaningful initialization work begins after the protected transition.

## Driver object

The live `DRIVER_OBJECT` is conventionally linked. Its loader entry agrees with the linked module list. I found no evidence that this build hid its primary driver object or main image from normal kernel enumeration.

The dispatch table is intentionally narrow:

| Major function | Target |
| --- | --- |
| CREATE | shared EAC create/close dispatcher |
| CLOSE | shared EAC create/close dispatcher |
| DEVICE_CONTROL | protected EAC dispatcher |
| CLEANUP | `nt!IopInvalidDeviceRequest` |
| READ | `nt!IopInvalidDeviceRequest` |
| WRITE | `nt!IopInvalidDeviceRequest` |
| all other majors | `nt!IopInvalidDeviceRequest` |

CREATE and CLOSE do not establish a session object in the recovered trace. They write success and zero information, complete the IRP, and return. The communication surface is DEVICE_CONTROL.

## Registered callback architecture

The stable registration set is:

```text
Ps process notify        1 EAC callback
Ps thread notify         1 EAC callback
Ps load-image notify     1 EAC callback
OB process type          create + duplicate, pre + post
OB thread type           create + duplicate, pre + post
Cm registry              altitude 327530
Bugcheck reason          two records
Filter Manager           CREATE, SET_INFORMATION, acquire-for-section
```

The process, thread, image, Object Manager, and registry entries all lead through short native veneers into protected context-save bridges. Their public registration structures are still useful because they expose ABI, operations, altitude, state, and callback identity before the virtualized body begins.

## The protected state machine

The recurring protected entry pattern is:

```text
native callback or handler veneer
  -> relative jump
  -> context-save stub
  -> 0x1c0-byte saved state frame
  -> keyed selector/dispatcher transitions
  -> native helper or another protected state
```

`r12` frequently anchors the saved state frame in the bounded models. Registers, continuation values, state qwords, resolver keys, and external return values are moved through that frame.

The machine contains deliberately awkward control flow:

- complementary conditional branches that reconverge;
- dead prefix bytes;
- state-dependent indirect calls;
- short veneers outside PDATA;
- mutable resolver inputs;
- native helper islands inside long protected flows;
- instruction-byte sequences that are not all reachable instructions.

That last point matters for low-level instruction counts. The raw protected section contains many `0f 30`, `0f 31`, `0f 32`, and similar byte pairs. I only promoted sites reached by the conservative control-flow inventory, then decoded and modeled each local body.

## Native API resolution

The driver does not expose a friendly conventional import table for every kernel service it uses. Protected paths recover or decode native targets from keyed state.

I closed a large resolver census by combining:

- callsite instruction bytes;
- captured mutable data slots;
- cross-launch state rotation;
- exact native image exports;
- expected calling ABI;
- downstream field behavior.

Two caller-frame-keyed sites remain because the needed runtime RCX values were not captured. I keep them unresolved rather than naming a convenient export.

## Function census

The exception directory defines 5,476 exact PDATA functions:

```text
.text                 1,462
small unnamed code        1
protected executable  4,013
```

The first complete census produced:

| Measure | Count |
| --- | ---: |
| Fully decoded | 1,380 |
| At least 90% decoded | 1,455 |
| Zero decoded | 1 |
| Named PDATA functions | 64 |
| Unnamed PDATA functions | 5,412 |
| Phase-1 unresolved records | 18,125 |
| Deduplicated direct edges | 25,548 |
| Direct calls | 4,424 |

Fully decoded is not the same as understood. A function can decode perfectly while calling an unresolved state-dependent target or operating on an unnamed proprietary object.

## Private per-thread execution contexts

EAC maintains a private circular list rooted at RVA `0x30cb90`. Each active record is `0x78` bytes and stores:

```text
+0x00  LIST_ENTRY
+0x10  callback veneer
+0x18  associated context
+0x20  flags/mode
+0x28  current KTHREAD
+0x30  KEVENT
+0x48  KEVENT
+0x60  queryable byte
+0x68  unresolved qword
+0x70  unresolved qword
```

The allocator stores the current thread, initializes both notification events, sets the query byte, validates the list tail, and inserts the object. The query routine finds a record by thread. The remover validates both neighbors, unlinks, releases the lock, and frees the allocation.

Fourteen active records persisted across both physical captures. A fifteenth lifecycle-only record with a null callback disappeared through an exact backlink-valid unlink.

For every active node, the callback pointer and matching dispatcher return address appeared on the associated System thread's captured stack at the exact spacing produced by the recovered indirect call. That turns passive list membership into active-dispatch evidence.

## Deferred execution

I separately walked the standard scheduler structures:

- 32 executive worker lists;
- two DPC queues on each of 16 CPUs;
- current and saved kernel/user APC lists for 2,246 and 2,303 threads;
- two 256-slot timer tables on each CPU;
- a complete physical EAC-code-pointer scan.

No EAC-owned work item, DPC, APC, or timer survived either capture.

Six APCs did survive at steady state. They were exact embedded `KTHREAD.SchedulerApc` records using the normal Windows scheduler routines. They belonged to Fortnite and a background task, not to EAC callbacks.

The negative is capture-scoped. I did not conclude that EAC never queued a DPC or worker item at another time.

## Bugcheck integration

Two `KBUGCHECK_REASON_CALLBACK_RECORD`s sit on the matched kernel reason list:

- reason 1, state 1;
- reason 2 (`KbCallbackSecondaryDumpData`), state 1.

Reason 1 reaches a bounded path with an atomic global state transition.

Reason 2 preserves reason-specific data through a protected bridge, resolves a kernel offset, derives self-map bases, checks page-table presence and large-page state, reads and reloads CR3, and advances candidates by `0x1000` in the observed loop model.

I have not observed an actual bugcheck invocation. Registration is exact. Final secondary-dump output is not.

## Minifilter integration

The Filter Manager graph contains one EAC filter with four instances in the captured state. The operation table covers create, set-information, and acquire-for-section synchronization. No post callbacks or Filter Manager contexts are registered.

This keeps most state in EAC's own structures. The 41-row name-policy table, per-record counters, retained names, access snapshots, timing, and frame-chain snapshots are proprietary state beside the Filter Manager objects.

## Page permissions

One live page-table sample marked 43,864,064 of 44,986,368 driver-image bytes both writable and executable.

```text
W+X fraction: 97.505235%
```

The exact cross-run code comparison found no executable mutation. Those two facts coexist:

- I did not observe modified executable bytes.
- The sampled permissions would allow modification without a permission transition if some independent kernel write primitive existed.

I treat that as a hardening issue, not proof of exploitation.

## Outside-image execution

The broad executable-memory surveys found no new MZ image unique to the live EAC phase. One 4 KiB W+X thunk page appeared near the module in one capture, but no driver pointer, callback, dispatch field, or thread start targeted it.

Its proximity is interesting. Its ownership is unknown.

## Unload

The final snapshot after normal teardown contains no linked EAC module, named EAC driver/device object, or EAC process/thread/image callback. The baseline and final enumerated object/callback sets match.

A supplemental lifecycle capture shows the minifilter dispatcher entering a teardown target, callbacks disappearing, instances draining, and fresh identities on reload.

What I still lack is one exact, fully ordered destructor graph covering callbacks, minifilter, device, symbolic link, private lists, events, section handles, and every initialization-failure rollback.

```

`docs/methodology.md`:

```md
# How I Reversed It With Memflow

I treated the VM as evidence, not as a place to install more evidence-generating code.

Everything observable came from the Linux host through memflow: process enumeration, virtual reads, kernel objects, page tables, thread structures, handle tables, loaded images, and two complete physical-memory sweeps. I launched the game in the Windows guest because EAC needs a real lifecycle. I did not inject into the service, hook the driver, attach a debugger, load an auxiliary kernel driver, open the EAC device, or replay private requests.

That constraint made the work harder. It also made the evidence cleaner.

## Stage 1: establish the timeline

The first collector watched the launch chain for:

- process creation and disappearance;
- EAC module appearance and disappearance;
- mapped ranges;
- readable page transitions;
- process threads;
- driver and device object state.

The nominal module watcher ran at 25 ms. The full process/page pass was slower because it performed many sequential reads. Its median active pass was roughly one second, with larger startup gaps.

That immediately defined a boundary: the output was a sequence of resident-state observations, not a complete event log.

I later built a focused observer that held 500 Hz across 210,001 samples. The median interval was 1.954 ms and p95 was 1.984 ms. That tightened the lifecycle brackets dramatically, but even a 1.954 ms interval can miss a short-lived object. I never used “not sampled” as “never existed.”

## Stage 2: fix the collector before trusting the data

The original fast observer used an aggregate batch result to decide whether dependent stages should run. A partial range failure could therefore suppress records that were individually valid.

I wrote a failing test around that behavior, changed the collector so successful list items survived independently, and forced zeroed/failed items through per-record structural validation. A later 15,001-sample validation confirmed the corrected behavior.

This sounds mundane compared with reverse engineering a protected kernel driver. It was more important than several days of instruction decoding. A fast collector that silently drops good records gives precise-looking nonsense.

## Stage 3: recover one exact raw driver

I found the raw driver staged in memory before I had an authoritative disk file. There were partial pages, repeated views, sparse convenience dumps, and multiple launch epochs. I did not pick the cleanest-looking blob and call it original.

I tracked every source fragment by:

- process and virtual address;
- raw-file offset;
- observed length;
- page hash;
- capture timestamp;
- source epoch;
- conflict status.

I built an offset-labelled union only from agreeing observations. Conflicting or missing bytes stayed unresolved until another capture supplied them. The completed raw-length file then passed PE parsing, section consistency, security-directory reconstruction, and signature verification.

The second launch independently produced the same raw SHA-256. From that point forward, every RVA, function model, and callback pointer could be bound to one exact build.

## Stage 4: distinguish raw, loaded, and live-mutated bytes

A raw PE is not a loaded image. I normalized:

- section placement;
- zero-fill;
- image base;
- relocations;
- loader-populated IAT state;
- writable live data.

After 6,015 relocation normalizations, all three executable sections matched the signed raw content in five complete images. That let me use one captured loaded image as static code authority while treating writable data as epoch-specific state.

I never used a time-smeared mapped fragment as an original file. Sparse views are useful for navigation; their hashes do not identify the original binary.

## Stage 5: bind the Windows kernel ABI

The VM's running kernel exposed a CodeView GUID and age. I used that identity to select the exact public PDB and derived structure offsets from it.

That gave me build-matched layouts for the important structures:

```text
EPROCESS
ETHREAD
KTHREAD
KAPC
KDPC
KTIMER
IRP
IO_STACK_LOCATION
OBJECT_HEADER
OBJECT_DIRECTORY
FILE_OBJECT
DEVICE_OBJECT
DRIVER_OBJECT
Filter Manager objects
callback registration records
```

I validated the layouts against live invariants: canonical pointers, list backlinks, embedded object addresses, type bytes, sizes, owners, and known public symbols.

This caught two parser mistakes that would otherwise have infected later conclusions. `_KAPC.Header.Size` is `0x58` bytes, not eleven qwords. Timer header bytes adjacent to Type belong to timer-specific union fields, not the same generic tuple.

## Stage 6: enumerate registered surfaces twice

I walked the kernel's registered callback structures in two independently sealed physical acquisitions. Payload fields were accepted only when immediate paired reads matched.

For each family I recorded:

- list or slot owner;
- callback address and module-relative RVA;
- context;
- operation flags;
- altitude;
- state;
- structure closure;
- pre/post executable-section hashes.

The complete EAC registration projection repeated across both acquisitions, excluding only changing bugcheck checksum fields. That separated stable registration state from one torn snapshot.

## Stage 7: build object graphs, not directory listings

A name in `\Device` is not enough. I resolved:

```text
process handle
  -> object header
  -> object body
  -> file object
  -> device object
  -> driver object
  -> dispatch table
```

For sections I followed:

```text
handle
  -> section
  -> control area
  -> segment
  -> prototype PTEs
  -> process VADs
```

For events I retained object identity, signal state, wait-list topology, waiter thread, and every cross-process handle reference.

This is how I proved that two processes referenced the same section and game event. Matching names would have been weaker and vulnerable to same-name object confusion.

## Stage 8: use physical scans when list roots are incomplete

Some private records were not discoverable from a public kernel list. I scanned every aligned qword in both 19.33 GB physical images for pointers into EAC executable sections.

The scan retained all hits—2,959 in one epoch and 7,507 in the other—without a cap. Each structural candidate then had to pass:

- virtual-address reconstruction;
- physical/virtual byte equality;
- canonical pointer checks;
- executable-section ownership;
- complete field layout;
- forward backlink;
- backward backlink;
- cross-capture identity comparison.

The process was expensive. It was also the only reason the private callback family became visible as a coherent object type.

## Stage 9: combine static instructions with dynamic ownership

Static decoding gives me offsets and branches. Dynamic state gives me objects and values. ABI binding gives me names. The claim becomes strong only when the three agree.

For example:

1. Static code loads `gs:[0x188]` into object offset `+0x28`.
2. The matched ABI identifies `gs:[0x188]` as the current `KTHREAD` path.
3. Captured objects contain valid live `KTHREAD` pointers at `+0x28`.
4. Each callback pointer and dispatcher return appears on that thread's stack with the exact call-induced spacing.

That is enough to classify the object as a private per-current-thread callback context. A `LIST_ENTRY` and callback pointer alone were not.

## Stage 10: model protected code in bounded domains

The driver has 5,476 exact PDATA function extents, a huge protected executable section, short veneers, context-save stubs, mutable resolver state, and state-dependent indirect transfers.

I wrote small deterministic interpreters for selected entry paths. Each model fixed the exact code bytes and changed one controlled dimension:

- request length;
- null versus non-null pointer;
- callback information class;
- previous mode;
- process/thread equality;
- string-helper result;
- external call return;
- captured table value.

The result is a bounded semantic statement. If lengths `0..32` were enumerated, I say `0..32`. If an external routine was forced to zero to reveal a branch, I say that return was forced. This avoids the usual trap where a symbolic-looking model gets described as an instruction trace.

## Stage 11: track calls and ownership independently

For every interesting call I tried to answer three separate questions:

1. Where does control go?
2. What arguments are passed?
3. Who owns the objects before and after the call?

Many reverse-engineering reports stop after question one. That leaves reference leaks, use-after-free boundaries, and cleanup semantics invisible.

The process/thread callback models therefore record lookups and releases. The minifilter paths record allocation/free ownership. The private callback package records list lock, insertion, query, unlink, and free. The device CREATE/CLOSE path records that no per-handle allocation exists at all.

## Stage 12: test the analysis itself

Every focused package carries some combination of:

- RED-first fixtures;
- unit tests;
- statement or branch coverage;
- raw-byte hash assertions;
- PDATA-boundary assertions;
- provenance checks;
- exact-count invariants;
- manifest verification;
- independent full-image rehashing where warranted.

The deferred-execution package is a representative example:

```text
15/15 collector tests
114/114 semantic, identity, provenance, and safety checks
104/104 independent deep-snapshot checks
153/153 manifest verification checks
```

The point is not that a green test proves the reverse engineering is perfect. The point is that corrections propagate through machine-readable invariants instead of relying on me remembering which paragraph became stale.

## Stage 13: keep epochs separate

The hardest failure mode was not obfuscation. It was time.

Kernel bases changed between launches. Pool objects disappeared. protected control data survived in one captured image but not another. A valid pointer from epoch A could point into unmapped or unrelated memory under epoch B.

I therefore attach an epoch to every dynamic value. Code RVAs can be rebased when the loaded image is identical. Heap pointers, list heads, CR3 values, process objects, thread objects, and resolver tables cannot be transplanted without an explicit relationship.

That is why the secondary bugcheck path is partially closed rather than “fully emulated.” The code and page-table walker are known. The coherent live callback arguments are not.

## What this methodology can and cannot prove

It can prove:

- exact resident bytes;
- exact object/list/handle state;
- exact build-matched field offsets;
- exact static instructions and local data flow;
- deterministic behavior over declared model inputs;
- cross-capture invariance;
- captured-state absence under a complete enumerator.

It cannot automatically prove:

- calls that completed between polls;
- invocation merely from registration;
- transport merely from a resident buffer;
- causal order across a non-atomic physical sweep;
- a live return value from a forced model return;
- ownership merely from proximity;
- a private source-level name without symbols.

That boundary is not a weakness of the report. It is the reason the findings are usable.

```

`docs/platform-and-crypto.md`:

```md
# Platform, Timing, Crypto, and Decompression

The protected image contains enough low-level instructions to tempt lazy conclusions. A raw search finds hundreds of CPUID, RDTSC, MSR, and descriptor-looking byte sequences. Many are data or unreachable instruction interiors.

I only counted sites that survived conservative reachability and local instruction validation. Then I classified every one of those sites.

## Reachable low-level census

| Family | Conservatively reachable sites |
| --- | ---: |
| CPUID | 20 |
| XGETBV | 2 |
| RDMSR | 15 |
| WRMSR | 18 |
| RDPMC | 1 |
| RDTSC | 22 |
| Other privileged/transition sites | 15 |
| Total | 93 |

The last group contains OUT, SIDT, SGDT, WBINVD, INVD, XBEGIN, XEND, VMREAD, SLDT, SMSW, and STR.

These 93 sites are static reachable mechanisms. They are not 93 captured detections.

## CPUID and XGETBV

Nine CPUID sites are entry-keyed memoized bit predicates. They retain an encoded boolean token rather than the full four-register response. Two of those predicates select SHA-NI versus scalar SHA-256 compression.

Six CPUID sites plus both XGETBV sites belong to the byte-fill and general ISA dispatch initialization paths.

The remaining notable classes are:

- leaf-zero vendor tuple construction using `EBX || EDX || ECX`;
- a protected-interpreter CPUID operation that writes sign-extended register results into its state record;
- repeated CPUID timing compared with an adjacent-RDTSC baseline;
- a cached CPUID.ECX gate selecting an accelerated or table-driven checksum path;
- one CPUID body that increments an external counter before converging on an intentional breakpoint path.

The vendor and feature detection is exact. The ultimate policy meaning of the protected class token is not.

## RDTSC timing

The 22 reachable RDTSC sites split into several patterns:

- seed only when an existing state value is zero;
- consume EAX and discard EDX;
- preserve the full timestamp and mix it into state;
- bracket or compare other instructions;
- build a timing record and hand it to an unresolved protected callee.

The CPUID-timing path is the strongest platform-timing result. It measures repeated RDTSC/CPUID latency and compares it with an equal-relation adjacent-RDTSC baseline. It retains both cycle totals plus the last CPUID tuple.

That is a real timing primitive. The final classification threshold or policy consumer remains protected.

## MSR and performance-counter operations

The 15 RDMSR, 18 WRMSR, and one RDPMC sites are mostly protected-machine micro-operations.

Nine RDMSR bodies read the index from protected state, save a continuation, execute the read, write EAX/EDX back into state, set a success/fault status, and jump through the saved continuation.

Fifteen WRMSR bodies similarly load EAX/ECX/EDX from protected state, execute the write, preserve state, set status, and continue indirectly.

The RDPMC body does the same for its selected performance counter.

Other native bodies include:

- an invocation-keyed RDMSR qword reader;
- a keyed low-byte predicate with exception fallback;
- a read-modify-read-restore MSR sequence around CLI/STI;
- an R14-keyed fenced reader with LFENCE before and after.

No conservatively reachable body embeds a complete fixed MSR index. The concrete index comes from protected or invocation state. Publishing a guessed “EAC reads MSR X” list would be fiction.

## Descriptor, VM, cache, transaction, and port paths

### SIDT and SGDT

The protected interpreter resolves an output address, writes a ten-byte IDTR or GDTR pseudo-descriptor there, and returns to a shared dispatcher.

### VMREAD

The native helper executes `VMREAD [RDX], RCX` and returns a normalized status:

```text
0 success
1 VMfailValid
2 VMfailInvalid
```

The selected field comes from the caller. I did not capture a live field value.

### Cache operations

One helper always executes WBINVD, stores a caller value, then executes INVD. A protected-interpreter operation also exposes WBINVD.

### RTM

The XBEGIN/XEND leaf performs an empty transaction probe. Abort returns the hardware status in EAX; success commits and returns with entry RAX preserved.

### Port output

Four OUT sites sit in two keyed native bodies. Their port and value formulas are exact, but the concrete DX/EAX/AX values depend on invocation keys, descriptor bytes, mode, and helper results. There is no fixed port literal to report.

### Selector queries

Three tiny leaves execute SLDT, SMSW, and STR into AX and return while preserving the upper RAX bits.

## Crypto inventory

I recovered exact or bounded implementations for:

| Primitive | Status |
| --- | --- |
| SHA-1 hardware transform | exact instruction family |
| SHA-256 hardware transform A | exact instruction family |
| SHA-256 hardware transform B | exact instruction family |
| SHA-512 compression, workspace ABI | behaviorally modeled |
| SHA-512 compression, packed-context ABI | behaviorally modeled |
| MD5 compression | behaviorally modeled |
| MD4 48-round family | bounded conditional match |
| CRC32 multi-width update | exact instruction family |
| memcmp-like comparison | behaviorally modeled |
| memmove | behaviorally modeled |
| memset | behaviorally modeled |
| large fill | behaviorally modeled |

The SHA-256 implementation has both hardware and scalar selection plumbing. CPU feature detection populates state consumed by the fill, checksum, and hash paths.

Algorithm identity is not data ownership. Without a live caller and input object, I do not label every SHA-256 call “driver integrity verification.”

## Kraken/Oodle-family entropy decoder

The image contains a substantial framed decoder with exact tables, bit readers, scratch-space gates, dispatch, and seam validation.

The closed path families are:

- type 0: Huffman;
- type 2: binary;
- type 4: unary.

The named but not fully expanded paths are:

- type 1: TANS;
- type 3: RLE;
- type 5: recursive.

The scratch-space boundaries are exact. The early one-symbol path needs alignment padding plus `0x1520`; the multi-symbol path additionally requires padding plus `0x25c0`. Tests cover offsets 0, 1, 7, and 15 immediately below and exactly at both gates.

The dispatcher has exact direct calls into the framed, recursive, RLE, TANS, and final validation paths. Two optional callback targets come from mutable state and cannot be recovered from static bytes alone.

I have not captured a live compressed input/output pair. So I can describe the decoder, not the product feature that supplied its data.

## TPM evidence

The user-mode EAC service retained exact TPM 2.0 command buffers.

Across both launches:

```text
TPM2_GetCapability
capability: TPM_CAP_HANDLES
property:   0x810eac00
count:      1
```

In the first launch:

```text
TPM2_ReadPublic
object handle: 0x810eac00
```

The persistence of the exact GetCapability bytes across launches makes accidental random data unlikely. But the capture has no paired response, completion status, or transport record.

The honest statement is that the companion constructed or retained those request buffers. The driver is not automatically the owner, and the request is not automatically completed.

## Network state

I reconstructed a handle-rooted AFD endpoint for the service in the early snapshot and a surviving wrapper endpoint in the steady snapshot. The service endpoint was IPv4/TCP/443. The peer value is deliberately absent from the public repository.

The global endpoint list was torn in one sweep, so I only make handle-rooted ownership claims. I do not claim the surviving wrapper endpoint was the only active connection.

There is also no proven data-flow edge from the TPM buffers to a socket send. That requires a same-epoch buffer, call, IRP/completion, or protected state relationship.

## What I think these mechanisms are for

The mechanisms give EAC ingredients for:

- CPU vendor and feature classification;
- virtualization and platform-state checks;
- timing anomaly detection;
- protected-machine execution and fault recovery;
- hashing and integrity work;
- compressed data transport or storage;
- persistent platform-key discovery;
- remote service coordination.

I am comfortable saying the capability exists. I am not comfortable inventing the exact policy result or claiming every path executed in this capture.

```

`docs/process-staging-and-lifecycle.md`:

```md
# Process Staging and Launch Lifecycle

The launch is a small distributed system inside one machine. The service, EAC-suffixed Fortnite wrapper, protected game process, kernel driver, shared section, readiness events, device handle, TPM/CNG state, and network connection all have different roles.

Mixing those roles produces bad conclusions quickly, so I kept them separate.

## Captured roles

| Role | Captured identity | Important state |
| --- | --- | --- |
| EAC service | `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.exe` | creates/owns staging view, owns three readiness-event handles, waits on game event, holds TPM/CNG/network/file state |
| EAC-suffixed wrapper | `FortniteClient-Win64-Shipping_EAC_EOS.exe` | maps second staging view, holds game-event modify handle, opens EAC device |
| Protected game | memflow-visible skeletal Fortnite process | opens EAC device, later expands to thousands of handles and many threads |
| Kernel driver | `EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys` | registers callbacks/minifilter/device and protected policy state |

The wrapper and protected game are not the same process. The wrapper has the second shared-section view. The protected game does not.

## Shared staging section

The service and wrapper reference one kernel section object named:

```text
EasyAntiCheat_EOSBin
```

Its size is 96 MiB. The control area has no file object, so it is pagefile-backed. The section has two mapped views and a committed prefix large enough to contain the staging envelope plus the complete raw driver.

The captured envelope places:

```text
offset 0x18  raw length
offset 0x1c  raw PE bytes
```

The embedded PE includes the complete security directory and primary/nested signatures.

Both process views are byte-identical across the committed region. The raw file recovered from them matches the independent watchdog captures.

## Creator evidence

The `_SEGMENT` structure uses unions. Naming one member without considering section type would be careless.

For this non-image, null-file-pointer section:

- the raw `u1` value conditionally selects `CreatingProcessId`;
- the raw `u2` value conditionally selects `FirstMappedVa`.

Those values identify the EAC service as the section creator for the captured run and the service view's first mapped address.

That is a derived member selection from exact bytes. It is not a captured historical `NtCreateSection` call.

## Readiness events

The service holds full-access-like handles to:

```text
EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventModule
EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventDriver
EasyAntiCheat_EOSEventGame
```

The wrapper holds synchronize/modify rights to the exact same game-event object.

Across the two physical epochs:

| Event | Early | Steady |
| --- | --- | --- |
| Module | signaled, no waiters | signaled, no waiters |
| Driver | signaled, no waiters | signaled, no waiters |
| Game | unsignaled, one EAC waiter | signaled, no waiters |

The early waiter is an exact EAC service thread. Its wait block points back to the same event body held by both processes.

The lifecycle interpretation is straightforward: module and driver stages completed before the early sweep, then the service waited for game readiness, and the later sweep caught that stage complete.

The exact creator and signaler code paths are not retained. The wrapper is a strong game-event signaler candidate because it has modify rights, but I have not upgraded that into a proved call.

## Device linkage

The wrapper and protected game each hold a distinct `FILE_OBJECT` that resolves to the same EAC device and driver.

Both handles persist across the early and steady snapshots. The device does not attach to a lower stack and has no extension in the captured state. CREATE/CLOSE allocate no file context.

That leaves DEVICE_CONTROL plus out-of-band global state as the likely command/session boundary.

## Protected process growth

The protected game is deliberately awkward to inspect from its captured address space. Its path and command line are empty in the memflow view, its DTB does not yield a normal module/range inventory, and its early handle table is tiny.

By steady state it has:

- thousands of handles;
- more than 150 active threads;
- an EAC device handle;
- ordinary file, registry, certificate, pipe, and network-related objects.

Kernel-owned structures remain readable even when its user address space does not. That is why I can make handle and ETHREAD claims without pretending I recovered its user-mode modules.

## EAC service state

The service address space retains evidence from several subsystems:

- the staging section and three readiness events;
- TPM device state and exact TPM 2.0 request buffers;
- CNG and KsecDD handles;
- certificate-store and registry objects;
- NSI and AFD objects;
- file-system paths;
- named pipes;
- an IPv4/443 endpoint.

A handle proves reachability and granted rights. A resident request buffer proves bytes were present. Neither proves the query completed or tells me the full application message.

## Certificate-store observation

At steady state, one exact surviving protected-process IRP is an NTFS directory-change notification against the user's `SystemCertificates\My` directory. Its completion filter requests file-name, directory-name, size, and last-write changes.

That IRP is protected-process state. One raw stack qword falls inside EAC code, but it is not an unwind-proven frame, so I do not attribute the request to the driver.

The other exact surviving user-requestor IRP is an NPFS pipe listen. It also does not target the EAC device.

## Network observation

The service's early handle table roots one AFD endpoint with exact IPv4/TCP/443 state. The steady wrapper inventory contains a surviving connect-helper endpoint.

The public material removes the peer address. More importantly, I never used the endpoint as proof of what EAC sent. Packet contents and application-level ordering were not retained by this capture path.

## Driver appearance and unload

The driver appears during the protected launch, registers its integration surfaces, and later disappears cleanly from the enumerated state.

Cross-run invariants include:

- same raw driver hash;
- same executable-section bytes;
- same callback RVAs and operation registration;
- same namespace;
- same staging-section design;
- same launch-role split.

Mutable addresses, process IDs, thread IDs, pool objects, bugcheck checksums, and loaded data state change by epoch.

The first module-watch path captured appearance but missed a precise disappearance event. A later corrected lifecycle collector captured two natural cycles and confirmed fresh object identities after reload.

## Composite-directory failure

One early survey reused an existing output directory. An older EAC image file remained next to newer JSON saying the driver was absent. Reading the directory as one instantaneous snapshot would falsely imply that EAC was both present and absent.

I proved the mismatch from write times and collector order, then changed the rule: every survey gets a unique output directory and embedded survey identity.

This failure is part of the write-up because evidence handling is part of reverse engineering. A perfect disassembly cannot repair a contaminated timeline.

## What the launch graph currently looks like

```text
EAC service
  ├─ creates/owns first EasyAntiCheat_EOSBin view
  ├─ holds Module/Driver/Game readiness events
  ├─ waits on Game in the early epoch
  ├─ retains TPM/CNG/certificate/network state
  └─ coordinates protected driver lifecycle

EAC-suffixed Fortnite wrapper
  ├─ maps second EasyAntiCheat_EOSBin view
  ├─ holds modify rights to the Game event
  └─ opens \Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS

Protected Fortnite process
  ├─ opens \Device\EasyAntiCheat_EOS
  ├─ grows into full handle/thread state
  └─ remains user-address-space opaque in these captures

EasyAntiCheat_EOS.sys
  ├─ creates/exposes device path
  ├─ registers callbacks and minifilter
  ├─ maintains protected policy/context state
  └─ unregisters from enumerated surfaces on unload
```

## The remaining lifecycle question

I know the objects and I know the broad ordering. I still need the exact producer-consumer state machine:

```text
staged bytes
  -> verifier/digest state
  -> driver load
  -> device ready
  -> wrapper/device session
  -> protected game ready
  -> normal teardown
```

That requires another focused memflow capture around user-mode call targets, event transitions, staging-buffer consumers, and unload rollback—not more guesses from object names.

```

`docs/registry-and-filesystem.md`:

```md
# Registry and File-System Policy

This is where the driver stops looking like a collection of generic callbacks and starts revealing what it cares about.

The registry side focuses on persistence, debugger/compatibility controls, network catalogs, firewall rules, and selected policy values. The file-system side focuses on path/name state, rename and hard-link style transitions, extension-specific callbacks, and executable section creation.

## Registry dispatcher

The recovered native dispatcher handles exactly three raw classes in its closed body:

```text
RegNtPreSetValueKey
RegNtPreQueryValueKey
RegNtPreOpenKeyEx
```

All other classes return `STATUS_SUCCESS` from that dispatcher body.

The live registration sits at altitude `327530` with a null public registration context. Internal helper contexts passed deeper into the protected policy are separate objects and must not be confused with the public callback context.

## String machinery

The core substring helper validates `UNICODE_STRING` state and compares UTF-16 code units after masking with `0xffdf`.

For the ASCII literals used by these policies, that produces case-insensitive comparison. It is not a full Unicode case-folding implementation.

I also recovered:

- strict braced-GUID validation;
- empty/malformed descriptor checks;
- object-name reconstruction;
- a `0x400`-byte `OBJECT_NAME_INFORMATION` allocation;
- `ObQueryNameString` invocation;
- deterministic cleanup through the EAC free wrapper;
- exact `RtlCompareUnicodeString(..., CaseInSensitive=TRUE)` callsites.

## Pre-set policy

The callback reads the standard set-value information fields:

| Offset | Field |
| ---: | --- |
| `+0x00` | key object |
| `+0x08` | value name |
| `+0x14` | type |
| `+0x18` | data pointer |
| `+0x20` | data size |

It requires a non-null internal context with a specific state marker, queries the key's full object name, evaluates policy, and frees the allocated name on every modeled post-allocation exit.

### Protected path families

The exact policy literals group into these system areas:

#### Startup and persistence

```text
CurrentVersion\Run
CurrentVersion\RunOnce
SafeBoot
Active Setup\Installed Components
Task Scheduler state
Services
Font Drivers
KnownDLLs
Credential Providers
```

#### Shell and browser injection surfaces

```text
ContextMenuHandlers
DragDropHandlers
ShellIconOverlayIdentifiers
Browser Helper Objects
htmlfile\shell\open\command
```

Both native and WOW6432Node variants appear where applicable.

#### Network stack configuration

```text
Winsock Protocol_Catalog9 entries
Winsock NameSpace_Catalog5 entries
32-bit and 64-bit catalog paths
```

A masked substring match against any of the 25 exact object-name forms returns `STATUS_ACCESS_DENIED` in the bounded policy model.

### Firewall rule predicate

The separate firewall branch requires all of the following:

- `Type == REG_SZ`;
- non-empty data larger than the exact minimum;
- first data character lowercasing to `v`;
- braced-GUID value name;
- case-insensitive `Action=Block`;
- case-insensitive `Active=TRUE`;
- exact firewall-rules key name and byte length.

The matched path returns `STATUS_CALLBACK_BYPASS` and does not write the original set-information structure.

## Pre-query policy

The callback reads through `ResultLength` in the standard query-value information structure.

Six object-name families can produce `STATUS_OBJECT_NAME_NOT_FOUND` without output writes:

```text
AppCompatFlags
Safer\CodeIdentifiers
Session Manager\AppCompatibility
Windows\AppCompat policy
AeDebug
Image File Execution Options
```

The same not-found result applies to selected values including:

```text
SafeDllSearchMode
AppInit_DLLs
AutodialDLL under Winsock parameters
```

The policy therefore does more than block writes. It can make selected configuration appear absent to the caller on the modeled paths.

### `RequireSignedAppInit_DLLs`

This case has exact class- and buffer-length-dependent behavior for `KeyValuePartialInformation`:

| Output length | Status | Writes |
| ---: | --- | --- |
| `0..15` | success | no output or result-length write |
| `16..19` | `STATUS_BUFFER_TOO_SMALL` | type `REG_DWORD`, zero data length, required size 20 |
| `20..32` | `STATUS_CALLBACK_BYPASS` | type `REG_DWORD`, data length 4, DWORD value 1, result length 20 |

The final path writes bytes only through output offset `+0x0f`, despite reporting a 20-byte result. I preserved that noncanonical detail instead of normalizing it into what the structure usually looks like.

Other tested information classes return success without output mutation in the declared domain.

## Pre-open policy

The pre-open callback reads `CompleteName`, gets previous mode and current thread ID, and compares the thread against an internal context field.

Kernel-mode callers return success on the closed path.

For user-mode callers, policy differs by thread relationship:

- the other-thread branch covers AppCompatibility, AppCompat policy, AeDebug, Image File Execution Options, and Wintrust configuration;
- the equal-thread branch covers Safer CodeIdentifiers.

A match returns `STATUS_OBJECT_NAME_NOT_FOUND`. A nonmatch returns success. No modeled path writes the open-information structure.

The ownership and lifecycle of the internal tracked-thread field remain open.

## Filter Manager registration

The live filter registers pre-operation callbacks for:

```text
IRP_MJ_CREATE
